The shipped EmbeddingSpace uses hand-authored synthetic coordinates and category/analogy toggles instead (this follows the research file's Q6 recommendation, so it is a deliberate deviation, but the interactive nearest-neighbor feature is absent).

**Exercises:** The four exercises form a meaningful progression (cosine-similarity inspection → intruder detection → CBOW implementation → analogy search) and are real problems, not filler; the hints are substantive and the CBOW and analogy tasks genuinely extend the chapter. Exercises 1 and 3 are clean. However Exercises 2 and 4 are miscaffolded: their example test cases use words (fish, car, red, blue, green) that do not exist in the section-4 or section-6 corpora the reader is told to reuse, so `word_to_id['fish']` raises KeyError as written — they were evidently authored against the widgets' richer animals/vehicles/foods/colors vocab. Exercise 1 also mislabels the section-4 corpus as '12-word' (it is 10 unique words / 21 tokens). The exercises need a single consistent corpus wired through all four.

**Widgets:** Both specced-equivalent widgets exist as .tsx and render (no placeholders). Word2VecDynamics correctly implements skip-gram negative sampling in 2D over a 15-word vocab; its gradient math mirrors the chapter's SGNS derivation and the emergent behavior (categories separate, connector words 'liked'/'saw'/'wanted' stay central) matches the prose — but the prose 'Each click advances a step of SGD' contradicts the actual Reset/Play/scrubber controls and the widget's own caption. EmbeddingSpace renders category clusters and analogy overlays; I verified every analogy group's arrows are internally parallel (gender delta (0,−2), capital (0.5,2), comparative (0.5,−1)). Its data is hand-authored synthetic coordinates, not real word2vec — so the prose 'pre-computed word2vec embeddings' overstates (the caption's 'word2vec-like' is accurate), and the curriculum's specced nearest-neighbor lookup is not implemented.


### Chapter 3 — grade B

A well-written, voice-clean chapter that teaches the core BPE algorithm (training + encoding) rigorously, with two real, correct widgets and genuinely runnable code. Its main weaknesses are a confirmed internal contradiction that misclassifies Mistral's tokenizer as Unigram LM, a WordPiece citation misattribution, and a curriculum-required topic (decoding / round-trip verification) that is entirely absent. The "long tail of consequences" and language-asymmetry material is a highlight; a couple of headline examples (GPT-2 number splits) look imprecise.

**Strengths:** Core BPE training and encoding are explained precisely and correctly; the greedy-by-learned-order insight callout is exactly right and the runnable train_bpe/encode code actually runs and produces the claimed behavior.; Voice contract is fully respected: no first-person-singular, no forbidden phrases, headings name the thing, opinions are owned (the language-asymmetry warning callout is exemplary).; Both widgets are real and correct: BPETraining runs live char-level BPE; TokenizerComparison uses genuine precomputed tiktoken/SentencePiece data (spot-checked byte/token counts match, e.g. ' SolidGoldMagikarp' as a single 18-byte GPT-2 token).; Cross-references and section-number references (section 3/4/6, Chapter 2 embeddings, Chapter 4 attention) are all correct per the canonical map.; Arxiv IDs and dates are accurate (Sennrich 1508.07909, Wu 1609.08144, Kudo 1804.10959, Petrov 2305.15425, Gage 1994, GPT-2 2019).

**Findings:**

- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** Mistral is classified as Unigram LM, but Mistral uses a byte-fallback BPE tokenizer (SentencePiece BPE, inherited from the Llama family), not Unigram LM. This directly contradicts the same chapter's line 88 and line 217 ("GPT-2, GPT-3, GPT-4, LLaMA, Mistral, Code Llama ... all use byte-level BPE in some form"), so Mistral appears in both the BPE list and the Unigram list. Gemma is likely also miscategorized (Gemini-derived SentencePiece BPE).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch03-tokenization/index.mdx:277 — "**Unigram LM:** T5, mT5, ALBERT, XLNet, current Mistral (with byte fallback), Gemma" (also line 271 "Unigram LM is used by T5, mT5, ALBERT, XLNet, and Mistral.")
  - _fix:_ Move Mistral from the Unigram LM list to the BPE/byte-fallback-BPE list (it belongs with Llama). Verify Gemma; if it is BPE-based SentencePiece, move it too. Reserve the Unigram LM list for T5/mT5/ALBERT/XLNet.
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** WordPiece is attributed as 'introduced' by Wu et al. 2016 (GNMT). WordPiece was actually introduced by Schuster & Nakajima 2012 ('Japanese and Korean Voice Search'); the GNMT paper itself cites them and only adopts the method. The curriculum spec (CURRICULUM.md:202) explicitly lists 'Schuster & Nakajima 2012 (WordPiece)'.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch03-tokenization/index.mdx:262 — "and by Google's earlier neural machine translation work, where it was introduced (Wu et al. 2016, arxiv.org/abs/1609.08144)"
  - _fix:_ Attribute WordPiece's origin to Schuster & Nakajima 2012, and describe Wu et al. 2016 as the work that popularized it for NMT (and preceded BERT), not as its introduction.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** Decoding is never taught or exercised. The chapter shows training and encoding runnable code and Exercises 1-2 cover train/encode, but there is no decode function (token IDs/bytes -> string), no round-trip (encode->decode) verification, and no discussion of losslessness or the partial-multibyte-token edge case. .decode('utf-8') appears only as a byte-to-string display helper.
  - _at:_ context/CURRICULUM.md:182 — "Implement BPE (Byte Pair Encoding) from scratch — training, encoding, and decoding" (and CURRICULUM.md:197 runnable spec "encoding and decoding round-trip verification")
  - _fix:_ Add a short decode section or runnable block that concatenates token bytes and decodes to a string, plus an encode->decode round-trip assertion; ideally add or convert an exercise to cover decoding.
- **[major/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** The tokens shown carry a leading space (' 1', ' 100'), which is impossible for the bare input strings '100'/'100000' under the GPT-2 pre-tokenization regex ( ?\p{N}+ can only capture a leading space if one is present in the input). Moreover, GPT-2 keeps common short numbers like these as single tokens — the chapter's own TokenizerComparison data shows ' 345' and ' 12' as single GPT-2 tokens — so splitting '100' into two tokens is very likely wrong. The research file used the same example but hedged it ('or similar split depending on context'); the prose drops the hedge and states it as fact.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch03-tokenization/index.mdx:372 — "GPT-2's BPE tokenizer splits the string `"100"` into the tokens `[' 1', '00']`. The string `"100000"` becomes `[' 100', '000']`."
  - _fix:_ Replace with an actually-verified tiktoken example (run tiktoken.get_encoding('gpt2')), e.g. show a genuinely fragmented longer number, and remove the phantom leading space or make the leading space consistent with an explicit ' 100' input.
- **[minor/math-error/CONFIRMED]** In the runnable corpus of line 184 ('the cat sat on the mat the dog sat on the rug the cat purred'), 'the' appears 5 times, not 6. Also count(t,h)=5 is tied with count(h,e)=5 and count(a,t)=5, so t+h is not a unique 'most frequent pair' — it wins only via the deterministic tiebreak in the code, which the prose does not mention.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch03-tokenization/index.mdx:199 — "The first merge on this corpus is usually *t* + *h*, the most frequent adjacent pair, because *the* appears six times."
  - _fix:_ Change 'six times' to 'five times', and note that the first merge is chosen among tied top pairs by the tiebreak rule (or adjust the corpus so 'the' genuinely dominates).
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The delivered comparison widget and its data ship only three tokenizers (GPT-2, GPT-4/cl100k_base, SentencePiece-T5). WordPiece/BERT is absent, and it is example-driven (fixed dropdown) rather than free-text paste. The curriculum specced a paste-text playground including WordPiece and merge history, and the runnable spec (CURRICULUM.md:198) asked for 4-5 tokenizers including BERT, Llama 3, and Gemma.
  - _at:_ context/CURRICULUM.md:193 — "TokenizerPlayground — paste text, see how it tokenizes under BPE, WordPiece, and tiktoken (cl100k_base)" vs delivered src/components/widgets/ch03/TokenizerComparison.tsx
  - _fix:_ Either add a WordPiece/BERT column (and ideally live free-text input) to the comparison, or explicitly note in prose that WordPiece is illustrated only in Exercise 4 and the comparison is intentionally limited to three families.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** The chapter's multilingual-cost economics (Korean ~2.5-3x English) are pinned entirely to cl100k_base and never mention o200k_base (the GPT-4o tokenizer, ~200k vocab, mid-2024), which substantially improved non-English tokenization efficiency. For a mid-2026 reader this reads as slightly stale.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch03-tokenization/index.mdx:343 — "GPT-4's tokenizer (the `cl100k_base` encoding shipped with `tiktoken`)"
  - _fix:_ Add a sentence noting GPT-4o's o200k_base and that larger, more multilingual vocabularies have narrowed (not eliminated) the per-language cost gap.
- **[minor/currency/CONFIRMED]** Internal numeric inconsistency: 4 chars/token (English) vs 1.5 (Korean) gives 4/1.5 = 2.67x, which the same chapter earlier (line 242) correctly describes as 'nearly three times'. Here it is rounded down to '2.5x'.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch03-tokenization/index.mdx:380 — "The same content in Korean costs roughly 2.5× more per character than the same content in English"
  - _fix:_ Use a consistent figure ('roughly 2.7x' or 'nearly three times') in both places.
- **[minor/pedagogy/CONFIRMED]** The chat-template discussion never forward-references Chapter 13 (SFT), although the curriculum tricky-spot explicitly says 'The reader will need this for SFT in Ch 13' and lists Ch 13 under Connections. Separately, plain-text 'Chapter 2'/'Chapter 4' mentions are not linked, contrary to the cross-reference convention in PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md:177.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch03-tokenization/index.mdx:362 — "The chat formats used by ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini ... are not freeform text wrappers."
  - _fix:_ Add an explicit forward pointer to Chapter 13 where chat-template tokens are introduced, and link the Chapter 2/4 references to their anchors.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Decoding and encode->decode round-trip verification (curriculum 'Reader will be able to: training, encoding, and decoding'; runnable spec 'encoding and decoding round-trip verification') are entirely absent — no decode function, no losslessness discussion.
- WordPiece is discussed in prose (section 5) and Exercise 4, but is missing from the comparison widget/runnable that the curriculum specced ('BPE, WordPiece, and tiktoken' in one playground; runnable with tiktoken/GPT-2/BERT/Llama 3/Gemma). Delivered comparison covers only GPT-2, GPT-4, and T5.
- The specced 'TokenizerPlayground — paste text' free-text interaction is replaced by a fixed set of precomputed examples; readers cannot tokenize arbitrary input live.
- 'Vocabulary size tradeoffs' (a listed Key Concept) is treated only lightly (small vs exploding vocab); the embedding-table/output-projection/softmax-cost tradeoff is not discussed here (partly, and legitimately, deferred to Ch 8).
- Code tokenization impact (from 'impact on ... numbers, and code') is only lightly covered in prose — numbers and multilingual get dedicated subsections, but code gets only whitespace/indentation mentions and widget examples.

**Exercises:** The four exercises are meaningful, correct, and well-scaffolded, and they build progressively: (1) train BPE and inspect first merges, (2) implement BPE encoding by lowest learned-merge index, (3) quantify language asymmetry with bytes/token, (4) implement WordPiece with the count(a,b)/(count(a)*count(b)) lift score. Scaffolding (TODO stubs, provided corpora/sentences, collapsed hints) is appropriate and the stubs run without error as-is. The one gap is that none exercises decoding, which the curriculum requires. Exercise 3's hint numbers (English ~2-3, Korean ~1, Russian between) are consistent with an English-trained toy tokenizer.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx and are exported. BPETraining runs a live character-level BPE trace (25 merges on a 10-sentence corpus) and correctly shows top pairs, chosen merge, recent merges, corpus state, and vocab growth — matching its caption; the char-level choice (vs the byte-level runnable) is intentional and disclosed in bpe-corpus.ts. TokenizerComparison renders genuine precomputed tokenizations for GPT-2, GPT-4 (cl100k_base), and SentencePiece-T5; spot-checks confirm the data is real (Korean 안녕하세요… = 43 bytes / 38 GPT-2 tokens = 1.13 bytes/token; ' SolidGoldMagikarp' = 1 GPT-2 token at 18 bytes, split into 5 under GPT-4 — exactly the glitch-token point). Divergences from spec: no WordPiece/BERT column, example-driven rather than free-text paste, and merge history lives only in the separate BPETraining widget rather than in the comparison playground as specced.


### Chapter 4 — grade B

A technically strong chapter on the core mathematics: the √d_k variance derivation, the softmax gradient/peaked-softmax connection, and causal masking are all correct, well-sequenced, and hit the curriculum's flagged "tricky spots" with targeted warning callouts. All runnable code executes and (mostly) produces the claimed output; the four exercises are meaningful and correctly scaffolded. The chapter is dragged down by inherited stale chapter numbering (transformer block cited as "Chapter 7", SSMs as "Chapter 11" — both wrong), a marquee widget whose narration flatly contradicts the near-uniform, self-dominant weights it actually renders, and an out-of-date "80 GB is the largest GPU" claim. The specced kernel-method aside is essentially missing.

**Strengths:** The √d_k variance derivation (section 4) is rigorous and correct end-to-end: mean-zero by symmetry, cross-terms vanish under independence, Var(q·k)=d_k, and the 'divide by std not variance' justification for √ over d_k. Verified line by line.; The softmax section correctly derives the Jacobian σ_i(δ_ij − σ_j) and explicitly ties the gradient-collapse view to the variance view ('two views of one phenomenon'), which is exactly the pedagogy the curriculum asked for.; Causal masking is handled precisely: pre-softmax additive mask, the two warning callouts nail the exact misconceptions the spec flagged (mask before softmax; −∞ vs −1e9), and the CausalMask widget computes correctly (verified lower-triangular, rows sum to 1).; Runnable code is clean and numerically stable (max-subtraction softmax), and the causal-mask demo runs without NaN despite using −np.inf, because the diagonal is always finite.; Exercises are genuine and well-scaffolded: the O(n²)-vs-FFN crossover hint (n≈16384 at d_model=4096) is arithmetically correct, and cross-attention (Ex 4) correctly stresses the non-square attention matrix.

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/stale-cross-ref/CONFIRMED]** The transformer block is Chapter 5 (ch05-multihead-and-block, 'Multi-head attention & the transformer block'), not Chapter 7. The chapter even treats multi-head (correctly cited as Ch 5) and the transformer block (Ch 7) as separate chapters, but they are the same chapter. This is inherited from the stale numbering in research.md.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch04-attention/index.mdx:29 and :558 — "The operation lives inside the transformer block (Chapter 7)" (also "Chapter 7 wraps attention in the transformer block.")
  - _fix:_ Change both 'Chapter 7' references for the transformer block to 'Chapter 5'.
- **[blocker/stale-cross-ref/CONFIRMED]** State-space models / Mamba are Chapter 12 (ch12-ssm-and-mamba). Chapter 11 is Mixture of Experts. Both references point a reader to the wrong chapter. Inherited from research.md's stale 'Ch 11 (Mamba)' numbering.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch04-attention/index.mdx:377 and :558 — "Chapter 11 introduces state-space models, alternative architectures with O(n) scaling" (also "Chapter 11 introduces an alternative that trades the formula for linear scaling.")
  - _fix:_ Change both 'Chapter 11' references (SSM / linear-scaling alternative) to 'Chapter 12'.
- **[major/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** I recomputed the widget's hand-tuned Q,K,V: the resulting attention weights are near-uniform (all in [0.118, 0.214]; uniform = 0.167) and every row's maximum is the DIAGONAL (each token attends most to itself). E.g. the 'cat' row is the=0.143, cat=0.214, sat=0.170, on=0.118, the=0.143, mat=0.211 — so 'cat attends mostly to the' is false ('the' is near the minimum). The described 'local neighbor' / 'syntactic structure' pattern does not exist in the rendered matrix; a reader who hovers the cells sees the contradiction.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch04/attention-data.ts:187 ("Notice how \"cat\" attends mostly to \"the\", \"sat\" to \"cat\", \"on\" to \"sat\" — local syntactic structure emerges.") and index.mdx:140 ("each token attends primarily to its neighbor")
  - _fix:_ Re-tune W_Q/W_K in attention-data.ts to actually produce a peaked local/neighbor pattern (e.g. make each query align with the previous token's key), OR rewrite the caption and stage-4 text to describe the true pattern (weak, self-dominant, near-uniform).
- **[major/currency/CONFIRMED]** Stale as of the book's mid-2026 readership. The H200 (141 GB), B200 (192 GB), and AMD MI300X (192 GB) all ship with substantially more than 80 GB and predate the knowledge cutoff. An 80 GB H100 is no longer the memory ceiling.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch04-attention/index.mdx:374 — "The largest single-GPU memory available today is 80 GB"
  - _fix:_ Update to reflect ~140–192 GB single-GPU HBM (H200/B200/MI300X), or phrase capacity-agnostically ('even the largest single GPUs, ~150–200 GB, cannot hold all these matrices at once').
- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** 32 GB per head per layer does NOT exceed a single GPU's memory — it is below even the chapter's own stated 80 GB ceiling (and far below H200/B200's 141–192 GB). The single-matrix figure is fine (~32 GB), but 'vastly exceeding any single GPU' only becomes true when aggregated across heads×layers×batch, which the sentence does not say (the very next callout, line 374, correctly makes the aggregate argument).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch04-attention/index.mdx:371 — "The full 128K attention matrix at fp16 is ∼32 GB per head per layer, vastly exceeding any single GPU's memory."
  - _fix:_ Attach 'vastly exceeding memory' to the aggregate (all heads/layers/batch), not to a single 32 GB per-head-per-layer matrix; a single such matrix fits on one GPU.
- **[minor/math-error/CONFIRMED]** 'Per forward pass per token' is self-contradictory. The ~2e12 figure (2e9 × 32 heads × 32 layers) is the attention compute for one forward pass over the whole n=4096 sequence, not per token (per token it would be ~n× smaller). Separately, the quoted per-head-per-layer figure '4096²×64×2 ≈ 2e9' counts only QK^T and omits the AV matmul (another ~2e9), so it undercounts attention compute by ~2×.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch04-attention/index.mdx:369 — "total attention compute is on the order of 10^12 FLOPs per forward pass per token"
  - _fix:_ Drop 'per token' (it is per forward pass over the full sequence), and note the per-head figure is one of the two matmuls (≈4e9 including AV).
- **[minor/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The prose explicitly recommends −1e9 over literal −np.inf to avoid NaN, then every code block and the exercise hint use −np.inf. It is safe in causal masking (verified: no row is all −∞ because the diagonal is always allowed), but the chapter contradicts its own stated best practice, and a reader following the prose would write different code than the chapter's.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch04-attention/index.mdx:263 ("...implemented as a large negative number (−10^9 is conventional) rather than literal `-np.inf`") vs the code at :274 (`mask[np.triu_indices(n, k=1)] = -np.inf`) and the Exercise-2 hint at :440 ("use `-np.inf`")
  - _fix:_ Either switch the code/hint to −1e9 to match the advice, or add one line noting −np.inf is safe here specifically because every row retains a finite diagonal entry.
- **[minor/code-output-mismatch/PLAUSIBLE]** The quoted numbers are the theoretical √d_k, not the code's actual output. Running the chapter's exact code (seed 0, 5×5) prints raw std ≈ 2.77, 7.66, 23.42, 70.03 — the d_k=4096 case is ~70, notably above the stated 64. Additionally, the d_k=4096 `Q @ K.T` emits spurious numpy RuntimeWarnings ('overflow/divide-by-zero/invalid value encountered in matmul') in this environment; results are still correct but the warnings could alarm a reader (may or may not reproduce in Pyodide).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch04-attention/index.mdx:209 — "The raw score std tracks √d_k: roughly 2.8 at d_k = 8, 8 at d_k = 64, 22.6 at d_k = 512, 64 at d_k = 4096."
  - _fix:_ Either quote the actual printed values (or say 'approximately √d_k, with sampling noise at small n'), and consider reducing the d_k=4096 case or noting the benign warnings.
- **[minor/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum lists 'Connect attention to... kernel methods' as a Reader-will-be-able-to outcome and 'attention as a kernel method (advanced aside)' as a key concept, with research pointing to Tsai 2019 (arxiv 1908.11775). The chapter delivers only two passing mentions in the context of linear attention; there is no aside actually framing softmax attention as kernel smoothing.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch04-attention/index.mdx:239 and :244 (only kernel mentions: "Linear attention... uses kernel tricks", "Performer-style methods approximate softmax with kernel features")
  - _fix:_ Add a short advanced aside connecting scaled dot-product attention to kernel methods (Tsai 2019's kernel-smoothing view), even a callout-sized one, to satisfy the specced outcome.
- **[minor/pedagogy/PLAUSIBLE]** The classroom analogy frames attention as attending to 'every other' student and blending 'everyone else's' values, which quietly excludes self-attention-to-self. In real self-attention a position attends to (and often most strongly to) itself — indeed the chapter's own AttentionHeatmap widget shows the diagonal dominating. The omission reinforces the same self-vs-neighbor confusion as the widget mismatch.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch04-attention/index.mdx:57 — "each student looks at every other student's Key card... a weighted blend of Value cards from across the room"
  - _fix:_ Say each student also reads their own Key/Value (include self) so the analogy matches self-attention, where the diagonal is a legitimate and often large weight.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Kernel-method connection is essentially absent. Curriculum lists 'Connect attention to soft retrieval and (in an aside) to kernel methods' as a Reader-will-be-able-to outcome and 'attention as a kernel method (advanced aside)' as a key concept (research cites Tsai 2019, arxiv 1908.11775). The chapter only mentions kernels in passing ('linear attention... uses kernel tricks', 'Performer-style methods approximate softmax with kernel features'); there is no aside developing the kernel-smoothing view of attention.
- AttentionHeatmap widget does not meet its spec: curriculum specifies a 12-token sequence with adjustable Q/K/V projections (or selectable presets) and click-a-query-token-to-highlight; the delivered widget is a fixed 6-token stage animation with no adjustable projections and no query-click interaction.
- Geometric interpretation of dot-product similarity (a listed 'in scope' item and research analogy 4, 'dot product as soft cosine similarity') is covered only in a single clause (line 81). The point that raw dot product is magnitude-sensitive (large-magnitude keys attract more attention, unlike cosine similarity) — flagged in the research — is not made.

**Exercises:** The four exercises (verify SDPA + peaked-softmax; causal masking; O(n²) memory crossover; cross-attention) are meaningful, correctly specified, and each has a working TODO template plus a correct hint. Exercise 3's crossover claim (n ≈ 4·d_model = 16384) is verified correct. Exercise 3 passes packages=["numpy"] but its template uses no numpy (harmless). Exercise 2's hint tells the reader to use `-np.inf`, which contradicts the chapter's own prose recommending `-1e9` (see findings). Overall: solid, not filler.

**Widgets:** The AttentionHeatmap marquee widget contradicts its own rendered data; CausalMask is correct.


### Chapter 5 — grade A-

A technically excellent, well-paced chapter that correctly derives multi-head attention, parameter/compute accounting, FFN, residuals, layer norm, and Pre-LN/Post-LN, with numpy code that actually runs and produces the claimed shapes. Cross-references are all correct (Ch4/Ch6/Ch8/Ch25 plus internal section refs) — notably clean given the book's known numbering drift — and the misconception callouts map directly onto the curriculum's MC list. The gaps are against spec (no PyTorch anywhere, GeGLU never mentioned, "why no biases" left unexplained) plus a handful of minor precision/pedagogy nits and one illustrative-data mismatch in the block-flow widget.

**Strengths:** All cross-references are correct: Chapter 4 (attention), Chapter 6 (positional encoding), Chapter 8 (building small LLM), Chapter 25 (interpretability), plus internal 'Section 7'/'section 4' pointers all resolve to the right targets.; Every numpy RunnableCode block is correct and produces the stated output; the multi-head reshape/transpose (n,d)->(h,n,d_k)->(n,d) split-concat is consistent and verified by shape trace, and the Pre-LN block forward pass is right.; Parameter accounting is rigorous and correct: 4 d_model^2 (attention), 8 d_model^2 (FFN), 12 d_model^2 + 4 d_model (block), and the GPT-3 check (12*12288^2*96 = 173.9B vs 175B published) is accurate.; Both curriculum-specced widgets exist, are registered, and match the prose; the four heads in MultiHeadDecomposition (local/previous/'the'/spread) match the caption, and TransformerBlockFlow correctly encodes the Pre-LN residual (AFTER_RESIDUAL_1 = INPUT + AFTER_MHA, i.e. unnormalized input added back).; Strong pedagogy that honors the curriculum's misconception list and derives the SwiGLU 8/3 param-equivalence correctly (3 * d_model * (8/3 d_model) = 8 d_model^2).

**Findings:**

- **[minor/technical-precision/CONFIRMED]** Overgeneralizes. PyTorch's default torch.nn.GELU and many modern frameworks compute the exact erf-based GELU; the tanh form is the historical GPT-2/BERT approximation and is opt-in (approximate='tanh') today.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch05-multihead-and-block/index.mdx:184 — "so implementations use a tanh-based approximation that is accurate to about three decimal places"
  - _fix:_ Qualify: 'GPT-2/BERT-era code used a tanh approximation; modern frameworks default to the exact erf-based GELU, and the chapter uses the tanh form for a self-contained numpy implementation.'
- **[minor/pedagogy/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum's tricky spot explicitly asks the chapter to be honest that the reason RMSNorm's mean-elision works is not well understood; the chapter instead states a confident just-so explanation.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch05-multihead-and-block/index.mdx:301 — "the mean subtraction in standard layer norm is not load-bearing; most of the benefit comes from the variance normalization"
  - _fix:_ Add a hedge: empirically RMSNorm matches LayerNorm, but the theoretical justification for dropping mean-centering is not well established.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The hand-authored AFTER_LN1/AFTER_LN2 rows are not actually normalized (AFTER_LN1 row0 mean=0.133, std=0.761; AFTER_LN2 row0 mean=0.483, std=0.799), so the illustrative 'normalized' stage contradicts the property the widget claims to be demonstrating.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch05/block-flow-data.ts:82 — "normalizes each token's features to zero mean and unit standard deviation"
  - _fix:_ Rescale each after-LN row to true zero-mean/unit-std, or soften the description to 'approximately zero mean and unit std.'
- **[minor/exercise/PLAUSIBLE]** The SwiGLU forward TODO uses column-vector matmul order (W @ x), inconsistent with the row-vector convention (x @ W) used in every other code block in the chapter; taken literally the shapes do not conform.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch05-multihead-and-block/index.mdx:698 — "# TODO: forward = W_down(Swish(W_gate @ x) * W_up @ x)"
  - _fix:_ Write as x @ W_gate / x @ W_up / h @ W_down to match the house convention used throughout.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** Uses Llama-3 as the modern dense reference; by mid-2026 the latest Llama generation (Llama 4) shipped as MoE. The dense-block description remains accurate and pedagogically sound, so this is a freshness note rather than an error.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch05-multihead-and-block/index.mdx:508 — "Stacked thirty-two times with RMSNorm in place of LayerNorm, SwiGLU in place of GELU, and RoPE in place of additive positional encoding, it is Llama-3 8B."
  - _fix:_ Optionally note Llama-3 8B is a dense reference and that the newest Llama generation moved to MoE (Chapter 11).

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- PyTorch is specced but entirely absent. 'Reader will be able to: Implement multi-head attention in numpy AND PyTorch' and the Runnable-code spec ('transformer_block.py — full block in PyTorch matching Llama's choices (RMSNorm, SwiGLU FFN, no biases), shown as static reference') have no counterpart in the chapter, which is numpy-only. May be an intentional book-wide numpy-first choice, but it is a deviation from this chapter's spec.
- GeGLU is a named Key concept ('SwiGLU and GeGLU activation variants') but is never mentioned; only ReLU/GELU/SwiGLU are covered.
- The 'why no biases in modern transformers' tricky spot is under-delivered: biases are noted as 'sometimes omitted' (lines 172, 231) but the rationale the curriculum flagged — small compute saving, marginal quality effect, memory/aesthetic simplification after normalized inputs — is never explained.
- RMSNorm mean-centering is presented with a confident explanation rather than the curriculum-requested acknowledgment that the 'why' is not well understood (see finding at line 301).

**Exercises:** Four meaningful, well-scaffolded, escalating exercises (LayerNorm easy -> multi-head attention medium -> full Pre-LN block medium -> SwiGLU hard) that build exactly the chapter's artifacts rather than filler. Templates have correct TODO structure and sensible collapsed hints; the Exercise 1 hint's gamma=2/beta=1 -> mean=1/std=2 property is correct, and Exercise 4's parameter-count comparison runs as written and prints ratio ~= 0.996 (verified). The only nit is Exercise 4's forward-pass TODO comment using W_gate @ x order, inconsistent with the chapter-wide x @ W convention.

**Widgets:** Both curriculum-specced widgets exist as .tsx, are exported in widgets/index.ts, and are referenced in the prose. MultiHeadDecomposition renders four hand-tuned attention heads whose labels (Local, Previous, 'the', Spread) exactly match the chapter caption, and the concat->W^O narrative is faithful. TransformerBlockFlow is a play/scrub animation through input->LN1->MHA->residual1->LN2->FFN->residual2 with per-stage descriptions and highlighted residual arrows; it correctly implements Pre-LN by adding the UNNORMALIZED input in the residual (AFTER_RESIDUAL_1 = INPUT + AFTER_MHA, AFTER_RESIDUAL_2 = AFTER_RESIDUAL_1 + AFTER_FFN, both computed in code). The curriculum named a single 'TransformerBlockDissection' click-a-layer widget; the delivered scrub-animation plus the bonus multi-head widget meet or exceed that intent. One confirmed defect: the widget's 'after LayerNorm' illustrative matrices are not actually normalized despite the description claiming zero mean / unit std.


### Chapter 6 — grade B

A technically strong, well-written chapter: the math derivations (the sinusoidal linear-transform identity, RoPE's rotation-composition proof) are correct line by line, all four RunnableCode blocks compute what they claim (with one exception below), every chapter/EqRef cross-reference is correct per the canonical map, citations and arxiv IDs are accurate, and the voice is clean with no forbidden phrases. Two real defects undercut it: the ALiBi demo prints decay labels that are exactly inverted (and contradict the chapter's own body text), and the RoPE widget's description states a period that is off by ~30x and contradicts the number the widget itself displays. The chapter also omits KV-cache compatibility entirely, which both the curriculum and the project's own voice contract name as a central reason RoPE won.

**Strengths:** Math is correct and carefully worked: the sinusoidal (sin,cos)->rotation-matrix identity (line 126) and the RoPE relative-position derivation R(a)^T R(b)=R(b-a) (line 234) both check out.; All four exercises are meaningful, correct, and well-scaffolded, mirroring the worked RunnableCode demos with clear TODOs and useful hints (verifying sin^2+cos^2=1, the learned-PE IndexError, the RoPE offset-invariance loop).; Every cross-reference is correct: Ch5 for the block, Ch4 for attention scores, Ch1-3 recap, Ch7 hand-off all match the canonical numbering; EqRef 6.sin and 6.rope resolve correctly.; Citations and arxiv IDs are accurate: Vaswani 2017, Su 2021 (2104.09864), Press 2021 (2108.12409), Shaw 2018 (1803.02155), Chen 2023 (2306.15595); numeric claims (GPT-2 small ~800k PE params vs 124M, Llama 3 base 500000) are right.; Strong pedagogy and voice: misconception callouts are sharp and correct, the 'why RoPE won' narrative is opinionated in the right way, no forbidden phrases, no first-person-singular.

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The ALiBi demo's decay labels are exactly inverted. Head 0 uses slope 2^(-8*1/4)=0.25 (LARGEST slope, steepest decay, max |bias|=1.75) but is labeled 'gentlest decay' and 'minimal penalty'; Head 3 uses slope 2^(-8)=0.0039 (smallest slope, gentlest decay, max |bias|=0.027) but is labeled 'steepest decay' and 'strongly negative bias'. This directly contradicts the chapter's own body text (line 325: 'The first head (h = 1) gets the largest slope and decays the fastest'), and a reader running the code will see head 0's matrix reaching -1.75 under a 'gentlest decay' label.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch06-positional-encoding/index.mdx:349-354; prints 'Head 0, slope = {2 ** (-8 * 1 / n_heads):.4f}  (gentlest decay)' and 'Head 3, slope = {...4...} (steepest decay)' and 'distant positions get strongly negative bias (head 3), while head 0 lets them through with minimal penalty.'
  - _fix:_ Swap the labels: Head 0 (slope 0.25) is the steepest/fastest decay; Head 3 (slope 0.0039) is the gentlest. Correspondingly flip the 'Notice' summary so the strongly-negative-bias head is head 0 and the minimal-penalty head is head 3.
- **[major/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** Pair 3 (d_k=8, k=3) has omega = 1/10000^(6/8) = 0.001, so its period is 2*pi/0.001 = 6283 positions, not ~200. The claim is off by ~30x and contradicts the widget's own footer, which renders 'period: 6,283 positions' for pair 3 via PAIR_PERIODS. No pair has a ~200-position period (periods are 6.3, 62.8, 628, 6283).
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch06/RoPERotation.tsx:65; 'High pairs (e.g. pair 3) rotate slowly — full revolution every ~200 positions.'
  - _fix:_ Change '~200 positions' to '~6,300 positions' (or reword to 'thousands of positions') to match the computed PAIR_PERIODS the widget displays.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** KV-cache compatibility is never mentioned, yet the curriculum's 'Reader will be able to' lists 'Compare ALiBi, learned, sinusoidal, and RoPE on the dimensions that matter: KV-cache compatibility, length extrapolation, training stability', and the chapter's Tricky-spots explicitly require 'Why RoPE composes cleanly with KV caches'. PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md:97 even uses 'RoPE won ... because it composes cleanly with the KV cache' as the canonical example. The chapter's why-RoPE-won argument (parameter-free, per-layer, extrapolation) omits this central reason.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch06-positional-encoding/index.mdx:305-313 (the 'why RoPE won' callout and paragraph); the string 'KV cache' never appears in the chapter.
  - _fix:_ Add a short paragraph in the RoPE section (or the length-extrapolation section) noting that because RoPE is applied at Q/K projection time, cached K vectors are already rotated and stay valid as the cache grows, whereas learned absolute PE would require re-indexing; contrast the training-stability angle briefly to close the specced comparison.
- **[minor/math-error/CONFIRMED]** For d_model=64, dims 60-63 correspond to k=30,31 with periods ~35,300 and ~47,100, not ~62,000. The 62,831 figure applies to omega=1/10000 exactly, which is only approached as d_model grows large; at d_model=64 (the value used in this very code block) the slowest period is ~47k. The prose at line 66 uses ~62,000 correctly for d_model=512, but the code comment reuses it for d_model=64.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch06-positional-encoding/index.mdx:108; '(nearly identical, these dimensions have period ~62000)'
  - _fix:_ Change the comment to '~47,000' (the actual slowest period at d_model=64) or drop the specific number.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** NTK-aware scaling did not originate with Peng et al.; it was a community contribution (bloc97 / kaiokendev, mid-2023), as the research file itself notes ('kaiokendev's NTK-aware scaling notes'). YaRN is Peng et al. 2023 (arxiv 2309.00071), but attaching '(Peng et al. 2023)' to 'NTK-aware scaling and YaRN' misattributes NTK-aware scaling.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch06-positional-encoding/index.mdx:375; 'NTK-aware scaling and YaRN (Peng et al. 2023) refine the idea further'
  - _fix:_ Attribute YaRN to Peng et al. 2023 and NTK-aware scaling separately (community/bloc97, 2023), e.g. 'NTK-aware scaling (community, 2023) and YaRN (Peng et al. 2023, arxiv 2309.00071)'.
- **[minor/stale-cross-ref/CONFIRMED]** The rendered chapter's sections are unnumbered (## headings with no numbers), so '(Section 3)' is not resolvable by the reader; the intended target is the 'The implicit relative-position argument' section. Line 137 similarly uses the loose 'RoPE, the next two sections'.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch06-positional-encoding/index.mdx:203; 'Sinusoidal PE allows the model to learn relative-position attention (Section 3) but does not enforce it.'
  - _fix:_ Replace '(Section 3)' with a named reference, e.g. '(the implicit relative-position argument above)'.
- **[minor/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum tricky-spot asks to distinguish the two extension mechanisms crisply: 'NTK adjusts the base frequency; PI rescales positions.' The chapter contrasts PI (divide position indices) with NTK/YaRN (frequency-dependent), but never states that NTK-aware scaling works by increasing the RoPE base frequency, so the specced distinction is only half-made.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch06-positional-encoding/index.mdx:375; 'NTK-aware scaling and YaRN ... refine the idea further by interpolating different frequencies differently'
  - _fix:_ Add one clause noting that NTK-aware scaling adjusts the base (theta) rather than rescaling positions, so the two families change different knobs.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- KV-cache compatibility of each PE scheme is entirely absent, despite being a named 'Reader will be able to' comparison dimension AND a listed Tricky spot ('Why RoPE composes cleanly with KV caches') AND the project overview's canonical example of why RoPE won.
- Training-stability as a comparison dimension (also named in 'Reader will be able to') is not addressed for any of the four schemes.
- The NTK-aware-scaling-vs-position-interpolation distinction that the curriculum flags as a tricky spot ('NTK adjusts the base frequency; PI rescales positions') is only partially explained — the base-frequency mechanism of NTK is never stated.
- The curriculum's specced single 'PositionEncodingVisualizer' (toggle sinusoidal/RoPE/ALiBi, vary sequence length to see extrapolation past training length) is not delivered; there is no ALiBi visualization and no interactive extrapolation demo (the chapter follows the research file's alternative two-widget recommendation instead).

**Exercises:** The four exercises are genuinely useful, not filler, and are correctly scaffolded. Ex1 (verify sinusoidal structure via sin^2+cos^2=1 and fast/slow dims) is a clean easy warm-up. Ex2 (learned vs sinusoidal extrapolation) is pedagogically well-aimed and the provided sinusoidal_pe_at helper is correct. Ex3 (implement RoPE + verify offset-invariance at offsets (0,5),(3,8),(10,15),(50,55)) directly reinforces the centerpiece result. Ex4 (ALiBi bias + attention) is a reasonable hard exercise; the '(gentlest/steepest)' inversion bug does NOT appear in the exercise scaffolding, only in the worked demo. All TODO templates would run once filled in.

**Widgets:** Both delivered widgets exist and their core computations are correct. SinusoidalPE (heatmap + click-to-plot-wave) matches its prose and the sinusoidal formula exactly. RoPERotation correctly rotates each of 4 pairs by position x frequency and its footer displays correct periods (6.3, 62.8, 628, 6283). However, RoPERotation.tsx:65 states 'High pairs (e.g. pair 3) rotate slowly - full revolution every ~200 positions', which is wrong (pair 3's period is 6283) and contradicts the '6,283 positions' the same widget renders in the pair-3 card. Separately, the curriculum specced a single 'PositionEncodingVisualizer' with a sinusoidal/RoPE/ALiBi toggle and a sequence-length control to visualize extrapolation; the chapter instead ships two separate widgets with no ALiBi view and no extrapolation demo (this matches the research file's own recommendation, so it is a spec-vs-delivery divergence rather than a defect).


### Chapter 7 — grade B-

A well-structured, voice-compliant chapter that teaches the modern data pipeline clearly, with correct MinHash/LSH math and — unusually for this book — every chapter cross-reference pointing to the right target. It is dragged down by three real problems: the primary quality-filter runnable demo is broken (every sample, including the one labeled "expect ok," is under the 100-char length threshold, so the demo only ever exercises the length filter and its printed output contradicts its own labels), the headline DCLM claim overstates the source ("outperforms" vs the paper's "comparable to" Llama-3 8B, plus a dataset-size/training-token conflation), and a specced marquee corpus (RefinedWeb) plus a specced tricky spot (the Phi synthetic-data debate) are missing. Solid content with a few load-bearing defects.

**Strengths:** Cross-reference integrity is clean: Chapters 1-6 (architecture), Chapter 8 (training loop), Chapter 13 (synthetic data in SFT) are all cited to the correct canonical targets — no stale numbering, the defect this book is prone to.; The MinHash/LSH core is technically sound: the Broder probability argument, the S-curve 1-(1-s^r)^b, and the concrete (k=200, b=50, r=4 → transition ~0.38) worked example are all correct, and the exercise-2 (b,r) transition annotations (~0.38, ~0.74, ~0.10) check out.; The exact-dedup and decontamination runnable blocks execute correctly and genuinely demonstrate their point (the n-gram decon block correctly shows a paraphrase slipping through with zero 13-gram overlap).; Voice contract is respected — direct, opinionated, no forbidden filler phrases, no first-person-singular, headings name the thing.; arXiv IDs are accurate (Pile 2101.00027, Lee 2107.06499, FineWeb 2406.17557, DCLM 2406.11794), and the FineWeb-Edu 9% ≈ 1.3T figure is internally consistent with the 15T FineWeb size.

**Findings:**

- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** Every one of the five demo samples is under 100 characters (the "expect ok" normal sentence is 87 chars; the repetitive/spam/Japanese samples are 67/78/6 chars), so length_ok is False for all of them and the loop prints reason "too short/long" for every row — including the sample labeled "expect ok", which is shown as "drop". The repetition filter and language filter are never actually exercised, so the demo teaches the opposite of its intent and contradicts its own inline labels ("expect repetitive", "expect wrong-language").
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch07-pretraining-data/index.mdx:307-322 (samples) with threshold at line 289 "length_ok = 100 < length < 1_000_000"; sample at line 309 "(\"A normal sentence about training data quality and modern pipelines for LLM pretraining.\", \"expect ok\")"
  - _fix:_ Lower the length threshold for the toy demo (e.g. min length 20) or lengthen the samples so the good sentence passes and each junk sample trips the filter it is meant to illustrate; verify the printed reason column matches the inline expectation labels.
- **[major/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** The DCLM paper frames DCLM-7B as *comparable to* Llama-3 8B (matching on an average of ~53 tasks with ~6.6x less training compute; Llama-3 8B actually scores higher on MMLU), not as outperforming it — the chapter states "outperforms/beats" four times. Also the flagship DCLM-7B was trained on ~2.5-2.6T tokens; 3.8T is the size of the DCLM-Baseline pool, not the training-token count, so "trained on a ... 3.8T-token corpus" and "roughly a quarter of the data" (2.6/15 ≈ 1/6) are imprecise.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch07-pretraining-data/index.mdx:13 "a 7B model trained on a carefully filtered 3.8T-token corpus outperforms LLaMA-3 8B trained on 15T tokens"; repeated at :23 and :400
  - _fix:_ Soften to "comparable to / competitive with LLaMA-3 8B while using ~6.6x less training compute," and separate the 3.8T dataset size from the ~2.6T training tokens.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** RefinedWeb (Penedo 2023, arxiv 2306.01116) is entirely absent, yet the curriculum lists it explicitly under "Reader will be able to" ("Name and characterize the major pre-training corpora (Common Crawl, RefinedWeb, FineWeb, The Pile, RedPajama)") and in Key concepts, and it is a designated research key paper. RefinedWeb was the influential "web-only, aggressively filtered, can beat curated mixtures" result (Falcon) that sets up the FineWeb/DCLM story the chapter builds on.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch07-pretraining-data/index.mdx:386 "## Modern datasets: a timeline" (RefinedWeb never appears anywhere in the file)
  - _fix:_ Add RefinedWeb to the dataset timeline (2023, between RedPajama and FineWeb) with its one-line thesis: filtered web-only data can match curated mixtures.
- **[minor/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** GPT-3's dataset totals ~499B tokens (410+19+12+55+3 from Table 2.2) and the model was trained on ~300B tokens; neither is 570B. The number 570 is the *disk size in GB* of GPT-3's filtered CommonCrawl, not a token count — the figure has been mislabeled as tokens (the same slip is present in the research file).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch07-pretraining-data/index.mdx:390 "Total: roughly 570B tokens."
  - _fix:_ Replace with "~300B training tokens (from a ~500B-token dataset)"; if the 570 figure is wanted, attribute it as ~570 GB of filtered CommonCrawl.
- **[minor/math-error/CONFIRMED]** The MinHash Jaccard estimator is a mean of k Bernoulli(J) indicators, so its standard error is sqrt(J(1-J)/k), which is at most 1/(2*sqrt(k)) (maximized at J=0.5). The stated 1/sqrt(k) overstates the error by up to 2x: at k=200 the worst-case SE is ~3.5%, not 7%, and at a typical dedup threshold J=0.8 it is ~2.8%. Same over-statement in the code comment at line 231 and for the k=64/1000 figures.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch07-pretraining-data/index.mdx:167 "its standard error is roughly $1/\sqrt{k}$. With $k = 200$ hash functions, expect about 7% error"
  - _fix:_ State SE = sqrt(J(1-J)/k) ≤ 1/(2*sqrt(k)); note ~3.5% worst-case at k=200. If keeping 1/sqrt(k) as a loose bound, label it a (conservative) upper bound rather than the expected error.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** Doc 1 ("buy buy buy click here free shipping limited time act now act now") has unique/total = 9/13 = 0.69 — higher than the non-spam Doc 4 (0.65), and well above the >0.3 repetition threshold the chapter uses later — so it is NOT flagged by the repetition signal and is not "notably low." The claim is contradicted by the code's own output and by the chapter's own filter threshold.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch07-pretraining-data/index.mdx:73-74 "Doc 1 (the spam-like one) has a notably low unique-word ratio. That signal alone: repetition, is enough to flag it for the quality filter."
  - _fix:_ Either pick a spam sample with genuinely low unique-word ratio (heavy verbatim repetition) or drop the claim that repetition alone flags this document.
- **[minor/widget-coverage/CONFIRMED]** The specced DedupInteractive is "paste two documents, see exact / fuzzy(MinHash) / semantic(embedding cosine) scores update live." The delivered widget is a fixed 8-document precomputed-Jaccard matrix with a clustering threshold slider — no live editing, no MinHash computation, and no semantic/embedding channel at all. The author reasonably followed research Q5's alternative recommendation, and the caption is honest that values are hand-computed, but the semantic-dedup dimension (a specced key concept) is lost in both widget and prose.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch07/DedupInteractive.tsx (whole widget) vs CURRICULUM spec line 402
  - _fix:_ Acceptable as a design deviation, but add at least a short prose treatment of semantic/embedding-based near-dup so the specced "exact, MinHash, semantic" trio is complete; optionally add a semantic-similarity column.
- **[minor/exercise/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 3's provided corpus contains "clean" documents that are under 100 chars (corpus[0] "A normal sentence about training data quality and pretraining." ~61 chars; corpus[5] "Quantum entanglement..." ~85 chars), so a correctly-implemented pipeline drops the good docs on length and the "final kept set" the exercise asks students to report is dominated by the Lorem-ipsum placeholder — muddying the intended lesson about which filter does the most work.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch07-pretraining-data/index.mdx:528 "def length_filter(text, min_len=100, max_len=10000)" applied to corpus at :559
  - _fix:_ Lengthen the clean corpus samples past 100 chars (or lower min_len in the scaffold) so the pipeline keeps the genuinely clean docs and rejects the junk for the intended reasons.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- RefinedWeb (Penedo 2023, arxiv 2306.01116) — a specced major corpus in both 'Reader will be able to' and Key concepts, and a research key paper — is never mentioned.
- Semantic / embedding-based deduplication (specced key concept 'deduplication (exact, MinHash, semantic)') is absent from the prose, and the DedupInteractive widget omits the semantic/cosine channel the spec calls for; near-dup coverage stops at MinHash.
- The synthetic-data debate (specced tricky spot: Phi-1.5/Phi-3 'Textbooks Are All You Need', whether it scales or hits a ceiling, 'be honest about this') is reduced to a single paragraph footnote — Phi is never named and the scaling/ceiling debate is not engaged.
- Gopher quality-filter rules (Rae 2021) are not named or attributed; the specific Gopher rule list (mean line length, stopword fraction, fraction of duplicate lines, etc.) requested by the research is not given, only a generic heuristics list.
- DoReMi (Xie 2023) and data-mixture optimization are absent; data mixing (an in-scope-and-locked topic) is mentioned only in passing in the bridge/timeline, with no treatment of how mixture weights are chosen or why high-quality sources are upweighted.
- The 'tokens, not documents' point (an in-scope-and-locked item) is used implicitly ('trillions of tokens') but never stated as an explicit teaching point.

**Exercises:** Four meaningful, well-scaffolded exercises that map cleanly onto the chapter (MinHash + Jaccard/SE verification, LSH banding S-curve, multi-filter quality pipeline, decontamination + paraphrase limitation). Hints are correct and the (b,r) transition annotations in Exercise 2 (~0.38, ~0.74, ~0.10) are accurate; Exercise 4 correctly sets up the paraphrase-evasion demonstration. One weakness: Exercise 3's supplied corpus includes clean documents under the 100-char min-length filter, so a faithful implementation drops the good docs and the intended 'which filter does the most work / final kept set' lesson is muddied. Otherwise genuine, not filler.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx and render. QualityFilter is internally consistent and computes what the prose says: heuristic pass/fail booleans are hand-set per sample, the classifier column is computed as qualityScore >= threshold, and the dropsAlone/'classifier is most aggressive' claims check out (classifier alone drops 7/10 at threshold 0.5, length alone drops 4). DedupInteractive works (precomputed true 5-shingle Jaccard + union-find clustering + threshold slider) and its caption is honest that values are hand-computed rather than live MinHash. However, DedupInteractive diverges materially from the curriculum spec (which asked for paste-two-docs live editing with exact/fuzzy/semantic scores) and, together with the prose, drops the specced semantic/embedding-cosine dimension entirely — the author followed the research file's alternative recommendation instead, which is defensible but leaves 'semantic' dedup uncovered.


### Chapter 8 — grade C+

The prose is genuinely excellent: direct, opinionated, technically accurate in every core derivation (cross-entropy, Adam, AdamW decoupling, warmup+cosine, clipping), with clean voice and correct cross-references throughout. The problems are concentrated in the reader-facing runnable/interactive artifacts and in spec coverage: the OptimizerComparison widget teaches the OPPOSITE of its intended lesson (SGD converges fastest and smoothest here), the body cross-entropy demo prints output that contradicts its own narration, an exercise states a wrong expected value, and a "tricky spot" explanation contradicts its own numbers. TinyStories — a specced key concept and the named training target — is entirely absent. The core math is sound; the illustrations and coverage are where it slips.

**Strengths:** Every core derivation is correct and clearly motivated: the likelihood factorization, log-sum-exp stability, Adam moments + bias correction, the AdamW coupled-vs-decoupled analysis, and the warmup+cosine formula (all boundary/continuity claims verified).; Voice and tone are exemplary — no forbidden phrases, strong owned opinions ('AdamW is the default, Adam-plus-L2 is the historical mistake'), no filler. Callouts effectively target the curriculum's named misconceptions.; Cross-reference integrity is clean: every chapter pointer (Ch 2 embeddings, Ch 4 attention, Ch 5 block, Ch 6 positional, Ch 7 data, Ch 9 scaling, Ch 10 infra, Ch 17 KV cache, Ch 1 autodiff) maps correctly to the canonical numbering.; The forward-pass 'T predictions in parallel' and causal-masking section is a genuinely strong pedagogical treatment of why next-token prediction is sample-efficient.; The bf16-vs-fp16 dynamic-range explanation and the FP32-master-weights callout hit the curriculum's tricky spots precisely and correctly.

**Findings:**

- **[major/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The widget's own trajectories contradict its narration. With computeSGD(0.04) the x-update contraction factor is 1-0.04*20=0.2, so SGD converges monotonically to (3.0,1.001) with loss 0.000 and never overshoots; computeAdam(0.3) actually overshoots to x=3.994 before settling at loss 0.023. The rendered widget shows SGD as the fastest, smoothest optimizer with the lowest final loss — the opposite of the intended 'why not SGD' lesson.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch08-building-small-llm/index.mdx:218 (caption) + src/components/widgets/ch08/optimizer-data.ts:76,96 — "Watch how SGD oscillates badly, Adam smooths the path via adaptive scaling" / SGD "overshoots in x (steep gradient)"
  - _fix:_ Recalibrate learning rates so SGD visibly oscillates/overshoots in the steep x-direction (e.g. SGD lr ~0.09–0.11 so 1-lr*20 is negative, and reduce Adam lr toward ~0.1 so Adam does not overshoot), then re-verify trajectories match the caption.
- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The demo draws logits ~ N(0,1), which are NOT near-uniform, so by Jensen the cross-entropy exceeds log(V). The code prints loss=5.2996 next to 'Theoretical baseline (uniform over 100): 4.6052' and then asserts they are ≈ equal — a visible contradiction (0.69 above). It also contradicts the chapter's own correct claim at line 23 that a random-init model's next-token distribution is 'nearly uniform'.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch08-building-small-llm/index.mdx:109 (with 106-108) — "A random-init language model produces loss ≈ baseline initially."
  - _fix:_ Either draw near-uniform logits (e.g. np.random.normal(0, 0.02, ...)) so the printed loss actually lands ≈ log(V), or change the narration to explain that spread-out (N(0,1)) logits give loss strictly ABOVE log(V) and only small-variance init gives loss ≈ baseline.
- **[major/exercise/CONFIRMED]** A correct AdamW reference (lr=0.1, weight_decay=0.05, 200 steps, start x=0) converges to x≈2.998, not 2.5–2.9. The weight-decay pull on this quadratic is negligible, so the stated expectation is off by ~0.1–0.5 and will make students with a correct implementation believe they are wrong. It also contradicts the body demo's own framing that decay tugs only 'slightly'.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch08-building-small-llm/index.mdx:744 — "print(f\"Final x = {params[0]:.4f} (expected: < 3.0, somewhere around 2.5-2.9)\")"
  - _fix:_ Change the expected range to ~2.98–3.00, or increase weight_decay (e.g. 0.5–1.0) so the convergence point is visibly pulled below 3 as the comment implies.
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** Self-contradiction inside the gradient-accumulation 'tricky spot': the running example uses batch 4 → effective 32 = eight micro-batches (accum_steps=8, stated on lines 360 and 364), but the explanation says backward is called 'four times' summing 'four gradients'. Four vs eight directly clashes with the surrounding numbers and the 1/8 scaling.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch08-building-small-llm/index.mdx:372 — "calling `.backward()` four times sums four gradients. To match the gradient that a single batch of size 32 would have produced, each of the eight micro-batch losses needs to contribute 1/8"
  - _fix:_ Change 'four times sums four gradients' to 'eight times sums eight gradients'.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** TinyStories (Eldan & Li 2023, arxiv 2305.07759) is a specced Key concept, the named 'Reader will be able to' training target ('Train a working GPT-style model from scratch on TinyStories'), and the feasibility-demonstrator (small model → coherent English). The chapter never mentions it and substitutes Tiny Shakespeare, losing the specific pedagogical point that a <10M-param model produces coherent English when the data is simple.
  - _at:_ context/CURRICULUM.md:438,445,458 vs chapter (grep: no 'tinystories'/'eldan' match anywhere in index.mdx)
  - _fix:_ Add a short treatment of TinyStories as the feasibility demonstrator (or re-anchor the widget/closeout to it), citing Eldan & Li 2023.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The specced LossCurveInteractive should show 'the attention pattern at that step (cached snapshots)' alongside loss and generated sample. The delivered widget shows loss, learning rate, and sample text but no attention pattern.
  - _at:_ context/CURRICULUM.md:449 vs src/components/widgets/ch08/LossCurve.tsx (renders loss/lr/sample only)
  - _fix:_ Add cached attention-snapshot rendering per step, or note in the spec that the attention panel was intentionally cut.
- **[minor/math-error/CONFIRMED]** For GPT-2 small (the stated reference style: V=50257, d=768) the tied matrix is 50257×768 = 38.6M, not ~50M; the chapter's own code config (d=384) would save only ~19M. 'About 50M' overstates for the referenced scale.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch08-building-small-llm/index.mdx:156 — "weight tying drops about $50$M parameters with no measured cost to quality"
  - _fix:_ State ~39M for GPT-2 small (or specify GPT-2 medium, d=1024, ≈51M, if that is the intended anchor).
- **[minor/math-error/CONFIRMED]** 3.28e9 tokens / 124M params = 26.6 tokens/param, closer to 27 than to 20. The 'Chinchilla regime' framing is fine but the number is ~30% low.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch08-building-small-llm/index.mdx:429 — "about $20$ tokens per parameter at GPT-2's $124$M"
  - _fix:_ Say 'about 26 tokens per parameter' (still comfortably in the Chinchilla regime around 20).
- **[minor/consistency/CONFIRMED]** The reference model size is stated inconsistently: ~50M (§5 and closeout), ~10M (widget panel), '10M to 100M' (intro/closeout); the actual code config (d=384, 6 layers, vocab 50257) is ~30M. A ~50M char-level model on ~1MB Tiny Shakespeare (line 930) would also be badly oversized.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch08-building-small-llm/index.mdx:281,435,930 — "around 50M parameters" (281) vs "~10M params" (widget 435) vs "$\sim 50$M parameters" (930)
  - _fix:_ Pick one figure for the reference model and use it consistently; for the char-level Tiny Shakespeare run, ~10M (matching the widget) is the defensible number.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** LLaMA-1 and LLaMA-2 7B both used peak LR 3e-4, not 1e-4, and the chapter references LLaMA prominently elsewhere; GPT-3 6.7B used 1.2e-4. The 'larger models want smaller peak rates' trend is right but the specific 7B figure is recipe-dependent and contradicts the most-cited 7B recipe.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch08-building-small-llm/index.mdx:281 — "$\alpha_{\max} \approx 1 \cdot 10^{-4}$ for $7$B and above"
  - _fix:_ Soften to a range (e.g. '~1–3e-4 for 7B, depending on recipe') or cite the specific recipe the number comes from.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** The O(K^2)→O(K) asymptotic argument is correct, but a 100×–1000× wall-clock speedup from KV caching is optimistic for typical context lengths (real end-to-end gains are usually ~10–100× and memory-bandwidth-bound). Slightly hyperbolic for a forward reference.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch08-building-small-llm/index.mdx:537 — "the wall-clock factor is comfortably $100\times$ to $1000\times$ at long context"
  - _fix:_ Temper to '~10×–100× at long context' or attribute the large factor to very long sequences specifically.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- TinyStories (Eldan & Li 2023) — specced Key concept, the named 'Reader will be able to' training target, and the feasibility-demonstrator — is entirely absent; the chapter uses Tiny Shakespeare instead and never makes the small-model-coherent-English point.
- 'Batch construction (packed vs padded sequences)' — a specced Key concept — is not discussed anywhere in the chapter prose (only obliquely present as a research TID).
- 'Read and interpret training-loss curves, including warmup, plateau, and divergence patterns' — the chapter shows a smooth monotonic curve but never teaches how to read a warmup bump, a plateau, or a divergence/spike pattern.
- Tricky spot 'interaction between AdamW's weight decay and the LR schedule (decay coefficient multiplied by current LR, often a surprise)' is underdeveloped: the (1−αλ)θ formula appears, but the chapter never explicitly says the effective weight decay scales with the changing LR across the schedule.
- The specced second reference script full_training_run.py (production tricks: gradient accumulation, mixed precision, checkpointing, distributed hooks) is not delivered; the runnable loop omits AMP and grad-accum despite the summary at line 630 implying the loop 'runs all of it'.
- Model checkpointing (save/resume of model+optimizer state) and the glossary terms 'epoch' and 'loss scaling' are not covered; the chapter names only 'gradient checkpointing' (activation recomputation), a different concept, and defers the rest to Ch 10.
- Training instability at scale (large-model loss spikes requiring intervention such as batch skipping) is only touched via a passing 'occasional gradient spikes' mention, not the explicit treatment the curriculum tricky-spot asked for.

**Exercises:** Four exercises, well-scaffolded and mapped to outcomes 1/3/4/6. Ex1 (cross-entropy) and Ex3 (LR schedule) are correct with good asserts. Ex4 (tiny end-to-end loop) is well-built — the TinyGPT scaffold with nn.TransformerEncoderLayer and a boolean causal src_mask is correct and runnable, and the ~3.0 baseline (log(28)=3.33) is reasonable. Ex2 (AdamW from scratch) has a CONFIRMED defect: its stated expected outcome '(expected: < 3.0, somewhere around 2.5-2.9)' is wrong — the correct reference implementation converges to ~2.998 because weight decay at λ=0.05, lr=0.1 on this quadratic is negligible; a student with a correct solution will think they failed. Ex1's 'loss ≈ log(V)' framing is also imprecise (actual ~5.04 vs 4.61; assert tolerance 0.5 barely passes at 0.43), the same conceptual slip as the body demo.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx and render. LossCurve is faithful to its prose caption (baseline log(82)=4.4 → 1.22, sample snapshots) BUT omits the attention-pattern snapshots the curriculum explicitly specced. OptimizerComparison is materially broken pedagogically: I reproduced the exact trajectories from optimizer-data.ts and SGD (lr=0.04) converges monotonically to (3.0,1.0) with loss 0.000 by ~step 6 in x with NO oscillation and NO overshoot, while Adam (lr=0.3) overshoots to x=3.99 and ends at loss 0.023. The caption 'Watch how SGD oscillates badly, Adam smooths the path' and the data-file description 'overshoots in x' are both false — the widget shows SGD as the clear, smooth winner, refuting the section's entire 'why not SGD' argument. This is a learning-rate calibration bug (SGD lr too small for the x-curvature, Adam lr too large).


### Chapter 9 — grade B-

A well-written, well-organized chapter whose distributed-training half (DP/TP/PP/FSDP, 3D parallelism, communication primitives, memory reasoning) is excellent and technically sound, with a ParallelismDiagram widget that matches the prose exactly. But the scaling-law half carries a genuine, propagating numerical error: the stated Chinchilla parametric equation with the published constants does NOT have its compute-optimal at ~20 tokens/parameter (the true optimum is D/N ≈ 90 at C=6e23 and drifts with C). This false claim infects the marquee runnable code (prints D/N ≈ 320–700 while its comment claims "sits near 20"), the marquee widget (the Llama-3 marker plots lower loss than the "optimal" Chinchilla marker), and Exercise 2 (a correct implementation yields 60–123, not the ~20 the hint promises). There is also an internal memory-accounting inconsistency (12 bytes/param in the prose vs 18 bytes/param in Exercise 3). Coverage vs the spec is otherwise strong.

**Strengths:** The distributed-training half is genuinely strong: DP ring all-reduce (2(N-1) steps, ~2*model_size per GPU), Megatron column-then-row TP, GPipe bubble fraction (N-1)/(M+N-1), and FSDP all-gather/reduce-scatter are all explained correctly and at the right depth.; The ParallelismDiagram widget (parallelism-data.ts) is accurate and matches the prose: DP=full model + sharded batch + all-reduce; TP=sharded layers + full batch + per-layer all-reduce; PP=different layers + peer-to-peer; FSDP=sharded params+batch + all-gather/reduce-scatter.; Reference numbers that were checkable are correct: 7B BF16 = 14GB, AdamW fp32 m/v = 56GB; H100 ~1000 TFLOPS BF16; 6ND FLOP estimate and the ~1.3yr / ~20yr single-GPU time estimates; MT-NLG 8x35x64 = 17,920 GPUs; Chinchilla constants E=1.69/A=406/B=410; 32-stage/32-microbatch bubble ~50%.; Honestly addresses the curriculum's flagged tricky spots: the Kaplan-to-Chinchilla shift, Llama-3 8B on 15T (~100x past Chinchilla-optimal) for inference economics framed as a lower bound, and 'FSDP is essentially ZeRO-3'.; Cross-references are all correct (Chapter 8 training loop, Chapter 10 infrastructure, Ch 1-6 architecture, Ch 7 data, Ch 13/17 forward pointers) and the EqRef 9.chinchilla resolves correctly. No forbidden-phrase violations.

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The marquee runnable block's compute_optimal() uses k_N=0.6, k_D=1.7 with a=beta/(alpha+beta)=0.4516, giving D/N = (1.7/0.6)*(C/6)^0.0968. I ran it: it prints D/N = 320.6, 400.7, 476.5, 500.7, 695.9 for C=1e22..3e25 — nowhere near 20, and N at C=6e23 is 14.6B (not the ~7B the prose implies). The comment and the surrounding prose that the code makes the '~20 tokens/parameter' pattern visible are directly contradicted by the code's own output.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch09-scaling-and-distributed/index.mdx:106 (and :102, :83) — code comment "# Walk through several compute budgets: note the D/N ratio sits near 20." and prose "makes the \"$\sim 20$ tokens per parameter\" pattern visible across orders of magnitude."
  - _fix:_ Either recalibrate k_N/k_D so D/N lands near 20 at Chinchilla scale (k_D/k_N ~ 0.12, not 2.83), or — more honestly — replace with a brute-force minimize-along-6ND=C and state plainly that with the published parametric constants the equation's own optimum is ~90 tokens/param (the '20' rule comes from Chinchilla's isoFLOP approaches, not this equation).
- **[blocker/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The ScalingLawCalculator hardcodes the Chinchilla marker at r=20 and labels it 'The mathematical optimum' that 'Minimizes loss given a fixed compute budget'. But chinchillaLoss() with these constants has its curve minimum at r≈93 (I computed the analytic and brute-force optimum). At C=6e23 the plotted losses are: Kaplan(r=1.7)=1.9740, Chinchilla(r=20)=1.9355, Llama-3(r=250)=1.9317, true-min(r≈93)=1.9289. So the Llama-3 marker renders BELOW the 'optimal' Chinchilla marker, and the '★ optimum' does not sit at the bottom of its own curve — the widget visually contradicts the lesson and its own description of Llama-3 as having a loss penalty.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch09/chinchilla-data.ts:64-66 (ratio:20, "The mathematical optimum.") and :75 (Llama-3 "Slight loss penalty during training")
  - _fix:_ Compute the marker at the actual argmin of the plotted iso-compute curve (drifts with C), and reserve the 'optimum' label for that point; keep r=20 only as an annotated 'rule of thumb' distinct from the equation's true minimum. Adjust the Llama-3/Chinchilla descriptions to match the plotted ordering.
- **[major/exercise/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 2 asks the student to brute-force minimize L along 6ND=C and asserts the optimum is near r=20 for all C. I ran the intended solution over logspace(-1,3.3,200): the optimum is r = 61.3, 78.6, 91.3, 95.9, 123.0 for C=1e22..1e25 — not 20, and it clearly drifts with C (contradicting 'regardless of C'). A student who implements the exercise correctly will conclude their code is wrong.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch09-scaling-and-distributed/index.mdx:405 "The optimum should be near $r = 20$ regardless of $C$" (and :441 "# Verify: D/N should be near 20 regardless of C")
  - _fix:_ State the correct expected result (optimum ~60-120, increasing with C for these constants) and explain the discrepancy with the 20-token rule, or change the exercise's constants so the parametric optimum actually lands near 20.
- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** The prose presents ~20 tokens/param as the compute-optimal ratio derived from the just-stated equation and its fitted alpha/beta. It is not: minimizing E+A/N^0.34+B/D^0.28 subject to 6ND=C gives r_opt = ((B*beta)/(A*alpha))^(2/(alpha+beta)) * (C/6)^((alpha-beta)/(alpha+beta)) ≈ 93 at C=6e23, and r_opt=20 only near C~8e16 (a toy budget). The '20 tokens/param' figure is empirical (Chinchilla Approaches 1-2 / isoFLOP), not a consequence of the parametric-fit constants quoted here; the chapter conflates them.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch09-scaling-and-distributed/index.mdx:75 "the compute-optimal ratio works out to $D_{\text{opt}} / N_{\text{opt}} \approx 20$"
  - _fix:_ Add one sentence distinguishing the isoFLOP '~20 tokens/param' result from the parametric-equation optimum, or note that the parametric fit's implied ratio is higher and compute-dependent (a known Chinchilla-replication subtlety).
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** The chapter uses two incompatible memory accountings. Sections 1 and FSDP use 12 bytes/param (7B -> 84GB; 84/8 -> ~11GB per GPU under FSDP on 8 GPUs). Exercise 3 uses 18 bytes/param (7B -> 126GB; 126/8 -> ~15.75GB per GPU). A reader who does Exercise 3 gets ~15.75GB for the exact 7B/8-GPU FSDP case the prose says is ~11GB, and 126GB total vs the prose's 84GB — a direct numeric contradiction across the same chapter.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch09-scaling-and-distributed/index.mdx:461 "Total: $2N + 4N + 4N + 8N = 18N$ bytes per GPU" vs :253 "$\sim 14$ GB params + $\sim 14$ GB gradients + $\sim 56$ GB FP32 optimizer state = $\sim 84$ GB" and :267 "drops from $\sim 84$ GB to $\sim 11$ GB"
  - _fix:_ Pick one convention. If keeping the prose's 12 bytes/param (2 param + 2 grad + 8 optimizer, no fp32 master copy), drop the +4N master-weights and fp32-grad terms in Exercise 3; if keeping 18, update Sections 1 and FSDP (84->126GB, 11->15.75GB).
- **[minor/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 4's scaffolded DP volume uses the full model size and ignores both dp_rank and tp_rank sharding, contradicting its own hint on line 527 ('volume ~ 2 * model_size / DP-rank') and physical reality (under TP=8 each rank's gradient all-reduce covers model/TP params, not the full model). The provided starter formula overcounts DP communication by a factor of tp_rank.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch09-scaling-and-distributed/index.mdx:560 "# dp_volume = 2 * (2 * model_params)  # 2 bytes per param (FP16 grads), all-reduce factor 2"
  - _fix:_ Divide the DP gradient volume by tp_rank (per-rank params) in the scaffold, or note explicitly that the toy formula ignores sharding and is an upper bound.
- **[minor/currency/CONFIRMED]** arXiv 1811.06965 was posted in November 2018 (NeurIPS 2019). The curriculum spec (context/CURRICULUM.md:506) cites 'Huang 2018 (GPipe)'. The chapter's '2019' is inconsistent with the arXiv year and the spec; Megatron is cited as '2019' correctly (1909.08053), so the mismatch is only for GPipe.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch09-scaling-and-distributed/index.mdx:221 "GPipe (Huang et al. 2019, [arxiv.org/abs/1811.06965]...)"
  - _fix:_ Cite GPipe as 2018 (arXiv) or '2018/2019' to match the spec, or standardize on publication years across all citations.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** GELU is element-wise, but SwiGLU denotes a gated FFN variant (Swish(xW1) ⊙ xW3), not a pure element-wise activation — it involves a second projection and a gated product. The load-bearing claim ('stays local, no communication') is still correct under Megatron column-parallel, but calling SwiGLU 'element-wise' is imprecise.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch09-scaling-and-distributed/index.mdx:213 "The activation function (GELU or SwiGLU) is element-wise and stays local."
  - _fix:_ Reword to 'the nonlinearity (GELU, or the gated SwiGLU) is applied per-element within each rank's slice and needs no communication' or similar.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** For a mid-2026 reader the chapter uses H100/InfiniBand as the frontier hardware and never mentions the Blackwell generation (B200/GB200), which shipped through 2025. Not wrong, but the hardware framing reads slightly dated, and the research spec's 'H100 has ~3 TB/s HBM bandwidth' number is not surfaced at all.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch09-scaling-and-distributed/index.mdx:21-23 (H100 as the running frontier-GPU reference throughout)
  - _fix:_ Optionally add a one-line note that H100 is used as a concrete example and that newer accelerators (Blackwell-class) shift the constants but not the reasoning.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- The research spec's 'numbers to know' includes H100 ~3 TB/s HBM bandwidth; the chapter gives compute (TFLOPS) and interconnect bandwidth but never the HBM bandwidth figure, which is the relevant number for activation/parameter streaming.
- ZeRO stages 1/2/3 are named (optimizer/gradient/parameter sharding) but only in a single sentence; the ~2x/4x/Nx memory-reduction-per-stage progression from the research file is not shown, so 'ZeRO-stage-N' selection (a stated learning objective) is thin.
- The compute-optimal derivation is stated with the correct exponent a=beta/(alpha+beta) but, more importantly, the chapter never reconciles that this exponent applied to the quoted constants does not reproduce the ~20 rule it teaches — leaving the single most load-bearing quantitative claim of the scaling half unsupported by its own equation.

**Exercises:** Four exercises with good scaffolding and escalating difficulty. Exercise 1 (Chinchilla loss + monotonicity) is correct and its claimed property (Chinchilla 70B/1.4T loss 1.936 < GPT-3 175B/300B loss 2.002) verifies. Exercise 3 (per-GPU memory by strategy) is meaningful and mostly correct but uses 18 bytes/param, inconsistent with the chapter body's 12 bytes/param. Exercise 4 (3D-parallel comm time) is a reasonable hard/open-ended problem, though its DP-volume scaffold ignores dp_rank/tp_rank sharding, contradicting its own hint. Exercise 2 is the problem: its central claimed result ('optimum near r=20 regardless of C') is false for the given constants — a correct brute-force yields r_opt = 61–123, drifting with C — so a diligent student's correct answer will appear to disagree with the exercise.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx and render. ParallelismDiagram is accurate: memory layouts (full/shard/empty per layer), batch distribution, and communication arrows (all-reduce for DP/TP, peer-to-peer for PP, all-gather + reduce-scatter for FSDP) all match the prose faithfully — no issues. ScalingLawCalculator is the problem: it computes chinchillaLoss along the 6ND=C iso-compute curve correctly, but hardcodes the 'Chinchilla optimal ★' marker at r=20 and labels it 'The mathematical optimum,' when the plotted curve's actual minimum is at r≈93. As a result the Llama-3 marker (r=250, loss 1.9317) renders below the 'optimal' Chinchilla marker (r=20, loss 1.9355), directly contradicting both the widget's own description and the chapter's teaching. The widget computes real numbers, but they falsify the label it puts on them.


### Chapter 10 — grade B-

A well-written, voice-clean infrastructure tour with correct cross-references (notably free of the numbering drift seen elsewhere in the book) and two solid, prose-aligned widgets. But it carries several concrete numeric/technical errors — including a spurious factor in the FlashAttention I/O-complexity statement that the curriculum explicitly flagged as a must-get-right tricky spot — a broken Exercise 3 whose provided code prints negative memory, and it delivers only ~2 of the 4 curriculum learning objectives (compiler stacks and observability/fault-tolerance are essentially absent). Faithful to its own research file, which de-scoped those objectives, but still short of the assigned spec.

**Strengths:** Cross-reference integrity is clean: every 'Chapter N' (Ch 9 for parallelism, Ch 11 MoE, Ch 13 post-training, Ch 1-8 recap) and every 'Section N' self-reference resolves correctly against the canonical map and the 8-section structure.; Prose voice is disciplined and jargon is introduced before use; no forbidden-phrase violations spotted.
