The 'misconception' warning callouts are genuinely useful and mirror the research file's MC1-MC7.; Both specced widgets exist, are wired through the barrel, and compute what their captions claim: TrainingStackPicker uses 6ND FLOPs + Chinchilla 20 tok/param and picks the simplest stack that fits; StepTimeline models overlapped time as max(compute,comm) vs sequential compute+comm. No placeholder/broken widgets.; Core hardware specs (H100 989/1979 TFLOPS, A100 312 TFLOPS, MI300X 192GB/5.3TB/s/1.3PFLOPS, NVLink 900 vs 600 GB/s) and the ring all-reduce cost derivation 2(n-1)/n are correct.; The activation-memory and MFU runnables execute cleanly and print self-consistent numbers (107.4/21.5/10.7 GB; 19.0% MFU), and the arxiv IDs (2205.14135, 2307.08691, 2205.05198) are correct.

**Findings:**

- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** The N×N attention score matrix has N^2 elements, so materializing/reading it is O(N^2) HBM I/O, not O(N^2 d). The FlashAttention paper (Dao 2022) states standard-attention HBM accesses are Θ(N^2 + Nd). The spurious d on the leading term also contradicts the chapter's own Exercise 2 code (line 503: attn_matrix_io = 4 * N * N * dtype_bytes, i.e. O(N^2)). This is the chapter's centerpiece derivation, which the curriculum flags as a tricky spot to get right.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:223 — "The total HBM I/O is $O(N^2 d + Nd)$, dominated by the $N \times N$ attention matrix"
  - _fix:_ Change to O(N^2 + Nd) (memory-footprint claim O(N^2) on the next line is already correct). Optionally align the FlashAttention I/O with the paper's Θ(N^2 d^2 / M).
- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** 32000^2 × 2 bytes ≈ 2.05 GB, not 'over 4 GB'. The 4 GB figure corresponds to FP32 (4 bytes), so this looks like an FP16/FP32 mix-up.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:223 — "For $N = 32{,}000$, the attention matrix alone is over $4$ GB in FP16."
  - _fix:_ State '~2 GB in FP16' (or '~4 GB in FP32').
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** MT-NLG 530B was trained on ~2240 A100s = 8 × 35 × 8, i.e. DP=8. DP=64 implies 8×35×64 = 17,920 GPUs, wildly inconsistent with the documented ~2240-GPU run. TP=8 and PP=35 are correct; only DP is wrong.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:89 — "The Megatron-Turing $530$B configuration of TP$=8$, PP$=35$, DP$=64$ is not arbitrary"
  - _fix:_ Change DP=64 to DP=8.
- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** STATE_GB evaluates to 157.5, so the provided (non-TODO) reference code prints 'State memory per GPU: 157.5 GB' and 'Available for activations: -77.5 GB' (negative). The adjacent comment '70B * 18 bytes / 8 = 157 GB total / 8 ~= 19 GB/GPU' double-divides by 8 and is self-contradictory, and the hint (line 545) claims 17.5 GB/GPU using 2 bytes/param. Three inconsistent numbers; the exercise premise (full 18-byte optimizer state of 70B on 8×80GB) does not physically fit.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:566-571 (Exercise 3) — "STATE_GB = (70e9 * 18) / 8 / 1e9" and "Available for activations: {H100_MEM_GB - STATE_GB:.1f} GB"
  - _fix:_ Reconcile bytes/param and sharding: e.g. use ~16+ GPUs, or shard consistently and print a positive available-activation budget; fix the comment arithmetic and align the hint.
- **[major/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** Llama 3 pretraining was done in BF16 per the Llama 3 paper; FP8 there was used for inference/quantization of the 405B, not training matmuls. The canonical FP8-*trained* frontier model is DeepSeek-V3 (Dec 2024).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:303 — "FP8 is in production at the frontier, Llama-3's training used FP8 for some of its matmuls"
  - _fix:_ Replace the Llama-3 FP8-training example with DeepSeek-V3, or restate as 'Llama-3 used FP8 for inference'.
- **[major/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** The Llama 3 paper reports ~30.84M H100 GPU-hours for 405B pretraining (8B 1.46M, 70B 7.0M, 405B 30.84M). The '16M' figure appears to conflate the ~16,384-GPU count with GPU-hours; the derived dollar range is correspondingly low.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:375 — "**Llama-3 405B** is estimated at $\sim 16$M GPU-hours, around $\$50$M–$\$80$M"
  - _fix:_ Use ~31M GPU-hours for 405B and update the cost estimate accordingly.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** For a mid-2026 reader the spectrum omits H200 — which the CURRICULUM key-concepts list names explicitly ('H100/H200') — and NVIDIA Blackwell (B200/GB200 NVL72, NVLink 5.0), the frontier training substrate by 2026. The chapter is explicitly '2024'-framed, which softens this, but H200 is a specced omission.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:35 — "### GPU spectrum (2024)" (H100/A100/GH200/MI300X)
  - _fix:_ Add H200 and at least a forward-looking note on Blackwell/NVLink 5.0.
- **[minor/reference-error/CONFIRMED]** H100 has 50 MB of L2 cache; 40 MB is the A100 figure. Relatedly, line 52 '~128 KB per SM' understates the H100 (228 KB configurable shared / 256 KB combined L1+shared; 128 KB is V100-era).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:53 — "**L2 cache**, $\sim 40$ MB total on an H100"
  - _fix:_ Use 50 MB L2 for H100; note shared memory is ~228 KB/SM on H100.
- **[minor/consistency/CONFIRMED]** The exercise title claims ~20x but the hint (and 900/25 arithmetic) yields ~36x. Internal contradiction.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:420 vs 432 (Exercise 1) — title "Verify the $\sim 20\times$ speed difference" vs hint "The $\sim 36\times$ gap"
  - _fix:_ Make the title ~36x, or use IB≈45 GB/s to land near 20x.
- **[minor/consistency/CONFIRMED]** For the identical config, the runnable at line 351 computes 107.4 GB ('several hundred' overstates ~2-5x). Related multiplier drift: prose says '~6x memory reduction' (293/364) while the main code models 10x (none 10·bsd → full 1·bsd), and the Ex-3 hint (543) calls selective checkpointing a '10x reduction' while the main code models selective as 2·bsd (5x).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:289 — "the per-micro-batch activations sum to several hundred GB"
  - _fix:_ Reconcile the activation coefficient and the reduction multipliers across the prose, the main runnable, and the Ex-3 hint.
- **[minor/pedagogy/CONFIRMED]** The runnable prints 19.0% MFU — below even the 'un-tuned 20-30%' band it then lists — with no note that 500K tok/s is a deliberately low figure (a well-tuned 8B run is ~1M+ tok/s at ~40% MFU). A reader who runs it gets a flagship example that matches none of the reference bands.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:392 — "Llama-3 8B on 128 H100s, achieving ~500K tokens/sec"
  - _fix:_ Raise tokens_per_sec to ~1M so the example lands in a labeled band, or annotate that 500K represents an un-tuned run.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** The canonical FlashAttention-1/2/3 reference implementations are hand-written CUDA/CUTLASS, not Triton (a Triton port exists in the Triton tutorials but is not the reference). Line 213's 'Triton, originally developed at OpenAI' is also imprecise — Triton originated in Tillet's 2019 academic work (the very paper cited in the research file) and was later adopted by OpenAI. Note the CURRICULUM itself frames FlashAttention as 'a Triton-kernel exemplar,' so this follows spec.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch10-training-infra/index.mdx:262 — "FlashAttention uses the same Triton machinery"
  - _fix:_ Soften to 'the same tiling/online-softmax/SRAM strategy' and note the reference impl is CUDA; attribute Triton's origin to Tillet 2019.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- 'Recognize compiler stacks (PyTorch 2 compile, Triton, JAX/XLA) and when each appears' — a full curriculum learning objective. The chapter covers Triton but has NO coverage of torch.compile or JAX/XLA, and the curriculum's tricky spot 'compile vs no-compile tradeoff (stable shapes/standard ops vs dynamic shapes/unusual control flow)' is entirely absent.
- 'Describe how training observability and fault tolerance work at scale' — a full curriculum learning objective, essentially unmet. No Weights & Biases / TensorBoard, no distributed/model checkpointing for restart, no fault recovery. The chapter's 'checkpointing' section is activation checkpointing (a memory-optimization), not fault-tolerance checkpointing.
- NeMo and Lightning (named in key concepts 'Articulate the role of Megatron, DeepSpeed, NeMo, and torch-native FSDP') are not mentioned at all.
- T5X/MaxText (Google's stack) and JAX/GSPMD — listed in key concepts — are absent, so the reader gets no exposure to the TPU/Google training ecosystem.
- H200 (key concepts 'H100/H200') is not covered in the GPU spectrum. Note: the chapter faithfully follows research.md, whose own 8 outcomes (lines 540-550) already dropped the compiler-stack, observability/fault-tolerance, NeMo/Lightning, and JAX items — so these gaps trace to a research-file de-scope, but they remain gaps against the assigned CURRICULUM spec.

**Exercises:** Four meaningful, non-filler exercises mapping to the intended outcomes (bandwidth math, FlashAttention I/O, activation memory, MFU+cost), each with a clear TODO template and a collapsed hint. Ex2 and Ex4 are sound scaffolds (they legitimately print nothing until the student fills the TODO). Two defects: (1) Ex3's provided, non-TODO reference code computes STATE_GB=157.5 and prints 'Available for activations: -77.5 GB' (negative), and its three state-memory figures disagree ~8x — code 157.5 GB/GPU, its own comment '~19 GB/GPU', and the hint '17.5 GB/GPU'; the premise (full 18-byte state of a 70B model on 8x80GB) is itself physically over budget. (2) Ex1's title says 'Verify the ~20x speed difference' but its hint computes '~36x' (900/25). Both need reconciliation.

**Widgets:** Both curriculum-specced widgets are present as .tsx and match the prose. TrainingStackPicker (src/components/widgets/ch10/) correctly shards state across DP/FSDP/Megatron+FSDP/Megatron-DeepSpeed (18 bytes/param), recommends the first stack that fits, and estimates MFU/time/cost — exactly what the WidgetFrame caption at index.mdx:193 promises. StepTimeline's math (sequential total=compute+comm, overlapped=max, MFU=compute/total) matches its caption at index.mdx:317. Minor deviations, none blocking: the widget GPU list (h100/a100/mi300x) omits GH200 though the prose covers it; the megatron_fsdp rationale hardcodes 'TP=8' text even when tpRank=min(8,gpuCount)<8; and the CURRICULUM widget spec's suggested 'JAX/MaxText' stack option is absent (defensible, since the chapter never covers JAX). No computational errors in either widget.


### Chapter 11 — grade B-

A conceptually strong, well-ordered chapter with clean prose, correct cross-references (notably it gets "the transformer block, from Chapter 5" right where other chapters err), and honest treatment of MoE's downsides. But it has a blocker-level defect: the central parameter-counting code (and Exercise 2's scaffolding, and the ActiveVsTotalParams custom configurator) uses a 2-matrix FFN, so it computes 32.2B/9.7B for Mixtral and thus contradicts the 46.7B/12.9B numbers repeated ~10 times in the prose and in the code's own trailing comment. There are also two genuine technical errors (the fine-tuning "1/N per expert" arithmetic, and an Exercise-2 "same memory footprint" comment that inverts the chapter's own thesis) plus a labeled equation whose gate definition contradicts the renormalization the chapter and curriculum stress.

**Strengths:** All eight cross-references are correct against the canonical map (Ch5 transformer block, Ch4-6 attention stack, Ch15 PEFT, Ch12 SSM), avoiding the numbering drift the audit specifically warns about.; Three of four runnable code blocks (moe_forward, aux_load_balance_loss demo, and the exercises 1/3/4 scaffolds) are correct and would run and produce the claimed qualitative results.; Strong, misconception-driven pedagogy: the three warning callouts (56B fallacy, more-params-means-more-compute, MoE-always-wins) directly target the curriculum's flagged misconceptions.; Voice contract is respected: no forbidden phrases, correct we/you tense, headings name the thing, honest 'MoE loses on simplicity' framing.; Good coverage of the memory-vs-compute split, load balancing, capacity/dropped tokens, and the Switch->GLaM->Mixtral->DeepSeek-V3 progression with the aux-loss-free refinement.

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The parameter-count code uses a classic 2-matrix FFN, but Mixtral/Llama use gated SwiGLU with 3 matrices per expert. Running the chapter's own moe_parameter_count(8,2,4096,14336,32) prints total=32.2B, active=9.7B — flatly contradicting the 46.7B/12.9B repeated throughout the prose, the code's own trailing comment (L339 'Mixtral uses ~12B params per token'), and Exercise 2's stated verification target ('published: 46.7B / 12.9B'). Verified numerically: 2-matrix gives 32.2B/9.7B, 3-matrix SwiGLU gives 47.2B/13.4B (matching published). The custom configurator in ActiveVsTotalParams shows the same defect: a Mixtral-config custom bar reads 33.3B/10.7B beneath the labeled real 46.7B/12.9B Mixtral bar.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:310  "ffn_per_expert = 2 * d_model * d_ffn" (also L479 in Exercise 2, and model-data.ts computeCustomMoEParams)
  - _fix:_ Change ffn_per_expert to 3 * d_model * d_ffn (SwiGLU: w1, w3 up-projections + w2 down) in the body code (L310), Exercise 2 scaffold (L479), and computeCustomMoEParams in src/components/widgets/ch11/model-data.ts. Note that the dense Llama-2 70B path (4d^2 MHA attention) will also still undercount because Llama-2 70B uses GQA; add a one-line caveat that these are simplified estimates.
- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** The labeled central equation (Equation 11.moe) defines the gate as a softmax over the full N-logit vector, but the prose 85 lines later (L141) explicitly insists the opposite: 'softmax is not applied over the full N-logit vector and then masked; it is applied over the k selected logits', and the code (L96) does softmax(top_k_logits). The curriculum flags this exact renormalization as a tricky spot. As written, the equation is the older GShard convention and yields different gate values than the renormalized top-k convention the chapter teaches.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:56  "g_i(x) = \text{softmax}(W_r x)_i"
  - _fix:_ Rewrite the gate as a softmax renormalized over the selected experts, e.g. g_i(x) = exp(l_i) / sum_{j in top_k} exp(l_j), so the equation matches the prose and code.
- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** With top-2 routing over 8 experts, each expert sees k/N = 2/8 = 1/4 of tokens under balanced routing, i.e. ~2500 examples-worth, not 1250. The number 10000/8=1250 assumes top-1 (1/N), which contradicts the stated 'top-2 routing'. The bolded claim 'each expert sees roughly 1/N of the data' is only true for top-1.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:368  "A fine-tuning corpus of $10$K examples becomes effectively $\sim 1250$ examples per expert in an $8$-expert model with top-2 routing."
  - _fix:_ Either state 'each expert sees roughly k/N of the data' and compute 10000 x 2/8 = ~2500, or drop the specific 'top-2' qualifier if the intent was the top-1 (1/N) case.
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** Mixtral's memory footprint is set by its TOTAL 46.7B params (~93GB in BF16), not by its active count. It is nowhere near Llama-2 13B's footprint (~26GB). This inverts the chapter's own central teaching (L163: 'GPU memory needs the total parameter count'). The intended point is that Mixtral's COMPUTE/active cost (~12.9B) is Llama-2-13B-like.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:511  "Same memory footprint as Llama-2 13B; quality closer to Llama-2 70B."
  - _fix:_ Change to 'Same per-token compute as Llama-2 13B (active ~13B); memory footprint of a ~47B model; quality closer to Llama-2 70B.'
- **[minor/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The FFN equation at L48 is defined with GeLU ('FFN(x) = W_2 * GeLU(W_1 x)'), but the runnable implementation uses ReLU. Minor internal inconsistency between the stated activation and the code.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:104  "h = np.maximum(x[t] @ expert_W1s[expert_idx].T, 0)   # ReLU"
  - _fix:_ Use GeLU in the code, or note that ReLU is used for simplicity, or change the L48 equation to a generic nonlinearity sigma(.).
- **[minor/math-error/CONFIRMED]** 5.6 x 6 = 33.6, not 33.8. The 33.8 is reverse-engineered from 46.7 - 12.9; the per-expert figure and the product are mutually inconsistent by rounding.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:159  "The other $5.6$B $\times 6 \approx 33.8$B of expert parameters"
  - _fix:_ Use 33.6B, or round the per-expert figure so the arithmetic closes.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** For the textbook's mid-2026 readers the modern-variants survey stops at DeepSeek-V3 (Dec 2024) and omits 2025 MoE developments that predate the Jan-2026 cutoff: notably Llama-4 (MoE), Qwen3-MoE, DeepSeek-R1/V3.1, and Kimi K2. The 'as of late 2024' hedge bounds the claim but the section reads stale for its audience.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:295  "It is, as of late $2024$, the current open-weights state of the art on most benchmarks."
  - _fix:_ Add a short forward-looking sentence acknowledging that 2025 frontier open-weights models (e.g. Llama-4, Qwen3-MoE, DeepSeek-R1) are predominantly MoE, reinforcing the chapter's thesis while updating currency.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** The original DeepSeekMoE (arXiv 2401.06066, the paper named here) uses 64 routed + 2 shared experts; '256+ per layer' is DeepSeek-V3 territory (256 routed). The number is attributed to the wrong model.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:291  "First, **fine-grained experts**: many smaller experts (256+ per layer)"
  - _fix:_ Attribute '256 routed experts' to DeepSeek-V3, and describe the original DeepSeek-MoE as splitting into finer-grained experts (64 routed) relative to Mixtral's 8.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** Mixtral 8x7B weights were dropped via magnet/torrent in December 2023; the paper (arXiv 2401.04088) and formal announcement were January 2024. 'released the weights publicly in January 2024' is slightly off on the weight-release date.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:287  "Mistral released the weights publicly in January $2024$"
  - _fix:_ 'Mistral released the weights (via torrent) in December 2023, with the paper following in January 2024.'
- **[minor/code/CONFIRMED]** The sparsity printout hardcodes 2 rather than the variable k. It happens to be correct for the demo (k=2), but a reader editing k to experiment gets a misleading, un-updated sparsity line.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch11-moe/index.mdx:126  "Sparsity: {2}/{num_experts} = {2/num_experts:.0%}"
  - _fix:_ Interpolate k: f"Sparsity: {k}/{num_experts} = {k/num_experts:.0%} ...".

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Grok is specced in 'Reader will be able to: Recognize modern MoE designs: Mixtral, DeepSeek-V3, Grok' but is never mentioned in the chapter. Grok-1 (314B total, 2-of-8 experts active, open-sourced March 2024) is a notable data point and is missing from the Modern MoE variants section.
- The MoERoutingVisualizer widget spec calls for user-adjustable 'gating temperature and load-balance penalty' with routing changing in real time; the delivered widget only offers a top-k toggle over static hand-tuned logits, so the specced interactive exploration of temperature and the aux-loss penalty is absent.
- Expert parallelism (a listed Key Concept) is only named in passing (all-to-all collectives, sharded experts) and never actually explained as a distribution strategy; the reader gets the memory-resident point but not how tokens are dispatched across expert-parallel ranks.
- The curriculum research scope lists GShard (Lepikhin 2020) and the MoE scaling-laws paper (Krajewski 2024) as key sources; neither the GShard lineage nor MoE-specific scaling laws are mentioned, leaving the 'is MoE better per training-FLOP' claim (L373) asserted without the scaling-law grounding the research file intended.
- Per the voice/citation contract (link arxiv abstract pages when citing), the chapter contains zero inline arxiv links despite citing ~8 papers (Shazeer, Switch, GLaM, Mixtral, ST-MoE, DeepSeek-MoE, DeepSeek-V3); acceptable-but-thin given research.md carries the reading list.

**Exercises:** Four meaningful, well-scaffolded exercises (not filler): forward-pass top-1/top-2, parameter counting, aux load-balance loss, and a capacity/drop sweep. Exercises 1, 3, 4 are correct and the scaffolding TODOs are sound. Exercise 2 is broken in two ways: (a) its FFN formula `ffn_per_expert = 2 * d_model * d_ffn` yields ~32.5B total / ~9.9B active, so the exercise's own "Verify against Mixtral 8x7B's published numbers (46.7B total, 12.9B active)" fails by ~14B; (b) the trailing comment "Same memory footprint as Llama-2 13B" is false and contradicts the chapter's memory=total-params thesis (Mixtral needs ~93GB in BF16, not Llama-2-13B's ~26GB) — it conflates active-compute with memory.

**Widgets:** Both widgets exist and render real logic. MoERoutingVisualizer correctly implements top-k routing and correctly marks Expert 4 under-utilized, but diverges from the spec (which wants gating-temperature and load-balance-penalty sliders; only a top-k toggle is implemented). ActiveVsTotalParams uses correct hardcoded real-model bars, but its custom configurator's 2-matrix FFN formula makes a Mixtral-config custom bar read 33.3B/10.7B directly beneath the real 46.7B/12.9B Mixtral bar.


### Chapter 12 — grade B+

A technically strong, well-voiced chapter with clean derivations, runnable numpy that traces correctly, and cross-references that all resolve (the FlashAttention→Chapter 10 citations are correct — Ch 10 carries the FlashAttention walkthrough; every post-training forward-ref is right). The main defects are a factor-of-2 memory number in the opening hook that even contradicts the chapter's own widget, a scaling widget whose "crossover" annotation is inconsistent with the FLOPs it actually plots, and an under-developed treatment of the Mamba-2 SSD duality (a specced key concept reduced to "matmul is faster"). Coverage is otherwise faithful; the code and exercises are meaningful and correct.

**Strengths:** Derivations are correct and carefully staged: the ZOH B-bar formula (ΔA)⁻¹(exp(ΔA)−I)ΔB, the recurrence→convolution unrolling, and the associative-operator composition for the parallel scan all check out line by line.; All runnable code traces correctly and would produce the claimed output; the duality-verification cell genuinely matches recurrence vs convolution to machine precision, and the half-life arithmetic (0.693/(Δ|a|)) is right.; Cross-reference integrity is excellent — FlashAttention→Ch10, MoE→Ch11, and the SFT/alignment/PEFT/distillation forward-refs (Ch13–16) are all correct against the canonical map, and internal 'Section N' refs are consistent.; Honest, non-overselling framing throughout (where SSMs lose: needle-in-haystack, in-context learning, short-context wall-clock), matching the curriculum's required 'honest assessment'.; Voice matches the house style — punchy, concrete, no forbidden announcement-filler; the 'shutter speed', 'decisive note-taking', and 'compressed memory' analogies land well.

**Findings:**

- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** BF16 is 2 bytes/element. 32,768² ≈ 1.07e9 entries × 2 bytes ≈ 2.1 GB, not 4 GB; at 1M tokens 10¹² × 2 ≈ 2 TB, not 4 TB. Both numbers are 2× too high (they correspond to 4 bytes/FP32). The chapter's own widget contradicts the prose: scaling-data.ts attentionMemory uses seqLen²×2 ≈ 2.1 GB at 32K.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch12-ssm-and-mamba/index.mdx:11 — "the attention matrix has a billion entries, about $4$GB in BF16 just to hold the scores for a single layer... four terabytes"
  - _fix:_ Change to '~2 GB in BF16' and 'two terabytes', or keep 4 GB/4 TB but state FP32 instead of BF16.
- **[major/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The widget plots FLOPs/bytes, on which SSM is strictly cheaper across the entire visible 256–1M range (the attention and SSM FLOP curves cross at N≈24, off the left edge; at N=1000 attention already uses ~42× more FLOPs). Yet the widget draws a vertical 'crossover (~7K)' line and asserts attention 'dominates' below it, while the comparison ratio simultaneously reads '42× more' for attention. A wall-clock crossover concept is overlaid on a FLOPs/memory plot that does not support it, so the annotation contradicts the plotted quantity.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch12/SSMvsAttentionScaling.tsx:150 — "Below crossover; attention's matmul efficiency dominates." with scaling-data.ts:54 crossoverSeqLen → 7200
  - _fix:_ Either drop the crossover line from the FLOPs/memory plot, or explicitly label it a wall-clock (constant-factor) crossover distinct from the plotted asymptotic cost, so the 'attention dominates below crossover' note isn't read against curves that show the opposite.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum lists 'Mamba-2 and the SSD (Structured State Space Duality) framework' as a key concept, and the paper's headline result is that attention is itself a special case of a structured masked SSM ('Transformers are SSMs'). The chapter reduces Mamba-2 to 'matmul is faster' and only alludes to the duality once (line 15, 'matrix-form computation that connects SSMs to attention'). The actual duality claim — that attention and SSMs are two views of one framework differing in the structure of the mask M — is never explained.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch12-ssm-and-mamba/index.mdx:366 — "Dao and Gu reformulate selective SSMs by restricting $A$ to a scalar per channel... the entire computation can be expressed as a structured matmul."
  - _fix:_ Add a short paragraph stating the SSD claim: both attention and selective SSMs are structured-masked matmuls differing in the mask (attention's data-dependent softmax mask vs Mamba-2's 1-semiseparable decay mask). Research derivation Y=M⊙(XW_C)W_B^T X is available in research.md.
- **[minor/coverage/CONFIRMED]** Samba (Ren et al. 2024) and Zamba (Glorioso et al. 2024) are named in the 'Reader will be able to' outcome and in the research scope, but neither appears in the delivered chapter. Only Jamba is discussed by name (Falcon Mamba / Codestral are cited as pure-Mamba, not hybrids).
  - _at:_ context/CURRICULUM.md:637 — "Recognize hybrid architectures (Jamba, Samba, Zamba) and why they exist"; chapter names only Jamba (index.mdx:398)
  - _fix:_ Add one sentence naming Samba (Mamba + sliding-window attention) and Zamba (shared-attention hybrid) as other points in the hybrid design space.
- **[minor/math-error/CONFIRMED]** N is defined as the state dimension (h∈ℝᴺ), and d_state is the same quantity, so 'O(N·d_state) per token' spuriously squares the state dimension; a diagonal SSM step is O(N) per channel (O(d_state·d_model) across channels). The Exercise 2 hint then reuses N to mean sequence length in 'O(N log N) overall', flipping the symbol's meaning within one hint.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch12-ssm-and-mamba/index.mdx:160 — "$O(N \cdot d_{\text{state}})$ time per token"; Exercise 2 hint (index.mdx:506) "$O(N \cdot \text{state\_dim})$ per token... $O(N \log N)$ overall"
  - _fix:_ Use O(d_state) (or O(d_state·d_model)) for the per-token cost and reserve a distinct symbol (T) for sequence length in O(T log T), as the main text correctly does at line 161.
- **[minor/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** The bars denote discretized parameters, but Linear_B/Linear_C produce the pre-discretization B and C. In Mamba, C is applied without discretization (no bar), and B is discretized separately via Δ_t (B̄_t = Δ_t·B_t) — exactly as the runnable code does (B_bar = delta_t * B_t; C_t used directly). The displayed equation puts bars on both, mismatching the code and the algorithm.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch12-ssm-and-mamba/index.mdx:252 — "$\bar{B}_t = \text{Linear}_B(x_t), \quad \bar{C}_t = \text{Linear}_C(x_t)$"
  - _fix:_ Write B_t = Linear_B(x_t), C_t = Linear_C(x_t) (no bars), then B̄_t = Δ_t B_t; note C is not discretized.
- **[minor/currency/CONFIRMED]** The widget computes and labels the crossover at ~7K, while the surrounding prose (caption and Callout) states ~8K. Small but visible inconsistency between the number the reader is told and the number the widget draws.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch12/scaling-data.ts:54-56 (returns 7200 → "~7K") vs index.mdx:36 "the crossover is somewhere around $8$K tokens" and :31 "The crossover (~8K tokens)"
  - _fix:_ Reconcile to a single figure (bump crossoverSeqLen to 8000 or change the prose to ~7K).
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** Stated in the Jamba subsection, but Jamba's published ratio is 1 attention layer per 8 (a:m = 1:7), i.e. ~12.5% attention / ~87.5% Mamba, not 20/80. The '80/20' overstates the attention fraction relative to the canonical example being discussed.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch12-ssm-and-mamba/index.mdx:398 — "The current common pattern is roughly $80\%$ Mamba layers and $20\%$ transformer layers"
  - _fix:_ State Jamba's actual 1:7 (≈12.5% attention) ratio, or soften to 'roughly one attention layer per handful of Mamba layers' without a specific 20%.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** The chapter is explicitly timestamped 'late 2024' and its landscape snapshot predates several production hybrid-SSM releases a mid-2026 reader would expect (e.g., Zamba2, NVIDIA Hymba, and 2025-era hybrid-SSM models), and cites 'reportedly GPT-4' as the MoE frontier exemplar. The chapter is honest about its timestamp, so this is dating rather than an error.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch12-ssm-and-mamba/index.mdx:430 — "MoE transformers (Mixtral, DeepSeek-V3, reportedly GPT-4)" and :400 "as of late $2024$"
  - _fix:_ Optionally add a dated 'as of publication' hedge and note that production hybrids matured through 2025; low priority given the explicit timestamp.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- SSD / 'Transformers are SSMs' duality (specced key concept) is under-developed — reduced to 'matmul is faster'; the core claim that attention is a special case of a structured-masked SSM is never explained.
- Hybrid architectures Samba and Zamba, named in the 'Reader will be able to' outcome, are absent — only Jamba is discussed.
- The ZOH derivation is stated as a formula but not 'walked through' as the curriculum's tricky-spot asks ('the reader needs intuition for why this gives a recurrence'); acceptable at the recommended depth but on the thin side.
- The 'Repeat After Me'/copying weakness (research honest-assessment item, Jelassi/Wen 2024) is covered conceptually via needle-in-haystack but the specific copying limitation is not named.

**Exercises:** The four exercises are meaningful and well-matched to the learning outcomes (Ex1 diagonal recurrence/impulse response, Ex2 recurrence-convolution duality, Ex3 selective vs fixed-Δ, Ex4 associative parallel scan). Scaffolding is sound: the commented reference solutions I traced (compute_kernel, ssm_convolution, and the scan_via_associative composition A_new=A_t·composed_A, b_new=A_t·composed_b+b_t) are correct and would verify against the provided direct implementations. Difficulty labels (easy/medium/medium/hard) are appropriate. Minor: Ex2's hint carries the same O(N·state_dim)-per-token notation slip flagged above, and Ex3's fixed_delta_ssm takes an unused C argument.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx and are wired into the widget index. SelectiveScanAnimation is faithful to the prose: 16 hand-tuned tokens (capital/France/Paris/weather/rainy/winter as important), 8 state components with distinct eigenvalues, and simulateStateEvolution implements h_i = exp(Δ·a_i)·h_i + Δ·B·x exactly as described — caption and computation agree. SSMvsAttentionScaling correctly implements attention 4dN² / SSM 6·d_state·dN and the O(N²) vs O(N) slopes, but its 'crossover' line (hardcoded 7200) and 'attention dominates below crossover' annotation are inconsistent with the FLOPs it plots (SSM is strictly cheaper across the whole visible range; curves cross at N≈24 off-screen) — the flagged major widget/prose mismatch. crossoverSeqLen correctly ignores d_model (d cancels), so that is not a bug.


### Chapter 13 — grade B+

A well-written, honest, pedagogically strong chapter that nails the core SFT recipe, loss masking, and chat templates, with clean cross-references and two widgets that compute exactly what the prose claims.

**Strengths:** Every cross-reference is correct: Chapter 8 (training loop), 10 (FSDP/infra), 14 (preference opt), 15 (PEFT), 16 (distillation), 22 (RAG), and Chapters 11/12 (MoE/SSM) all map correctly; EqRef id=8.loss and 13.sft both resolve to real equations. No chapter-numbering drift.; The loss-masking equation (13.sft) is correct and matches the curriculum spec, and the section-3 runnable is numerically sound (numerically-stable CE, correct axes/shapes, correct mask denominator).; Both widgets exist as .tsx, render correctly, and match the prose: SFTLossMasking computes 14/41 = ~34% in-loss exactly as the caption states, with role colors (violet/amber/cyan/gray) matching the CSS; ChatTemplateComparison renders all four templates with a correct feature table (system-role yes/yes/no/no).; Voice is direct, opinionated, and honest about SFT's lack of glamour, with essentially no forbidden-phrase violations; the LIMA 'shallow alignment' framing and the capability-tax/InstructGPT discussion are accurate and well-motivated.; Exercises 3 (multi-turn mask construction with state tracking) and 4 (multi-stage boolean filter) are genuinely non-trivial and well-scaffolded.

**Findings:**

- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** In Llama 3, `<|begin_of_text|>` IS the beginning-of-sequence (BOS) token (id 128000) — it is not distinct from BOS. The claim is factually wrong and self-contradictory with line 158, which correctly calls it "A document-level `<|begin_of_text|>`" (i.e., exactly the BOS role). A reader could conclude Llama-3 sequences carry both begin_of_text and a separate BOS.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch13-sft/index.mdx:143 — "Llama-3's `<|begin_of_text|>` is similarly one token, distinct from the BOS token."
  - _fix:_ Delete "distinct from the BOS token" or rephrase to "which is Llama-3's BOS token." If a contrast is wanted, contrast begin_of_text (BOS) with `<|end_of_text|>` (EOS) and `<|eot_id|>` (end-of-turn), as line 158 already does.
- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** With the hardcoded examples, the two archetypally-bad responses each incur a single ×0.5 penalty and land at exactly 0.5, so with `>= 0.5` they are KEPT: the 'As an AI language model...' meta-reference (kept) and the 200×-repeated padded response (kept). Meanwhile the correct answer 'What's 2 + 2?' → '2 + 2 equals 4.' (15 chars) trips the <20 length check (×0.3) and is REJECTED. The demo's output therefore keeps the bad examples and drops a good one, directly contradicting the surrounding prose that the heuristics flag low-quality data.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch13-sft/index.mdx:358 (keep threshold `keep = "yes" if s >= 0.5 else "no"`) with penalties at 329-339, prose at 315/362 "Even simple heuristics flag a noticeable fraction of synthetic examples."
  - _fix:_ Strengthen the penalties on the clearly-bad cases (e.g., AI-reference and padded → ×0.3) so they fall below 0.5, and/or exempt short-but-complete answers (raise or condition the too-short cutoff). Ensure the printed keep/drop decisions actually reject the AI-reference and padded examples and retain '2 + 2 equals 4.'
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** FLAN — a named curriculum Key Concept and, per research.md, the "central reference for 'SFT works' ... the empirical foundation" — is entirely absent, as are Dolly and Super-NaturalInstructions. The term 'instruction tuning' itself is never introduced (only 'instruction-tuned model' appears once). The data lineage starts at Self-Instruct (2022), skipping the FLAN (2021) instruction-tuning-at-scale origin, so the chapter's history begins mid-stream.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch13-sft/index.mdx:295-309 (synthetic-data section); grep confirms 0 occurrences of 'FLAN', 'Dolly', 'Super-Natural', or 'instruction tuning' anywhere in the file.
  - _fix:_ Add a short paragraph on FLAN / Super-NaturalInstructions (academic multi-task instruction tuning) and a mention of Dolly (Databricks' human-written set) before or alongside the Self-Instruct/Alpaca discussion, and introduce the term 'instruction tuning' explicitly.
- **[minor/coverage/CONFIRMED]** The Alpaca template format (the '### Instruction:' / '### Response:' text format) is specced as a chat template but never shown; it appears only as a dataset/recipe name. (Note the 'Reader will be able to' list only requires ChatML/Llama/Mistral/Gemma, so this is minor.)
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch13-sft/index.mdx:146-178 ("Four major templates"); curriculum Key Concepts lists "chat templates (ChatML, Llama 3, Alpaca, Mistral, Gemma)".
  - _fix:_ Optionally show the Alpaca instruction/response template alongside the other four, or note explicitly that Alpaca uses a plaintext '### Instruction/### Response' scheme rather than special tokens.
- **[minor/coverage/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum specs 'packing vs padding', but padding is never introduced — packing is contrasted with truncation instead. The pad-token baseline that packing improves on (and the labels=-100 masking convention from research TID3) is absent, so a reader doesn't learn what 'more efficiently' is relative to.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch13-sft/index.mdx:251 — "Packing stuffs multiple shorter conversations into one fixed-length sequence ... using GPU memory more efficiently at the cost of implementation complexity."
  - _fix:_ Name the padding baseline (pad tokens waste compute; packing avoids it) so the packing-vs-padding tradeoff the spec asks for is explicit.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** Native Mistral uses the [INST] template — as this same chapter states at line 168 ("Mistral's native template is simpler, [INST]") and as the widget's Mistral tab shows. Attributing ChatML adoption to Mistral is dubious and internally confusing (the same model family is presented as both a ChatML user and an [INST] user).

  - _fix:_ Drop the Mistral attribution or soften to 'adopted by many open models', keeping the [INST] statement as Mistral's native format.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** OpenOrca is a community/open reproduction of the Orca dataset, not a Microsoft release; only Orca 1 and Orca 2 are Microsoft. The '(Microsoft)' parenthetical reads as covering both.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch13-sft/index.mdx:307 — "**OpenOrca** and the **Orca-1/2** series (Microsoft)".

  - _fix:_ Drop the second 'is universal' (e.g., '...because it works and is cheap, not because it is clever').
- **[minor/voice/CONFIRMED]** Repeated bare 'Section N.' forward-pointer fragments sit in mild tension with the voice contract's forbidden 'sentence whose only function is to announce the next sentence' and the cross-reference convention (which asks for linked '§X.Y' anchors). The sections are also not visibly numbered, so a reader must count ## headings to resolve 'Section N'.

- **[minor/exercise/CONFIRMED]** The entire working solution for the ChatML formatter (including the return) is present verbatim as commented-out code, so the exercise reduces to uncommenting. Exercise 1 is likewise near-identical to the worked example in section 3.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch13-sft/index.mdx:490-501 (Exercise 2 starter body).
  - _fix:_ Replace the commented full solution with a partial skeleton (e.g., leave the range-tracking logic as the TODO) so the medium-difficulty exercise requires actual work.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- FLAN (Wei 2021/2022) — a named Key Concept and research.md's designated 'empirical foundation' for instruction tuning — is entirely absent.
- Dolly and Super-NaturalInstructions (both in the research 'in scope and locked' dataset list) are absent.
- The term and concept 'instruction tuning' is never introduced or defined, despite being the first Key Concept and a glossary item.
- The Alpaca chat-template format ('### Instruction:/### Response:') is specced under chat templates but only mentioned as a dataset.
- 'Packing vs padding' is specced, but padding (pad tokens, labels=-100 masking) is never introduced; packing is contrasted only with truncation.
- Widget interactivity is reduced from the curriculum's specced ChatTemplateVisualizer (type your own messages on the left, live re-render) to two preset-only display widgets; this matches research.md's refined recommendation but not the original curriculum widget spec.

**Exercises:** Four exercises covering the right outcomes (loss masking, ChatML formatting, multi-turn mask construction, synthetic-data filtering). Exercises 3 (state-tracking multi-turn mask, correctly noting the assistant's end-of-turn token must be in loss) and 4 (multi-stage boolean filter — notably better-designed than the section-7 demo, since its all()-based logic would actually reject the AI-reference and padded examples) are meaningful and well-scaffolded. Exercise 1 is nearly identical to the worked section-3 example, and Exercise 2 embeds its full solution as commented-out code, so both lean easy/giveaway for their stated difficulty. All four run as scaffolded (TODO/pass stubs are syntactically valid). Overall solid, with room to make Ex1/Ex2 demand real work.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx, are correctly imported/exported, and compute what the prose claims. SFTLossMasking: the 41-token ChatML conversation yields 14 in-loss tokens = 34.1%, matching the WidgetFrame's '~34%'; role colors (violet=system, amber=user, cyan=assistant) match the module CSS; the mask correctly includes the assistant end-of-turn markers as in-loss. One nit: the widget labels all 14 in-loss tokens 'assistant tokens' though 2 are special-role <|im_end|> markers, and the stats panel shows 'Assistant: 12' beside 'In loss: 14' without reconciling them. ChatTemplateComparison: renders ChatML/Llama-3/Mistral/Gemma correctly, with an accurate feature table (system-role yes/yes/no/no; special-token counts 2/4/4/2; correct EOT and role markers) consistent with the prose. Neither widget offers the curriculum-specced 'type your own messages' input — both use a fixed preset conversation — but that matches research.md's refined two-widget recommendation.


### Chapter 14 — grade B

A technically excellent chapter: the token-level MDP framing is genuinely clarifying, the DPO derivation is correct step-by-step, the PPO clip analysis is precise, the numpy code blocks would all run and produce the claimed behavior, and cross-references to other chapters are clean. However, it is missing an entire curriculum-specced learning objective and a topic named in the chapter's own title, Constitutional AI / RLAIF (Bai 2022), which is absent from the frontmatter, body, exercises, and even the upstream research file. Combined with a dead widget control (the DPO β slider does nothing) and a couple of internal count inconsistencies, the coverage gap pulls an otherwise A-grade chapter down to a B.

**Strengths:** The token-level MDP synthesis ("reweighted next-token prediction with a leash to the reference") is a strong organizing spine, previewed in section 2 and paid off in the section-10 table; it delivers the curriculum's second pedagogical centerpiece well.; The DPO derivation is mathematically correct at every step: closed-form optimal policy, inversion to r(x,y)=β log(π*/π_ref)+β log Z, cancellation of Z(x) in the Bradley-Terry difference, and the final loss all check out.; Code is correct and runnable: the PPO clipped-surrogate walk (min of clipped/unclipped, one-sided gating), the Bradley-Terry loss, the DPO loss, and the RLVR verifier all produce the behavior the prose claims; the DPO gradient signs in the Exercise-2 hint are right.; Internal section-number cross-references ("section 4", "section 11", etc.) are all consistent with the actual section order, and inter-chapter refs (Ch 13 SFT, Ch 15 PEFT, Ch 16 distillation, Chs 7-10 base model) are all correct per the canonical map.; GRPO is covered with genuine depth beyond the research file (k3 estimator, dead groups, Dr. GRPO's length/std-bias fixes, DAPO dynamic sampling, the difficulty-calibration intuition), and PPOClipExplorer/PreferenceLearningPipeline widgets are well-built and match the prose.

**Findings:**

- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** Constitutional AI / RLAIF (RL from AI feedback) is entirely absent from the chapter, despite being named in the chapter title ("...RLVR, CAI"), listed as a "Reader will be able to" objective ("Understand Constitutional AI / RLAIF"), named in Key concepts ("Constitutional AI (RLAIF — RL from AI feedback)"), and backed by a required paper (Bai 2022, arXiv:2212.08073). grep for constitution/rlaif/ai-feedback returns nothing in the chapter, and the upstream research.md (titled "RLHF, DPO, and RLVR") also omits it, so the gap is systematic.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch14-alignment/index.mdx:4 (frontmatter description: "walks four method families as answers to one weight-and-leash problem, classical RLHF ... DPO ... GRPO ... and RLVR"); topic absent from entire file
  - _fix:_ Add a section covering Constitutional AI / RLAIF: the self-critique-and-revise loop, using an AI (guided by a written constitution) to generate preference labels in place of humans, and how RLAIF slots into the same reward-model + PPO/DPO machinery this chapter already builds. Cite Bai et al. 2022.
- **[major/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The DPOLossLandscape β slider is a non-functional control. dpoLoss and dpoGradient never receive beta, the loss grid is memoized with empty dependencies (useMemo(..., [])), and currentLoss = dpoLoss(rChosen, rRejected) ignores beta. Sliding β changes only the label text; the landscape, loss readout, and gradient arrow are unaffected. This contradicts the MDX caption and the curriculum widget spec ("User adjusts β; sees how the loss reshapes preferences").
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch14/loss-landscape-data.ts:15 ("export function dpoLoss(rChosen, rRejected)") and DPOLossLandscape.tsx:19-24; MDX caption src/pages/ch14-alignment/index.mdx:302 ("Slide the implicit rewards or β to explore.")
  - _fix:_ Either remove the β slider (the loss is β-independent in implicit-reward coordinates), or re-label the axes as policy log-ratios log(π/π_ref) and compute reward = β·log-ratio so β visibly rescales the landscape; update the caption accordingly.
- **[minor/consistency/CONFIRMED]** Internal count contradiction: the intro (line 15, "Four families of methods divide the space") and the section-2 callout (line 26, "Four families, one frame") both say four families/answers, but line 23 says "three families ... three different answers." This is a leftover from an earlier three-method version and will confuse a reader on how many method families the chapter covers.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch14-alignment/index.mdx:23 ("The three families of methods correspond to three different answers")
  - _fix:_ Change line 23 to "four families ... four different answers" (RLHF, DPO, GRPO, RLVR) to match the rest of the chapter.
- **[minor/exercise/CONFIRMED]** The exercises intro claims four exercises, but five are delivered (Exercise 1 Bradley-Terry, 2 DPO, 3 RLVR, 4 length bias, 5 GRPO).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch14-alignment/index.mdx:582 ("Four exercises that build on the chapter's machinery.")
  - _fix:_ Change "Four exercises" to "Five exercises."
- **[minor/stale-cross-ref/CONFIRMED]** The EqRef points to equation 14.dpo, which is the final DPO supervised loss (line 294), yet the prose calls it "the constrained optimization" that "has a closed-form optimal policy." That inverts the logic: it is the KL-regularized RLHF objective (the unlabeled equation at line 125) that has the closed-form optimal policy; the DPO loss is the endpoint of the derivation, not the constrained optimization. A reader following the ref lands on the wrong object.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch14-alignment/index.mdx:264 ("the constrained optimization in <EqRef id=\"14.dpo\" /> below has a closed-form optimal policy")
  - _fix:_ Reword to "the KL-regularized RLHF objective from section 4 has a closed-form optimal policy ... which, substituted into Bradley-Terry, yields the DPO loss in <EqRef id=\"14.dpo\" /> below," or attach a label to the section-4 objective and point the EqRef there.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** For a mid-2026 reader the frontier-reasoning roster and the "late 2024 to early 2025" framing are ~1.5 years stale; later reasoning models (e.g., OpenAI's o3, announced Dec 2024 and released in 2025, and subsequent GRPO-lineage recipes) are not mentioned. The chapter does timestamp its claims, which softens the issue.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch14-alignment/index.mdx:953-955 ("as of late 2024 to early 2025 ... Frontier reasoning models, o1, DeepSeek-R1, Gemini Thinking, add stage four")
  - _fix:_ Add a one-line note that the reasoning-model line continued past R1/o1 through 2025-2026 (o3 and successors), keeping the timestamp but signaling the field moved on.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Constitutional AI / RLAIF (Bai 2022) — a titled topic, a "Reader will be able to" objective, and a Key-concepts item, entirely missing from the chapter.
- Ivison 2024 nuance that DPO leaves residual probability mass on dispreferred completions — curriculum flags this as the specific tradeoff behind the "DPO is easier RLHF without tradeoffs" misconception, but the chapter's DPO misconception callout (line 352) does not cover it.
- The PPO-vs-TRPO relationship (curriculum common-misconception: PPO is the simpler variant TRPO inspired) is never surfaced; the clip section presents PPO's trust-region behavior without naming TRPO.
- Curriculum "Runnable code" specced ppo_step.py (a minimal PPO update on a batch showing the per-token KL contribution) and grpo_step.py (a complete GRPO update demonstrating the dead group); these are delivered only as a scalar clip-illustration and as an exercise scaffold (Exercise 5) respectively, not as complete runnable updates.

**Exercises:** Five exercises (the intro miscounts them as \"Four\"), all meaningful and well-scaffolded with collapsed hints and TODO stubs rather than filler. Difficulty spread is sensible (Ex3 easy, Ex1/2/5 medium, Ex4 hard). Ex2's finite-difference cross-check against an analytical DPO gradient is a genuine learning device with correct target signs; Ex5's GRPO exercise correctly asks the reader to handle the std=0 dead-group case and verify the softmax gradient (1[j=t]-π(j)). The hint math is accurate throughout. No correctness defects in the scaffolding.

**Widgets:** All three imported widgets exist as .tsx and render real computations. PPOClipExplorer correctly implements clippedSurrogate = min(r*A, clip(r,1-eps,1+eps)*A) and the flat-region logic (flatRight for A>0, flatLeft for A<0) exactly matching the prose and the runnable code. PreferenceLearningPipeline (a bonus widget, not curriculum-specced) is solid. DPOLossLandscape's loss surface and gradient arrow are mathematically correct and match the caption — BUT its β slider is a dead control: dpoLoss/dpoGradient take no beta argument, and buildLossGrid is memoized with empty deps ([]), so moving β changes nothing on the plot, the loss readout, or the gradient. This contradicts both the MDX WidgetFrame caption (\"Slide the implicit rewards or β to explore\") and the curriculum widget spec (\"User adjusts β; sees how the loss reshapes preferences\"). In implicit-reward axes the DPO loss is genuinely β-independent, so the honest fix is to drop the slider or re-parameterize the axes as log-ratios scaled by β.


### Chapter 15 — grade B-

A well-written, technically strong chapter on the core LoRA/QLoRA material: the central math (128x per-matrix reduction, 840 GB full-FT budget, NF4 4x savings, ~44 GB QLoRA-70B) is all correct, the numpy runnables execute and print what the prose claims, and both widgets compute values that match the prose. It is let down by two exercise defects (Exercise 3's merge reference is transposed and fails the equivalence it claims; Exercise 2's stated takeaway #4 is backwards), a wrong FSDP cross-reference, and two explicitly specced learning outcomes that are simply not delivered (a PyTorch LoRA implementation, and the VeRA variant). Solid core, real gaps against the spec.

**Strengths:** Core LoRA/QLoRA math is correct and internally consistent: 4096x4096/131072 = 128x, 140+140+560 = 840 GB, NF4 35 GB, and the two memory runnables reproduce the widget's numbers.; The three numpy runnables in the body are correct and actually print the claimed results (step-0 equivalence, param-count table, QLoRA memory budget).; Misconception callouts are well-targeted and match the curriculum's 'common misconceptions' list (LoRA-at-inference, higher-rank-always-better, QLoRA-is-just-quantization).; Widgets exist, are wired into index.ts, and their computed figures (6.74B frozen / ~17M trainable; QLoRA-70B ~44 GB fits A6000) align with the prose.; Voice is clean and on-contract: direct, opinionated where the field has converged, no forbidden filler phrases detected.

**Findings:**

- **[major/exercise-code-bug/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 3 uses the transposed (row-vector) code convention where adapter-mode is `x @ A.T @ B.T`, so the merged weight that matches is `W_base + (alpha/r) * (A.T @ B.T)`. The hint's math 'BA' translated literally to `B @ A` is the TRANSPOSE and does NOT match. Because the dims are square (256x256) it silently produces a wrong result instead of a shape error. Verified numerically: `B@A` merge gives max diff 0.032 vs adapter mode, whereas `A.T@B.T` gives 1.2e-15. The exercise's whole point ('merging produces exactly the same output') therefore fails for the intended/reference implementation, and a reader will conclude their correct-looking code is broken or that merging is not exact.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch15-peft/index.mdx:500 (adapter fn `return x @ W_base + x @ A.T @ B.T * (alpha / r)`) with hint at :484 (`W_{\text{merged}} = W_0 + \frac{\alpha}{r} B A`); same bug shipped in research/ch15-peft/research.md:429 (`W_merged = W_base + (alpha / r) * (B @ A)`)
  - _fix:_ Change the merge to `W_base + (alpha/r) * (A.T @ B.T)` (equivalently `(B @ A).T`) in the research reference and make the Exercise-3 hint state the transpose explicitly, or make d_in != d_out so a `B@A` attempt raises a shape error and forces the correct orientation.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** An explicit 'Reader will be able to' outcome and a specced runnable (lora_pytorch.py: freeze base, train only adapters) are entirely undelivered. The chapter never shows a PyTorch LoRA layer, a frozen-base training loop, or requires_grad handling. Readers cannot meet the outcome from this chapter.
  - _at:_ CURRICULUM.md:818 ('lora_pytorch.py — wrap a small PyTorch model with LoRA adapters, freeze base weights, train only the adapters') and :803 ('Implement LoRA in PyTorch by wrapping linear layers'); ch15-peft/index.mdx has zero `torch`/`nn.Module`/`requires_grad` and all runnables use `packages={["numpy"]}` or `{[]}`
  - _fix:_ Add a PyTorch runnable/example that wraps a nn.Linear with a LoRALinear module, sets base weights to requires_grad_(False), and runs a few optimizer steps showing only adapters update.
- **[major/stale-cross-ref/CONFIRMED]** FSDP's sharding mechanism is taught in Chapter 9 (Scaling & distributed training), which has a dedicated 'FSDP: the modern default' section and the exact 'shard parameters, gradients, optimizer state / does not reduce total memory' framing; the curriculum maps 'FSDP, ZeRO, tensor parallelism -> Ch 9' (CURRICULUM.md:44). Chapter 10 covers FSDP only as a framework/tooling detail. The concept reference should be Chapter 9.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch15-peft/index.mdx:23 'FSDP from Chapter 10 helps by sharding parameters, gradients, and optimizer state across GPUs, but it does not reduce the *total* memory budget.'
  - _fix:_ Change to 'FSDP from Chapter 9' (or 'Chapters 9-10').
- **[major/exercise-math-error/CONFIRMED]** The stated observation is backwards. For fixed rank/targets, trainable params scale as d_model*n_layers while base scales ~d_model^2*n_layers, so the trainable/base RATIO decreases with model size: 7B 0.24% -> 13B 0.20% -> 70B 0.12% (using the exercise's own specs). Trainable scales SLOWER than total, not faster; the reader is directed to draw the wrong conclusion, which also contradicts observation #1 and the chapter's 'tiny fraction' thesis.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch15-peft/index.mdx:471 '# 4. Larger models have larger d_model AND more layers, so trainable scales faster than total'
  - _fix:_ Rewrite as: 'the trainable/base ratio DROPS for larger models because base grows quadratically in d_model while the adapter grows only linearly.'
- **[minor/coverage/CONFIRMED]** VeRA is a named Key Concept and one of the four variants the reader is specced to recognize, but it is never mentioned. The chapter covers QLoRA, DoRA, IA3, AdaLoRA, MoLoRA/X-LoRA but omits VeRA (Kopiczko 2023, in the research scope).
  - _at:_ CURRICULUM.md:809 ('VeRA (shared random matrices, only scales trained)') and :805 ('recognize variants (QLoRA, DoRA, VeRA, IA3)'); no 'VeRA' string anywhere in ch15-peft/index.mdx
  - _fix:_ Add one sentence in the Modern variants section: VeRA freezes shared random A/B and trains only small per-layer scaling vectors, cutting trainable params well below LoRA.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The ParameterBudgetCalculator computes 70B full FT = 848 GB and displays ceil(848/80)=11x A100 (memory-budget-data.ts + caption Math.ceil at :188); the chapter body (:23) says 'at least 10'. The static caption's '8x A100s' understates and contradicts the live widget and the body text.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch15-peft/index.mdx:265 WidgetFrame caption 'full FT of 70B needs 8x A100s; QLoRA of 70B fits on a single A6000.'
  - _fix:_ Change '8x A100s' to '~11x A100s' (or 'at least 10x') to match the widget and body.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** The original LoRA paper states only 'random Gaussian initialization for A and zero for B' without specifying a 1/r^2 variance; the released reference implementation uses Kaiming-uniform for A. Attributing the specific N(0,1/r^2) to the paper is inaccurate.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch15-peft/index.mdx:81 '$A$ is initialized with Gaussian noise, for example $\mathcal{N}(0, 1/r^2)$, the choice made by the original paper.'
  - _fix:_ Drop the specific variance attribution: 'A is initialized with small random Gaussian noise (the reference implementation uses Kaiming-uniform); B is initialized to zero.'
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** L*d for a soft prompt (d=4096, typical L=20-100) is tens to hundreds of thousands of parameters, not 'a few thousand'. The comparison to prefix tuning is right but the absolute figure is off by 1-2 orders of magnitude.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch15-peft/index.mdx:231 'The parameter count drops to just $L \cdot d$, a few thousand parameters total, regardless of model depth.'
  - _fix:_ Change to 'tens to hundreds of thousands of parameters (L*d), regardless of model depth.'
- **[minor/stale-cross-ref/PLAUSIBLE]** The residual-stream concept/term is not developed in Chapter 8 (Building a small LLM) - the term does not appear in the shipped ch05 or ch08 pages; the curriculum places 'residual stream' in Chapter 5's glossary, and the term is actually used in ch06 and ch25. The Chapter 8 attribution is unsupported.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch15-peft/index.mdx:27 'the residual-stream representations explored in Chapter 8.'
  - _fix:_ Point to Chapter 5 (transformer block / residual stream) or drop the specific chapter cite.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- No PyTorch LoRA implementation: the specced outcome 'Implement LoRA in PyTorch by wrapping linear layers' and runnable 'lora_pytorch.py' (freeze base, train only adapters) are absent; all four runnables are numpy-only.
- VeRA is never mentioned despite being an explicit Key Concept and one of the four variants (QLoRA/DoRA/VeRA/IA3) the reader is specced to recognize.
- Biderman 2024 'LoRA Learns Less and Forgets Less' (in the research scope and cited by the curriculum's LoRA-is-lossless misconception) is not cited; the chapter states LoRA can underperform full FT but never surfaces the specific paper or its math/code finding.
- The curriculum-specced marquee widget LoRARankVisualizer (show a weight-update matrix and watch the reconstructed DeltaW ~= BA vary with rank) was replaced by an architecture schematic (LoRAArchitecture). Param-count/savings goal is met, but the DeltaW-reconstruction visualization the spec calls for is not delivered.

**Exercises:** Four exercises, meaningful and mostly well-scaffolded, but two have real defects. Ex1 (LoRA forward + zero-init) is correct and clean. Ex4 (QLoRA 70B budget) is correct and the hint arithmetic checks out (35 GB NF4 base, ~84M adapters, ~0.67 GB optimizer). Ex2 (param counts) is fine as a coding task but its guided observation #4 states the trainable/base ratio grows with model size, which is backwards. Ex3 (merge equivalence) is the worst: the code convention is transposed (adapter = x @ A.T @ B.T) so the reference/hint merge B@A does not match and the exercise's claimed equivalence fails numerically (0.032 vs the expected <1e-5); the correct merge is A.T@B.T. So 2 of 4 exercises would mislead a careful reader.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx, are exported, and compute correctly. LoRAArchitecture's figures match the prose (6.74B frozen, ~17M trainable at r=16 Q/K/V/O, ~402x smaller adapter). ParameterBudgetCalculator's memory model is sound (70B full = 848 GB, QLoRA-70B ~44 GB fits A6000, LoRA/QLoRA no double-counting of frozen base). Two caveats: (1) the ParameterBudgetCalculator shows 70B full FT needing ~11x A100 while the WidgetFrame caption prose says '8x A100s' - a prose/widget mismatch; (2) the delivered LoRAArchitecture is an architecture schematic, not the curriculum-specced DeltaW ~= BA low-rank reconstruction visualizer, so the 'watch the reconstruction vary with rank' teaching goal is only partially met (rank slider + param savings yes, matrix reconstruction no).


### Chapter 16 — grade B

A well-written, technically sound chapter with correct cross-references (a nice change from the numbering drift seen elsewhere), clean widgets, and four genuine exercises. Its main weaknesses are a wrong hardcoded number in the primary teaching runnable that contradicts the cell's own output, and a set of curriculum-specced key concepts that were dropped entirely (on-policy vs off-policy distillation, MiniLLM/reverse KL, self-distillation) plus a missing specced runnable. The core Hinton math, T^2 explanation, and hard-vs-soft framing are accurate and clearly taught.

**Strengths:** The T^2 gradient-compensation argument is explained correctly and reinforced with a runnable that shows soft loss staying comparable to hard loss across temperatures (I verified: hard=2.693 constant, soft 1.88->2.64).; Cross-references are all correct: Ch 13 (SFT), Ch 14 (RLVR), Ch 15 (PEFT), Ch 17/18/19 (inference/quantization/sampling), and the Phase 11/12 framing is consistent with ch15 and ch17.; The hard-vs-soft distillation framing ('soft distillation is the theory; hard distillation is the practice') is honest, well-motivated, and matches how production recipes actually work.; Both specced widgets exist and are functional; the DistillationPipeline stage data is detailed and accurate (R1 671B, 800K prompts, correct teacher attributions).; Exercises are meaningful and correctly scaffolded (temperature softmax, KL properties, full loss with a T^2 ablation, hard-distillation pipeline sim) rather than filler.

**Findings:**

- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The runnable's hardcoded observation claims the top class gets ~0.83 at T=1, but with the cell's own logits ([2.5, 0.2, 0.8, ...]) the actual top-1 probability at T=1 is 0.554. The printed top-3 table shows class 0 at 0.554, so the observation directly contradicts the output the reader sees. The '0.83' appears to have been written for the Exercise-1 logits (top logit 4.0) and pasted into a cell whose top logit is 2.5. It also weakens the 'dark knowledge crushed at T=1' point, since 0.55 already leaves visible mass on other classes.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch16-distillation/index.mdx:93, `print("- T=1:  top class dominates (~0.83); dark knowledge hidden")`
  - _fix:_ Either change the observation to the true value (top ~0.55 at T=1) or, better, raise the top logit (e.g. to 4.0+) so the T=1 distribution is genuinely peaked (~0.83) and the 'crushed' narrative holds. Same error is inherited from research.md:481.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The chapter presents forward KL(teacher||student) as THE distillation objective and never mentions reverse KL or MiniLLM (Gu 2024), which the CURRICULUM lists explicitly as a key concept and as a required research paper (arxiv 2306.08543). Reverse KL's mode-seeking behavior vs forward KL's mode-covering behavior is a substantive, well-known distinction for generative LLM distillation that a reader would expect after Exercise 2 raises the 'match teacher's shape even on rare classes' point.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch16-distillation/index.mdx (whole chapter); e.g. line 447 Exercise-2 note 'In distillation, we use KL(teacher || student)'
  - _fix:_ Add a short subsection contrasting forward vs reverse KL (mode-covering vs mode-seeking) and cite MiniLLM as the canonical reverse-KL LLM distillation recipe.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** On-policy vs off-policy distillation is entirely absent. The spec lists it as a key concept, as a glossary term ('on-policy distillation'), AND as a common misconception ('confusing on-policy and off-policy: on-policy student generates, teacher scores; off-policy teacher generates, student imitates'). The chapter only ever describes off-policy (teacher-generates) distillation without naming the distinction.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch16-distillation/index.mdx (absent); CURRICULUM.md:856,871,872
  - _fix:_ Add a paragraph distinguishing on-policy distillation (student generates, teacher scores its own trajectories) from the off-policy teacher-generates recipe the chapter already covers.
- **[minor/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** The R1-Distill size enumeration is presented as complete but omits DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-70B, which was released alongside the others. A reader concludes 32B is the largest distilled student.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch16-distillation/index.mdx:326, 'distilled into students of size $1.5$B, $7$B, $8$B, $14$B, and $32$B'
  - _fix:_ Add 70B to the list (1.5B, 7B, 8B, 14B, 32B, 70B), or write 'from 1.5B to 70B'.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** With the widget's own logits (France=4.0, others as in temperature-data.ts), the softmax at T=1 gives France = 0.804, not 0.87. I computed it directly.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch16-distillation/index.mdx:63, caption 'At T=1: France dominates (0.87)'
  - _fix:_ Change 0.87 to 0.80 in the WidgetFrame caption.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The prose claims Gemma 2 small variants match Llama 2 7B/13B while the pipeline widget claims Gemma 2 9B matches Llama 2 70B (8x smaller). Both cannot be the intended comparison; the two surfaces of the same chapter disagree on the comparison baseline.
  - _at:_ prose src/pages/ch16-distillation/index.mdx:320 'match or exceed Llama 2 $7$B/$13$B' vs widget src/components/widgets/ch16/pipeline-stages-data.ts:84 'Gemma 2 9B: matches Llama 2 70B'
  - _fix:_ Pick one accurate comparison (Gemma 2 9B is genuinely competitive with Llama 2 70B on several benchmarks) and make prose and widget consistent.
- **[minor/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 1's verification comment says the top prob at T=1 should be ~0.9+, but with the given logits ([4.0, 1.2, 0.8, ...]) the actual value is 0.833. A reader who fills in the TODO and prints probs_t1.max() sees 0.833, mildly contradicting the '0.9+' hint.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch16-distillation/index.mdx:382, '# 1. At T=1, the top class should dominate (~0.9+)'
  - _fix:_ Change '~0.9+' to '~0.83' (or soften to 'clearly dominant, >0.8').
- **[minor/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The spec requires a second runnable, attention_distillation.py ('show how to align teacher and student attention maps with an auxiliary MSE loss'). Feature/attention distillation is covered in prose only; there is no code for it. The chapter's three body runnables are all output-level.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch16-distillation/index.mdx:288-304 (prose-only); CURRICULUM.md:865
  - _fix:_ Add a small numpy runnable that projects a student attention map and computes an MSE alignment loss against a teacher map, per the spec.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** The 'last year' / 'most recent' framing is anchored to R1 (Jan 2025) and will read as stale to a mid-2026 reader; newer distilled reasoning models plausibly exist by then. The deployment math also uses A100/A6000 as the reference GPUs, which are a generation or two behind mid-2026 serving hardware (H100/H200/B-series).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch16-distillation/index.mdx:333 'one of the most important post-training developments of the last year'; line 326 'the most striking recent example'
  - _fix:_ Soften time-relative phrasing ('a landmark 2025 demonstration' rather than 'of the last year') and optionally note newer-generation GPUs; the illustrative math is fine as-is.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- on-policy vs off-policy distillation: specced as a key concept, a glossary term, and a common misconception in CURRICULUM.md, but entirely absent from the chapter.
- MiniLLM / reverse KL: specced key concept and required research paper (Gu 2024, arxiv 2306.08543); chapter never mentions reverse KL or the mode-seeking/mode-covering distinction and presents forward KL as the sole objective.
- self-distillation: listed in the spec's key concepts; not mentioned anywhere in the chapter.
- MiniLM: spec's 'connect to modern small models (DistilBERT, MiniLM, Phi, R1-distilled)' names MiniLM; the chapter covers DistilBERT/TinyBERT/MobileBERT/Phi/Gemma/Orca/R1 but never MiniLM.
- attention_distillation.py runnable (specced in 'Runnable code') is missing; feature/attention distillation is prose-only.
- 'When distillation does NOT work' is thin: the capacity floor (~1B students struggle to inherit reasoning), the teacher-student ratio limit ('bigger teacher isn't always better', research MC2), and the 'student capacity bounds what can transfer' misconception are all in research.md but not surfaced in the chapter; the R1 callout only briefly gestures at limits.

**Exercises:** Four meaningful, non-filler exercises that map to the spec's target outcomes (compute the loss, hard-vs-soft, analyze a recipe): (1) temperature softmax, (2) KL divergence + properties, (3) full Hinton loss with a T^2-ablation flag (nicely reinforces the chapter's central 'don't forget T^2' point), (4) hard-distillation pipeline simulation. Scaffolds are correct and would run once the TODOs are filled; the Exercise-4 mock (np.random.beta with 160 prompts) is coherent. Only nit: Exercise 1's '~0.9+' verification comment overstates the actual ~0.83 top-1 probability for the given logits.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist and work. TemperatureScaling (the specced TemperatureScalingVisualizer) computes a correct stable softmax-with-temperature and drives a clean dark-knowledge readout that matches the prose; however the MDX WidgetFrame caption states France=0.87 at T=1 whereas the widget's own logits yield 0.80. DistillationPipeline is accurate, richly detailed, and faithful to the hard-distillation narrative, with one prose/widget inconsistency (Gemma 2 vs Llama 2 70B vs 7B/13B). No placeholder or broken widgets. Note the spec's feature/attention distillation is not represented by any widget or runnable, only prose.


### Chapter 17 — grade C

A well-structured, clearly-voiced chapter with clean cross-references and a genuinely strong speculative-decoding section that verifies numerically. But it has two serious content defects for a technical textbook: (1) the flagship "KV cache speedup" runnable code crashes with matmul shape errors in BOTH the naive and cached paths, so the chapter's central demonstration does not run; and (2) the entire KV-cache memory subsection — the quantitative heart of the chapter — is wrong by 8-14x (7B at 4K stated as 270 MB is really 2 GB; 70B at 128K stated as 22 GB is really ~320 GB with the stated heads, ~40 GB with true GQA). Several secondary numbers (Flash-Attention intermediate size, 70B-on-one-A100, exercise-1 comments) compound the same carelessness with reference numbers.

**Strengths:** Prefill vs decode is given its own section and handled carefully — the compute-bound/memory-bound distinction (the curriculum's #1 tricky spot) is explained well and used as an organizing lens throughout.; Cross-reference integrity is clean: every Ch 16/18/19 and Phase 11/12 reference points to the correct target per the canonical map; no stale numbering.; The speculative-decoding section is technically sound — the 1-to-(k+1) tokens-per-round accounting, the 'exactly equivalent' correctness claim, and the speedup formula all check out, and the speculative RunnableCode executes and matches the widget.; The ~700x KV-cache attention-FLOP reduction is derived correctly (sum i^2 ~ 3.6e8 vs sum i ~ 5.2e5 = ~683x).; Voice is disciplined: no forbidden phrases, headings name the thing, misconception callouts are used effectively.

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The chapter's flagship RunnableCode (the KV-cache speedup demo, 'The runnable cell below simulates the speedup directly') crashes. In naive_decode_one_step, (x @ w.T) @ x is (seq,256)@(seq,256) — inner dims 256 vs seq(101) mismatch -> ValueError. In cached_decode_one_step, np.stack of a list of (1,256) arrays gives keys of shape (n,1,256), so x @ keys.T also raises a matmul dimension error. I ran both paths: both raise 'matmul: Input operand ... mismatch in its core dimension'. The code prints no speedup; it errors immediately.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:106 and :115 — 'x = np.tanh(x @ w + (x @ w.T) @ x / x.shape[0])' and 'x = np.tanh(x @ w + (x @ keys.T) @ keys / len(kv_cache[layer_idx]))'
  - _fix:_ Fix the attention-like term so shapes are consistent: e.g. naive uses (x @ x.T) @ x (a (seq,seq)@(seq,d) contraction) and cached stacks keys to (n,256) (np.concatenate or squeeze) so (x @ keys.T) is (1,n) and @ keys is (1,d). Also the divide-by-zero/overflow RuntimeWarnings should be addressed. Re-run to confirm the printed speedup.
- **[blocker/math-error/CONFIRMED]** Wrong by ~8x. With the chapter's own formula 2*L*H*d_head*N*batch*2 bytes: 2*32*32*128*4096*2 = 2.0 GiB (2048 MB), not 270 MB. 270 MB is the N=512 value (2*32*32*128*512*2 = 256 MB). This is the central quantitative claim of the 'memory cost' subsection.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:82 — 'plugging in $L = 32$, $H = 32$, $d_{\text{head}} = 128$, $N = 4096$ gives roughly $270$ MB per sequence'
  - _fix:_ Change '270 MB' to '~2 GB' for Llama-7B at 4K (or re-label as a 512-token example).
- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** Both the number and the label are wrong. In BF16 the formula with H=64 gives 2*80*64*128*131072*2 = 320 GB, not 22 GB. '(post-GQA)' is mislabeled: Llama-70B's GQA uses 8 KV heads (64 is the query-head count); using the correct 8 KV heads gives ~40 GB, still not 22 GB. The stated 22 GB is roughly the H=64, N=8K value (20 GB).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:83 — 'with $L = 80$, $H = 64$ (post-GQA), $d_{\text{head}} = 128$, $N = 131072$, the number jumps to roughly $22$ GB *per sequence*'
  - _fix:_ Use KV heads for the cache formula (H_kv=8 for Llama-70B) and state ~40 GB in BF16 at 128K; drop or correct the '(post-GQA)' annotation on 64.
- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 1's answer comments are labeled '128K context' but give ~8K-context values. With the exercise's own MODELS (heads 32/40/64) and formula, 128K yields 64 GB / 100 GB / 320 GB; the 4/7/22 GB figures are the 8K values (16x smaller). A learner checking their implementation against these comments will conclude a correct implementation is 16x too large.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:351-354 — '# At 128K context:  # - Llama-7B:  ~4 GB  # - Llama-13B: ~7 GB  # - Llama-70B: ~22 GB'
  - _fix:_ Either relabel the comment block 'At 8K context' or replace the numbers with the true 128K values (~64/100/320 GB).
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** A 70B model in BF16 needs ~140 GB of weights and does not fit on a single 80 GB A100, so 'a 70B model on a single A100' is physically impossible at the implied precision. The throughput figures are also internally inconsistent: earlier the chapter gives a 7B naive baseline of '5 to 10 tokens per second' (line 21), yet here a 10x-heavier 70B naive stack is 50 tok/s — faster, not slower — and both models are claimed to reach the same optimized 200-500 tok/s.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:614 — 'A $70$B model on a single A100 with a naive stack delivers around $50$ tokens per second; the same hardware running a fully optimized stack delivers $200$ to $500$'
  - _fix:_ Either specify multiple GPUs / a quantized 70B, or switch the example back to the 7B used earlier; reconcile the naive/optimized tok/s so the 70B is slower than the 7B.
- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** 268 MB is the FP32 size. 67,108,864 entries (8192^2) in BF16 (2 bytes) = 134 MB (128 MiB); 268 MB (256 MiB) is 4 bytes/entry = FP32. The '67M entries' is correct but 'in BF16' contradicts the byte count.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:176 — 'that intermediate has $67$M entries, occupying $268$ MB in BF16'
  - _fix:_ Change to '~134 MB in BF16' (or '268 MB in FP32').
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** 2.9 is the expected number of tokens emitted per round, not the speedup. The chapter's own formula and the widget's ExpectedReadout give speedup = emitted/cost = 2.94/1.15 = 2.56x at k=5, alpha=0.7 (the prose at line 216 correctly says 'roughly 2.5x'). The WidgetFrame caption mislabels emitted-tokens as speedup.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:260 — 'At k=5, α=0.7: ~2.9× speedup.'
  - _fix:_ Change to '~2.6x speedup (2.9 tokens per big-model pass)'.
- **[minor/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum flags speculative-decoding correctness as a tricky spot with an explicit instruction to 'Walk through this.' The chapter only asserts exactness; it never explains the rejection-sampling mechanism (accept draft token with prob min(1, p_target/p_draft), else resample from the normalized residual max(0, p-q)) that guarantees the distribution is unchanged. The widget likewise models acceptance as an iid Bernoulli(alpha), which hides the actual correctness argument.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:206 — 'it is mathematically equivalent to the original decoder, just faster'
  - _fix:_ Add a short derivation or worked example of the accept/resample rule showing the output distribution equals the target's.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** Misleading order of magnitude. 10^9 is essentially the toy attention-term count (sum i^2 ~ 3.6e8) later used at line 61, but here it is attributed to redundant work 'on a 7B model.'
