The real redundant compute for naively regenerating 1024 tokens on a 7B model (re-projecting K/V and re-running attention over the growing prefix) is on the order of 10^14-10^15 FLOPs, dominated by the weight matmuls the '10^9' ignores.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:21 — 'that is roughly $10^9$ FLOP-equivalents of *redundant* work'
  - _fix:_ Drop the '7B model' framing for the 10^9 figure, or quote the true redundant-FLOP magnitude.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** FlashAttention-3 (Shah et al. 2024, arxiv 2407.08608) is never named, despite the curriculum key concept 'FlashAttention I/II/III' and the pre-research listing FA3 explicitly. FA3's Hopper/FP8 speedups are the current state of the art a mid-2026 reader would expect mentioned alongside FA1/FA2.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:182 — 'successors (Ring Attention, sliding-window variants)'
  - _fix:_ Add a sentence on FlashAttention-3 (async warp specialization / FP8 on Hopper) as the third generation.
- **[minor/pedagogy/CONFIRMED]** Garbled comparison. As written, 'the highest utilization any pre-2022 approach could match' says continuous batching merely equals the best pre-2022 method, which contradicts the paragraph's thesis that it broke the old dichotomy.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch17-inference-optimization/index.mdx:160 — 'The result is the highest sustained GPU utilization any pre-2022 approach could match while keeping per-request latency low'
  - _fix:_ Reword to 'higher sustained GPU utilization than any pre-2022 approach could achieve...'
- **[minor/widget/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum specced KVCacheAnimation to 'Toggle between "no cache" (recomputes everything each step) and "with cache" ... Compare FLOPs.' The delivered widget only animates the cache filling during prefill/decode and shows no recompute-vs-cache contrast and no FLOP comparison, so the redundancy the chapter is about is never made visible in the widget.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch17/KVCacheAnimation.tsx:101 — cachePanel only shows the 'with cache' fill; no toggle
  - _fix:_ Add a no-cache/with-cache toggle and a running FLOP counter contrasting the two, per the spec.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Speculative-decoding rejection-sampling correctness is only asserted ('mathematically equivalent to the original decoder'), not walked through. The curriculum explicitly lists this as a tricky spot and says 'Walk through this' — the residual-distribution / accept-with-prob-min(1,p/q) mechanism that makes the output distribution exact is never shown.
- FlashAttention-3 (Shah 2024, arxiv 2407.08608) is absent. The curriculum key concept is 'FlashAttention I/II/III'; the chapter covers I and II and gestures at 'successors (Ring Attention, sliding-window)' but never names or dates FA3.
- The online-softmax math (LogSumExp identity applied tile-by-tile), flagged as a curriculum tricky spot, is described only verbally ('running max and running sum'); the rescaling identity that makes tiling correct is not shown even schematically.
- The curriculum 'Builds on' connections to Ch 4 (the attention being optimized), Ch 5 (transformer block), and Ch 11 (MoE inference has special considerations) are not made — the chapter has essentially no backward cross-reference to the attention it is optimizing, and MoE-inference considerations are never mentioned.

**Exercises:** The four exercises are meaningfully scaffolded (KV memory calc, naive-vs-cached timing, speculative speedup, continuous-batching simulator) and cover the right concepts. Problems: (a) Exercise 1's answer comments are numerically wrong — labeled "At 128K context" but the values (7B ~4 GB, 13B ~7 GB, 70B ~22 GB) are actually the ~8K-context values; with the exercise's own MODELS specs and formula, 128K yields 64/100/320 GB. (b) Exercise 2 is nearly a verbatim duplicate of the (broken) worked KV-cache demo — same d_model=256, n_layers=4, 100+50 tokens — so it reads as redundant filler. (c) Exercise 4 defines ARRIVAL_RATE=0.4 but the skeleton pre-generates all requests and never uses it (dead variable). Exercise 3 is clean and its hint formula is correct.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx and render. SpeculativeDecoding is faithful — its expectedSpeedup() matches the prose formula and the runnable code (k=5, alpha=0.7 -> emitted 2.94, speedup 2.56x, verified by execution). KVCacheAnimation under-delivers versus the curriculum spec: it only animates cache filling and omits the specced no-cache/with-cache toggle and FLOP comparison. One caption mismatch: the WidgetFrame says "~2.9x speedup" where 2.9 is emitted tokens, not the 2.56x speedup the widget itself displays.


### Chapter 18 — grade B-

A technically strong, well-voiced chapter whose prose, math, and the two React widgets are largely correct and pedagogically sharp. But its signature runnable — the NF4 level construction — is broken: it prints levels that contradict the NF4 values stated in the prose just above it and the (correct) widget constant, and it undersells the very "denser near zero" effect it exists to demonstrate. On top of that, two specced concepts (GGUF format; KV-cache quantization) are asserted or promised but never actually taught, the KV-cache row even appearing in the chapter's own summary table with a quantitative claim and no supporting section.

**Strengths:** Basic float-to-int math is correct and cleanly derived: scale = grid spacing, error bound s/2, symmetric/asymmetric formulas all check out (line 47-49); Two working, well-designed widgets (QuantizationExplorer, GranularityVisualizer) that use the correct canonical NF4 levels and compute real per-row MSE; they match the prose narrative; The two non-NF4 runnables (symmetric quantization, per-tensor/channel/group) execute correctly and produce output that supports the surrounding claims; Cross-references to Ch 15 (QLoRA), Ch 17 (inference), Ch 19 (sampling) and the Ch 20-30 roadmap are almost all correct per the canonical map; Voice is clean: no forbidden phrases, no first-person-singular, opinionated-but-honest tone consistent with the project contract; LLM.int8 outlier-feature story, SmoothQuant migration algebra, and GPTQ/AWQ contrast are all accurate and at the right depth

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The runnable compute_nf4_levels() is wrong. The line `levels_half.append(norm.ppf(0.5 + p / 2))` compresses the positive half into CDF range [0.516, 0.734] instead of the equiprobable-bin midpoints. Running it produces levels [-1.000, -0.852, -0.711, -0.575, -0.444, -0.315, -0.188, -0.063, +0.063, ...], which (a) do not match the NF4 levels the prose lists three lines earlier, (b) do not match the correct widget constant NF4_LEVELS, (c) contain no exact 0.0, and (d) are nearly uniform: the code's own printed 'Ratio (tail/center)' comes out 1.18x, undercutting the 'Resolution concentrates near zero' claim (true NF4 ratio is ~3.8x). A reader who runs the marquee code of the chapter's signature format gets numbers that flatly contradict the book.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:242, function lines 236-247; contradicts prose levels at line 221: "-1.000, -0.696, -0.526, -0.395, -0.285, -0.184, -0.091,  0.000"
  - _fix:_ Use the correct construction: positive-half midpoints are norm.ppf(0.5 + (i+0.5)/16) for i in 0..7 (i.e. drop the erroneous /2), or simply hardcode the canonical QLoRA levels already present in quantization-data.ts. Verify the printed levels match line 221 and that the tail/center spacing ratio prints ~3-4x. The same broken approach is echoed in the Exercise 4 hint (line 522, 'Place 8 levels in the positive half at quantile midpoints').
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** KV-cache quantization is asserted as a Ch 18 contribution in the chapter's own optimization-stack table (with a quantitative 2-4x claim) and listed again in the closing paragraph (line 622), but it is never explained anywhere in the chapter body. The research file devoted a full Concept 8 to it and the curriculum lists it under Connections ('quantization interacts with KV cache — there's also KV cache quantization'). The chapter promises a technique in its summary and never delivers it.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:612, summary table row: "**KV cache quantization** | **Ch 18** | **$2$–$4\times$ cache memory**"
  - _fix:_ Add a short subsection (or fold into 'The full picture') explaining KV-cache quantization: per-token INT8/INT4 scaling of K/V, why the cache tolerates it (averaging over the attention sum), and that vLLM/TGI support it — matching research Concept 8/TID7.
- **[major/coverage-gap/CONFIRMED]** GGUF is a specced Key Concept ('GGUF format (llama.cpp)') and had a dedicated research subsection ('GGUF format details: the file format used by llama.cpp; includes quantization metadata so the model can be loaded directly'). The chapter mentions llama.cpp but never mentions GGUF or its k-quant formats at all. The 'Reader will be able to recognize the role of quantization in deployment stacks (llama.cpp, vLLM, TGI)' objective is only partially met.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:296, only stack mention: "widely supported in vLLM, TensorRT-LLM, TGI, and llama.cpp"
  - _fix:_ Add a sentence or two on GGUF: it is llama.cpp's container format carrying quantization metadata (scales, group sizes, the k-quant variants Q4_K_M etc.) so a quantized model loads directly on CPU/edge.
- **[minor/coverage-gap/CONFIRMED]** Hadamard-rotation methods QuIP/QuIP# and SpinQuant are a specced Key Concept ('Hadamard rotation tricks (QuIP, SpinQuant)') with two dedicated research papers (Tseng 2024 arXiv:2402.04396; Ashkboos 2024 SpinQuant arXiv:2405.16406). They appear nowhere in the chapter prose — only a passing 'QuIP#' inside a widget's insight string. The sub-4-bit frontier is named ('2-bit, 3-bit, 1.58-bit', line 29) but the named techniques for it are omitted.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch18/quantization-data.ts:163 (only mention anywhere): "works only with sophisticated methods (AQLM, QuIP#)"
  - _fix:_ Add a one-paragraph note on rotation-based sub-4-bit PTQ (QuIP#, SpinQuant): a Hadamard/random rotation makes weight and activation distributions more Gaussian and suppresses outliers, enabling 2-3 bit quantization. Keep it brief as the research suggested.
- **[minor/coverage-gap/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum Runnable-code spec calls for `int8_quantization.py` implementing 'symmetric AND asymmetric INT8 quantization from scratch in numpy; verify roundtrip' and the 'Reader will be able to: Implement INT8 quantization with symmetric and asymmetric scaling'. Every runnable and all four exercises implement symmetric only; asymmetric quantization (with zero-point) is described in prose but never coded or exercised.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:63 (runnable is symmetric-only); prose at line 49 defines asymmetric but no code
  - _fix:_ Add asymmetric quantize/dequantize (with zero-point) to the first runnable or as an exercise, showing the roundtrip on a skewed (e.g. post-ReLU) input, per the spec.
- **[minor/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The hardcoded observation claims INT4 MSE ~1e-5, but running the very code block above it (seed 0, N(0,0.1), 256 weights) prints MSE = 9.70e-05, i.e. ~1e-4 — an order of magnitude larger than the stated ~1e-5. The comment misdescribes its own output.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:93, printed observation: "INT4: visible quantization error (MSE ~1e-5) but still small"
  - _fix:_ Change '(MSE ~1e-5)' to '(MSE ~1e-4)' to match the actual printed value.
- **[minor/math-error/CONFIRMED]** The chapter gives two different per-group scale-overhead numbers for the same G=128 INT4 configuration: 0.13 bpw in 'Storage overhead' (line 57, implicitly FP16 scales, 16/128=0.125) and 0.25 bpw in 'Why per-group matters' (line 118, implicitly FP32 scales, 32/128=0.25). The scale dtype is never pinned, so the two sections silently contradict each other; line 229 then adds a third assumption (FP32 at G=64 = 0.5 bpw).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:118 vs 57: line 118 "at $G = 128$ they add about $0.25$" vs line 57 "$G = 128$ the scale overhead lands at roughly $0.13$ bits per weight"
  - _fix:_ State the scale dtype explicitly and use one consistent convention (e.g. FP16 scales throughout: G=64 -> 0.25 bpw, G=128 -> 0.125 bpw), or annotate each figure with its dtype.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** The asymmetric zero-point formula z = -round(x_min/s) maps x_min to integer 0, which is the unsigned-range convention, but the chapter's boxed mapping and clip bounds use the SIGNED range [q_min, q_max] = [-128, 127]. With signed bounds and this z, x_min lands at 0 rather than at q_min = -128, wasting the entire lower half of the grid. The two conventions are never reconciled.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:49, "asymmetric ($z = -\text{round}(x_{\min}/s)$ ...)"
  - _fix:_ Either present asymmetric with the unsigned range [0, 255] (as the research file does), or use z = q_min - round(x_min/s) so x_min maps to q_min under the signed range shown in the boxed equation.
- **[minor/stale-cross-ref/CONFIRMED]** Ch 24-26 is labeled 'safety and evaluation', but per the canonical map Ch 25 is Interpretability (SAEs, mech interp), not safety or evaluation. The two-word gloss silently drops interpretability.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:620, "safety and evaluation (Ch 24–26)"
  - _fix:_ Reword to 'safety, interpretability, and evaluation (Ch 24-26)'.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** All memory examples are framed exclusively around A100 80GB and H100 80GB. By mid-2026 higher-capacity parts (H200 141GB, B200 192GB) are shipping, which changes the 'largest single GPU' framing that underpins the chapter's 70B-on-one-GPU narrative. The claims are not wrong, but the hardware ceiling is dated.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:21, "which fits one H100 with room for cache and batching" / repeated A100 80GB / H100 80GB framing
  - _fix:_ Optionally note that newer 141-192GB parts raise the ceiling further; the pedagogical point (bit width moves the deployability threshold) is unchanged.
- **[minor/voice/CONFIRMED]** Seven section-ending 'The next section...' / 'The final section...' segue sentences. These border on the contract's forbidden pattern ('Any sentence whose only function is to announce the next sentence'). They do carry a little framing so they are not clear violations, but the repetition is a mild tic.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch18-quantization/index.mdx:35,108,184,204,274,302,320 — e.g. "The next section introduces the basic mapping..."
  - _fix:_ Trim or drop a few; let the section headings do the work, per the voice guide.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- GGUF format — specced Key Concept with a dedicated research subsection — completely absent from the chapter (llama.cpp is named but the format, metadata, and k-quant variants are not)
- KV-cache quantization — specced Connection and research Concept 8 — asserted in the summary table (2-4x) and closing list but never taught in any section
- QuIP / QuIP# / SpinQuant / Hadamard-rotation tricks — specced Key Concept with two research papers (Tseng 2024, Ashkboos 2024) — appear only inside a widget insight string, never in prose
- Asymmetric INT8 quantization is specced both as a 'Reader will be able to' outcome and as runnable code ('symmetric and asymmetric'), but is prose-only; no runnable or exercise implements it
- PTQ-vs-QAT contrast (a research 'two broad approaches' framing) is reduced to a single passing clause; QAT is essentially not covered as a concept

**Exercises:** The four exercises are meaningful and well-scaffolded, forming a clean difficulty ramp (symmetric -> per-channel -> per-group -> NF4) with collapsed hints, TODO templates, and commented verification harnesses — not filler. They correctly hit curriculum outcomes 2 (basic mapping), 3 (granularity), and 5 (NF4). Two caveats: (1) Exercise 4's hint ('Place 8 levels in the positive half at quantile midpoints ... norm.ppf') steers the reader toward the same flawed construction as the broken body reference; a reader who mirrors it will not get canonical NF4 levels (though, tested, the near-uniform result still happens to beat INT4 on MSE, so the exercise's directional conclusion survives). (2) No exercise covers asymmetric quantization, which the spec explicitly wanted implemented.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as working .tsx and are the strongest part of the chapter. QuantizationExplorer (bit-width 16/8/4/3/2 with an INT4/NF4 toggle) uses the CORRECT canonical NF4 levels hardcoded in quantization-data.ts and computes real MSE/max-error and grid lines that match the prose. GranularityVisualizer builds an 8x64 matrix with a 10x outlier row plus within-row hot regions and computes genuine per-row and overall MSE across per-tensor/per-channel/per-group with selectable G — exactly what the prose describes. Notably the widgets are correct where the NF4 runnable in the prose is wrong, so the widget/prose story is coherent but the widget silently outshines the broken text code. Minor spec drift (QuantizationExplorer offers NF4 and 3/2-bit instead of the spec's 'INT4 group' scheme selector) is an improvement, not a defect.


### Chapter 19 — grade B

A clean, well-written, and mostly technically-correct chapter that closes Phase 12 well: the greedy/temperature/top-k/top-p/beam/constrained arc is clear, the two working widgets match their prose, and the four exercises are meaningful and correctly scaffolded. It is dragged down by a handful of substantive issues: one cross-reference that is both mis-numbered and asserts a trick that does not exist in the target chapter, a broken internal promise that leaves top-k with no runnable implementation, and several curriculum-mandated concepts (min-p implementation, contrastive search, typical sampling, mirostat) that are absent or mention-only. There is also a real notation inconsistency between the repetition-penalty section (additive alpha) and the recipes table (multiplicative 1.05/1.1).

**Strengths:** Prose is clear and operationally focused; the recipes table and order-of-operations pipeline are directly actionable.; Both specced widgets exist as working .tsx (SamplingDistribution marquee + ConstrainedDecoding secondary) and their computations match the prose captions (nucleus-size adaptivity, entropy, FSM masking with the star = model-preferred-but-invalid marker).; The runnable numpy for greedy/temperature, top-p nucleus-size sweep, and frequency/presence penalties is correct and would produce the claimed qualitative outputs.; Cross-references to Ch 17 (17 covers cache/batching/flash/speculative/paging; decode memory-bound = Ch 17 §2) and the Phase 12/13 closeout are accurate and consistent with the canonical numbering.; Temperature-as-distillation callback to Ch 16 is a genuine, correct reuse, not a coincidental one.

**Findings:**

- **[major/stale-cross-ref/CONFIRMED]** The parenthetical is wrong on two counts. (1) Section number: Ch 18 §6 is 'Modern PTQ: GPTQ and AWQ'; activation quantization is §7. (2) The claim itself is false: grep of ch18 for softmax/max-subtraction/overflow/numerical-stability returns nothing — Ch 18 never discusses the softmax max-subtraction trick. Quantization uses absmax *division* (scaling), which is not 'the same trick' as subtracting the max before exponentiating.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch19-sampling/index.mdx:21 — "with the standard max-subtraction trick (Ch 18 §6 talks through the same trick for activation quantization) for numerical stability"
  - _fix:_ Delete the parenthetical, or replace with an accurate pointer (e.g. drop the 'same trick' framing entirely, since Ch 18 does not cover softmax stability).
- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** Broken internal promise. The next section's runnable (lines 154-207) implements only top_p_sample and nucleus_size; it never implements top-k and never compares the two. Line 206 even prints 'Top-p adapts where top-k cannot' with no top-k code present. Net effect: top-k is described in prose but implemented in NO runnable or exercise, though the curriculum's first learning objective is 'Implement greedy, top-k, top-p (nucleus), min-p, and temperature sampling'.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch19-sampling/index.mdx:128 — "The runnable code that demonstrates top-k lives in the next section, alongside top-p, comparing the two on the same logits"
  - _fix:_ Add a top_k_sample implementation to the section-4 runnable (or a dedicated top-k runnable) that compares nucleus size / kept-set against top-p on the same logits, matching the promise on line 128.
- **[major/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The marquee widget is missing the min-p control the curriculum explicitly specs. min-p appears only as a one-line prose mention (index.mdx:152) and is implemented nowhere — not in the widget, not in any runnable, not in any exercise — despite being in both the widget spec and the 'Reader will be able to Implement ... min-p' objective (CURRICULUM.md:999).
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch19/SamplingDistribution.tsx:11-77 (sliders: T, topP, topK only); spec CURRICULUM.md:1010 "user adjusts temperature, top-k, top-p, min-p"
  - _fix:_ Add a min-p slider to SamplingDistribution (keep tokens with p_i >= p_min * max_j p_j) and/or add a min-p runnable so the specced 'implement min-p' outcome is actually deliverable.
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** Notation inconsistency between two penalty conventions the chapter conflates. Section 6 defines ONLY the additive frequency/presence penalty (z' = z - alpha*count, typical alpha 0.0-0.3). The recipes table's 'Rep penalty' column uses 1.05 / 1.1 — the multiplicative HF repetition_penalty (Keskar 2019, >=1.0) convention, which the chapter never introduces. A reader who learned alpha=0.1 for chat cannot reconcile the table's 'mild (1.05)' for the same use case.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch19-sampling/index.mdx:242 "| Chat / instruction | $0.7$ | $0.95$ | (off) | mild ($1.05$) |" vs :274 "A mild frequency penalty of $\alpha = 0.1$ is usually sufficient for chat."
  - _fix:_ Either express the table column in the additive alpha the chapter teaches (e.g. mild ~0.1), or add one sentence in Section 6 introducing the multiplicative repetition_penalty (1.05-1.3) and note the two conventions.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** Contrastive search is an explicit learning objective and a listed Key Concept (with a dedicated research reference), but the word 'contrastive' never appears in the chapter. The reader cannot 'recognize contrastive search' from a chapter that omits it.
  - _at:_ spec CURRICULUM.md:1002 "Recognize repetition penalties, frequency penalties, and contrastive search"; research.md cites Su 2022 (2202.06417)
  - _fix:_ Add a short paragraph (or a callout) covering contrastive search (degeneration penalty + model confidence trade-off, Su et al. 2022) alongside beam search or repetition penalties.
- **[minor/currency/CONFIRMED]** json_object only guarantees syntactically valid JSON, not adherence to a schema — yet the section is specifically about *guaranteeing structure* via FSM masking. OpenAI Structured Outputs (response_format json_schema, strict:true), which is the schema-constrained-decoding feature that actually matches the section's FSM-masking story, is not mentioned. This predates the Jan 2026 cutoff (launched Aug 2024).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch19-sampling/index.mdx:363 — "OpenAI JSON mode (`response_format = json_object`) constrains output to valid JSON."
  - _fix:_ Add OpenAI Structured Outputs (json_schema/strict) as the schema-enforcing example, distinguishing it from json_object which only enforces JSON syntax.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** o1 does not expose a temperature parameter in the API (it is fixed / ignored), so attributing a T=0.6-0.7 sampling recipe to o1 is inaccurate. DeepSeek does recommend ~0.6 for R1, so the R1 half is fine.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch19-sampling/index.mdx:248 — "Reasoning models like o1 and R1: $T = 0.6$ – $0.7$, top-p $= 0.95$"
  - _fix:_ Split the claim: keep T~0.6 for R1; note that o1-family reasoning models do not expose temperature.
- **[minor/currency/CONFIRMED]** Internal inconsistency. Line 152 states nucleus is 'the default' for GPT/Claude, but line 248 says the OpenAI/Anthropic default is top-p=1.0 (nucleus effectively off, user-set per request). 'Default' is being used loosely in one place and precisely in the other.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch19-sampling/index.mdx:152 "Nucleus sampling is the default for GPT, Claude, Llama, Gemini" vs :248 "OpenAI: $T = 1.0$, top-p $= 1.0$ (effectively off)"
  - _fix:_ Reword line 152 to 'nucleus sampling is the standard truncation method used by' rather than 'the default for', or qualify that the API default leaves top-p at 1.0 and providers recommend ~0.95.
- **[minor/currency/CONFIRMED]** Two illustrative throughput figures for the same 'production stack' claim disagree (500 vs 500-1000). Not load-bearing, but the intro and closeout should match.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch19-sampling/index.mdx:15 "a $500$-token-per-second production service" vs :683 "$500$ to $1{,}000$ tokens per second"
  - _fix:_ Use one consistent range in both the intro and the Phase-12 closeout.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- min-p: specced as implementable and as a widget slider (CURRICULUM.md:999, :1010) but only mentioned in one prose sentence (index.mdx:152) — no runnable, no exercise, no widget control.
- top-k: described in prose (Section 3) but implemented in no runnable or exercise despite being a specced 'implement' objective; the promised top-k runnable (line 128) never materializes.
- Contrastive search: a stated learning objective and Key Concept (Su 2022) — entirely absent.
- Typical sampling: listed in Key concepts (CURRICULUM.md:1006) — never mentioned.
- Mirostat: research scope locked it in as a brief mention and open-question Q4 recommended including it in a TID/misconception — absent from the chapter.
- Interaction with speculative decoding (research 'in scope and locked', Concept 9 / TID9, bridge to Ch 17) — not covered beyond the Ch 17 recap.
- Tricky spot 'constrained decoding doesn't make the model smart' (CURRICULUM.md:1030 / research MC7) is only lightly touched; the failure mode where a model that wasn't going to produce valid JSON is forced into nonsense-within-structure is not really surfaced.

**Exercises:** Four exercises, all meaningful and well-scaffolded with collapsed hints and runnable templates: (1) greedy+temperature, (2) top-p nucleus with adaptive-size verification, (3) frequency penalty, (4) FSM masking for a tiny JSON grammar. Scaffolding is correct — TODO stubs return pass and the driver code is commented out, so the shipped files run without error. The FSM/vocab in Ex4 is internally consistent (fake_logits boosts the invalid 'random' token to demonstrate masking). Two gaps: they cover curriculum outcomes 2/3/7 but not outcome 4 (combine temperature+top-p in one stack — Ex3 substitutes repetition penalty), and neither top-k nor min-p is ever exercised, so those 'implement' objectives are untested. Ex4's while-loop scaffold would infinite-loop if a student forgets the state update, but that is standard fill-in-the-blank risk, not a defect.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist and are functional, no placeholders. SamplingDistribution (marquee) correctly implements temperature -> top-p ∩ top-k truncation -> renormalize, and its stats (nucleus size, top probability, entropy in bits) and cyan-kept/rose-masked coloring match the WidgetFrame caption; the nucleus-adaptivity story it tells is accurate. Its one shortcoming is the missing min-p control the curriculum specs. Note the top ('Original') chart is intentionally temperature-independent (softmax at T=1), which is consistent with both captions. ConstrainedDecoding (secondary) faithfully matches its prose: per-step FSM state, valid (✓) vs masked (✗) tokens, and the ★ = model-preferred-token-without-constraints marker that is invalid in 7 of 9 non-terminal steps, exactly as the caption claims ('often invalid'). No prose/compute mismatches beyond the min-p omission.


### Chapter 20 — grade B-

A well-written, cleanly-structured chapter with flawless cross-reference integrity (every "Chapter N" and section pointer is correct) and runnable code that actually produces the claimed output. It is let down by two genuine technical errors in the modern-era material (a wrong claim about how PRM800K was labeled, and conflation of DeepSeek-R1 with R1-Zero on the "no SFT" point), and by two curriculum-specced core outcomes that are essentially absent: GRPO's group-relative advantage / "shift away from value models," and the R1 "aha moment." The marquee widget's insight text also overstates its own plotted curves. Solid but not airtight.

**Strengths:** All cross-references (Chapters 14, 16, 17, 18, 19, 21, 22, 23 and all section/Topic self-references) are correct — no numbering drift, which is notable for this book.; Every executed RunnableCode block was traced by hand and produces exactly the stated output (self-consistency 80% confidence, PRM best-of-N ordering, mock CoT).; The four exercises are meaningful, correctly map to curriculum outcomes 2/3/5/6, and Ex4's binomial majority-vote derivation is mathematically correct and non-trivial.; Strong pedagogy on the CoT-as-scratchpad mechanism and the honest 'trace is generation, not introspection' caveat, matching the research misconception list.; Both specced widgets exist, are exported, wired into the page, and broadly match the prose framing (6 techniques / difficulty toggle; 3 problems × 20 traces / N slider).

**Findings:**

- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** PRM800K's step-level labels were produced by human annotators, not GPT-4. GPT-4 (a fine-tuned generator) produced the candidate solutions; humans labeled each step positive/negative/neutral. The sentence also self-contradicts: it opens by saying the data is expensive 'because someone has to label intermediate reasoning steps,' then claims GPT-4 generated the annotations (which would make it cheap and automated).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:214 — "the canonical dataset (PRM800K, the dataset behind Lightman 2023) used GPT-4 to generate step annotations for math problems at scale"
  - _fix:_ Change to: PRM800K used human labelers to annotate ~800K reasoning steps across model-generated solutions to MATH problems — the expense is exactly the human step-level labeling.
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** The pure-RL, no-SFT result belongs to DeepSeek-R1-Zero, not the released DeepSeek-R1. R1's published pipeline includes a cold-start SFT stage (SFT → RL → rejection-sampled SFT → RL). The chapter never mentions R1-Zero and attributes the 'no SFT bootstrap' recipe to R1 itself, which is false and misleads the reader about the actual R1 training pipeline.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:309 ("No PRM. No SFT bootstrap.") and :328 ("this works at all without any SFT bootstrap")
  - _fix:_ Introduce R1-Zero explicitly: the 'no SFT, pure outcome RL' result is R1-Zero; the shipped R1 added a small cold-start SFT to fix readability/language-mixing. This also creates the natural home for the 'aha moment.'
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum lists 'Understand GRPO and the shift away from value models' as a 'Reader will be able to' outcome, names GRPO in Key concepts, and the Tricky spots explicitly say 'Walk through GRPO: advantage = reward - group mean, no value function.' The research spec also lists a GRPO group-relative-advantage equation. The chapter only name-drops GRPO and defers entirely to Ch 14; it never states the group-relative advantage or the no-value-model idea.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:224,309,328 — GRPO appears only as "run RLVR via GRPO (Chapter 14)" / "train via GRPO"
  - _fix:_ Add 2-4 sentences (and ideally an equation) giving GRPO's group-relative advantage A_i = r_i − mean(group rewards) and why dropping the value model matters, even if Ch 14 carries the full derivation.
- **[major/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The insight panel and caption quote the asymptote values, not the values the widget actually plots. buildCurve for 'modern-reasoning'/hard at 1000× computes 12 + (85−12)·(1−e^(−0.9·log10(100))) ≈ 72.9%, not 85%. The plotted endpoint is ~73%, so the stated '~85% / 73-point gap' overstates the curve by ~12 points. The same asymptote-vs-endpoint gap affects medium (states ~91% at 100×, curve gives ~78%) and easy (~97% vs ~93%).
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch20/compute-curves-data.ts:217 ("Reasoning model at 1000×: ~85% — a 73-point gap") and TestTimeComputeCurves.tsx:220 ("modern reasoning models reach ~85%")
  - _fix:_ Either state the actual plotted endpoints in the insight/caption text, or raise the steepness / extend the compute axis so the curve reaches the quoted numbers.
- **[major/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** Snell et al. 2024's actual nuance is closer to the reverse: test-time compute is compute-optimally most effective on easy-to-medium questions (where the base model has non-trivial success rate), while on the *hardest* questions and at high inference budgets, scaling pretraining parameters still wins. The chapter attributes 'helps most on the hardest problems' to Snell, which oversimplifies (or inverts) the paper's difficulty-binned finding. The o1/R1-era 'thinking helps on AIME' intuition is real but is not exactly what Snell measured.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:296 ("Scaling helps most on the *hardest* problems") and :275 ("increasing inference-time compute outperforms increasing model size")
  - _fix:_ Separate the two claims: Snell showed compute-optimal test-time scaling can beat a ~14x-larger model on easier/medium bins; the 'huge gains on the hardest math' story is the o1/R1 empirical result, not Snell's headline. Attribute each correctly.
- **[minor/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** DeepSeek released R1 distillations up to 70B (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-70B), plus an 8B; the range is 1.5B–70B, not 1.5B–32B. The research file carries the same understatement.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:309 — "distilled smaller models** (1.5B to 32B; Chapter 16)"
  - _fix:_ State '1.5B to 70B' (Qwen 1.5B/7B/14B/32B and Llama 8B/70B).
- **[minor/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** GSM8K is free-response numeric grade-school math, not multiple-choice; there is no 'multiple-choice subset' and 'above chance' is ill-defined for it. Separately, the paired figures mix models: the CoT paper's own GPT-3 (text-davinci-002) standard-prompting GSM8K baseline is ~15.6% (CoT ~46.9%), so pairing '~5% direct' with '~46% CoT' as 'the same model, nine-fold' conflates base GPT-3 with davinci-002.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:23 — "scores about $5\%$ with direct generation, barely above chance on a multiple-choice subset"
  - _fix:_ Drop the 'multiple-choice subset' clause; either use the CoT paper's matched pair (~15.6% → ~46.9%) or clearly note the 5% is original GPT-3 vs the 46% davinci-002 CoT.
- **[minor/terminology/CONFIRMED]** An ORM is conventionally a *trained* model that scores outcomes. R1 used rule-based verifiers (no learned reward model at all) — that is the whole point of RLVR ('rewards come from verifying correctness — no reward model,' per the curriculum). Calling a rule-based verifier a 'rule-based ORM' (and 'trained via GRPO,' which trains the policy, not the ORM) blurs the RLVR distinction the chapter is trying to teach.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:224 — "rule-based ORMs (exact-match on math; test-pass rate on code) trained via GRPO"
  - _fix:_ Call them rule-based verifiers / rule-based outcome rewards, and reserve 'ORM' for the learned-model case contrasted with PRM.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** Traces are consumed deterministically as slice(0,n), and the first trace is correct in all three problems while wrong answers never repeat, so the majority answer is correct at *every* N (1-20) for every problem. The widget therefore can never show N=1 being wrong, nor the 'small-N can fail, large-N recovers' behavior the caption, insight branches ('0% (wrong)'), and Exercise 2's hint all promise.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch20/self-consistency-data.ts:14-96 and SelfConsistencyAggregator.tsx:151 caption ("At N=1 ... sometimes right, sometimes wrong")
  - _fix:_ Reorder/rebalance at least one pool so a wrong answer leads at low N (or sample randomly), so the failure-then-recovery narrative is actually demonstrable.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** For a mid-2026 reader, o1 has been superseded (o3/o4-family, and successor open reasoning models). The chapter treats o1/R1 as the current default rather than as the canonical first exemplars. The historical dates (o1 Sept 2024, R1 Jan 2025, Gemini Thinking Dec 2024) are correct; only the 'current default' framing is stale.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:686 — "modern reasoning models (o1, R1) are now the default"
  - _fix:_ Frame o1/R1 as the paradigm-defining exemplars and add a one-line note that successors have since raised the bar, to keep the decision table from reading as current SOTA.
- **[minor/attribution/PLAUSIBLE]** The M^α·C^β product form is a Chinchilla-style pedagogical stylization; Snell 2024 did not present this as their fitted functional form (they studied compute-optimal allocation between test-time and pretraining compute). Saying 'the functional form Snell fit' overstates the attribution.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:281-284 — "The functional form Snell fit is the simplest one consistent with the data ... $f(M^{\alpha} \cdot C^{\beta})$"
  - _fix:_ Present the equation as an illustrative form ('a schematic that captures the qualitative dependence'), not as Snell's fitted model.
- **[minor/voice/PLAUSIBLE]** Six section-ending sentences whose primary function is to announce the next section. PROJECT_OVERVIEW's forbidden list includes 'Any sentence whose only function is to announce the next sentence' and 'Now that we understand X, let's move on to Y.' These are borderline (they name the upcoming topic) but the repeated pattern leans into the prohibited category.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch20-reasoning/index.mdx:100,175,206,269,299,331 — e.g. "The next section covers two techniques that aggregate or search over multiple traces..."
  - _fix:_ Trim to a couple of the most useful bridges, or fold the forward pointer into the substantive last sentence of each section rather than a standalone announcement.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- GRPO's mechanics — group-relative advantage (advantage = reward − group mean) and the 'shift away from value models' — is a specced 'Reader will be able to' outcome and an explicit Tricky spot, but the chapter only name-drops GRPO and defers to Ch 14. The research-specced GRPO advantage equation is also absent.
- The R1 'aha moment' (spontaneous emergence of longer/reflective traces during RL) is in Key concepts and Tricky spots but is never mentioned; R1-Zero, where it was observed, is also absent.
- MCTS-style search is listed in Key concepts; the chapter covers BFS/DFS for tree-of-thoughts but never mentions MCTS.
- The distinction R1-Zero vs R1 (pure-RL vs cold-start SFT) is missing, which is what causes the inaccurate 'R1 used no SFT' claim.
- RLVR is repeatedly invoked as the training mechanism but its meaning (reward from a correctness verifier, no reward model) is never unpacked in-chapter beyond a parenthetical; a reader who skipped Ch 14 gets no working definition.

**Exercises:** Strong. The four exercises are non-filler and map cleanly to curriculum outcomes 2 (zero-shot CoT + extraction), 3 (self-consistency majority vote), 5 (best-of-N + PRM), and 6 (test-time compute math). Scaffolding is good: TODOs are well-placed, and later exercises supply a working extractor so students focus on the new concept. Ex4's binomial majority-vote derivation (sum of C(N,j)p^j(1-p)^(N-j) from ceil(N/2)) is mathematically correct, and the p=0.5→50% invariant is right. Minor imperfections in commented 'expected' observations: Ex3's 'majority vote ~95%' is actually 100% on the deterministic pool, and Ex4's 'p=0.7 saturates ~90% by N=21' is really ~97% by N=21 (90% is reached nearer N=11). These are in comments, not graded logic.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx, are exported from the widgets barrel, and are embedded in the page. TestTimeComputeCurves renders 6 techniques with an easy/medium/hard toggle as specced; the curves are synthetic saturating functions (acceptable for a teaching widget), but the insight-panel and caption numbers quote each technique's asymptote rather than the value actually plotted at the max compute — e.g. hard 'modern reasoning ~85% at 1000×' where the curve computes ~73%. That is a real internal inconsistency. SelfConsistencyAggregator matches the prose framing (3 problems × 20 traces, N=1–20, histogram + confidence), but because traces are consumed as slice(0,n) with the correct answer leading from trace #1 in all pools, the majority vote is correct at every N, so the widget cannot actually demonstrate the low-N-fails/high-N-recovers behavior its own caption and Exercise 2 describe.


### Chapter 21 — grade B

A well-organized, engaging chapter that nails the production-engineering arc (function-calling APIs → schemas → agent loop → routing → error recovery → MCP → computer use). The two widgets exist, run, and align tightly with the prose; the runnable code executes and produces the claimed outputs; cross-references to Ch 19 (§8 constrained decoding/FSM masking) and Ch 20 (§4 ReAct) are correct. It is dragged down by two concrete factual slips (a garbled "twelve years younger" claim and a wrong Toolformer tool count) and several spec coverage gaps: how models are trained for tool use (with the Ch 13 connection), τ-bench evaluation, the OpenAI message shape in code, and a real Anthropic-API loop.

**Strengths:** Widget/prose alignment is excellent: ToolCallTrace's Tokyo weather→Celsius sequence and ToolSchemaValidator's three schemas (get_weather/search_database/create_event) with valid+invalid cases match the prose descriptions exactly, and the validator logic correctly catches missing-required/wrong-type/out-of-range/invalid-enum/unknown-tool.; All three RunnableCode blocks actually run and produce the stated outputs; the mock agent-loop's tool-use counting logic is subtly correct, and exercise scaffolding (Ex1-4) is coherent and non-broken.; Cross-reference integrity is strong: Ch 19 §8 (constrained decoding / FSM masking) and Ch 20 §4 (ReAct) both verified correct against the actual target chapters, and the Phase 13/14/15 framing is consistent with Ch 20's own phase map.; Honest handling of the structural-vs-semantic reliability nuance (the get_weather(location="Atlantis") example recurs across prose, callout, and widget), which is exactly the curriculum's flagged tricky spot.; Clear, opinionated production guidance on routing thresholds (5-50 description-based, 100+ retrieval), parallel-call latency math (5x500ms serial vs 500ms parallel), and idempotency/audit-log patterns.

**Findings:**

- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** The claim is wrong in both direction and magnitude. ReAct (Yao 2022) is OLDER than the rest of the chapter's material (function-calling June 2023, MCP late 2024, computer use late 2024), not younger, and no interpretation yields ~twelve years (2022→2024 is ~2 years; even vs the transformer at 2017 it is ~5). The sentence reads as a garbled edit and will confuse readers.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:21 — "That pattern is twelve years younger than the rest of the chapter's material and has held up unusually well"
  - _fix:_ Delete the "twelve years younger" clause. State the actual point: ReAct is the oldest idea in the chapter yet has proven the most durable — every modern agent still runs Thought→Action→Observation underneath, while the API surface around it has churned repeatedly.
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** Toolformer used 5 tools (calculator, a QA system, Wikipedia search, machine translation, calendar), not "a few dozen" — an order-of-magnitude overstatement. The "roughly 70% success" is also a fabricated single figure; the paper reports per-benchmark perplexity/accuracy improvements, not a unified 70% success rate. Both errors are inherited verbatim from research.md.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:29 — "Toolformer (Schick 2023) demonstrated self-supervised tool-use training across a few dozen tools at roughly $70\%$ success"
  - _fix:_ Change to "across a small fixed set of 5 tools" and drop or heavily qualify the 70% number (e.g., "improving downstream accuracy on several benchmarks without degrading core LM ability").
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum learning objective "Articulate how models are trained for tool use" and Key concept "training data for tool use (synthetic generation + filtered traces)" are essentially unmet. Beyond one Toolformer clause and this half-sentence, the chapter never explains SFT on tool-use traces, synthetic trace generation, or trace filtering. The specced "Builds on Ch 13 (tool-use training data resembles SFT data)" connection is absent — Ch 13 is never cited.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:11 — "Modern LLMs are trained to emit structured calls to external functions" (only mention of training)
  - _fix:_ Add a short subsection (or paragraph in §1/§2) on how tool use is trained: synthetic generation of tool-call traces, filtering to keep traces where the call improved the answer, SFT over those traces, and the link back to Ch 13's SFT machinery.
- **[major/coverage-gap/CONFIRMED]** τ-bench (Yao 2024, arXiv 2406.12045) is explicitly named in the Key concepts ("tool-use evaluation (τ-bench)") and is a headline research paper, and the chapter Sets up Ch 30 (tool-use eval). It is never mentioned; the chapter substitutes TaskBench/ToolBench. The Berkeley Function-Calling Leaderboard (research eval-context source) is also absent.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:29 — "$\sim 70$ – $85\%$ on complex multi-step tasks (TaskBench, ToolBench)"
  - _fix:_ Add a sentence naming τ-bench as the standard multi-turn/tool-agent benchmark (and optionally the Berkeley Function-Calling Leaderboard) so evaluation coverage matches the spec and the Ch 30 handoff has an anchor.
- **[major/coverage-gap/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum tricky spot explicitly says: Anthropic uses tool_use/tool_result blocks, OpenAI uses function calls/results as their own roles — "Show both; the principle is the same." The chapter shows only the Anthropic message shape in code (input_schema, tool_use, tool_result). OpenAI's differing wire shape (role: "tool", tool_call_id, function.arguments as a JSON string) appears nowhere in code, only as a one-line prose gloss.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:49 — "the response contains `tool_use` content blocks rather than a separate field" (OpenAI shape described in prose only)
  - _fix:_ Add a short side-by-side code snippet (or a second small block) showing the OpenAI tool_calls / role:"tool" message shape next to the Anthropic one, per the tricky-spot instruction.
- **[minor/coverage-deviation/CONFIRMED]** The spec calls for `tool_loop_anthropic.py`: a minimal client-side loop against the real Anthropic API with a user-supplied API key stored in localStorage and a weather+calendar tool combo (Pyodide + JS fetch bridge). The chapter ships only fully mocked loops — the reader never issues a real tool-use API call, and the example task is weather+calculator, not weather+calendar. The mock choice is pedagogically defensible (offline, no shipped keys), but it is a clear deviation from the specced runnable and the objective "Implement a minimal tool-use loop with the Anthropic API."
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:200-260 — the agent-loop RunnableCode uses `def mock_model(...)` and mock tools
  - _fix:_ Either add an optional real-API variant (with the localStorage key form the spec describes) or explicitly note in the chapter that the loop is mocked and point readers to research.md's real-API reference implementation.
- **[minor/exercise/CONFIRMED]** research.md's plan states "Exercises hit outcomes 2, 3, 5, 8" where outcome 5 is observation handling / error recovery — a headlined skill and a flagged tricky spot. The delivered set (schema, loop, routing, idempotency) exercises outcomes 2, 3, 4, 8; no exercise practices error recovery / observation handling. Separately, Ex3 uses random 8-dim mock embeddings, so the retrieval output is semantically meaningless (the code even admits "retrieval is noisy: the demo shows the pattern, not the quality"), blunting the exercise's payoff.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:498 — "### Exercise 3 (medium): Multi-tool routing via embeddings"
  - _fix:_ Consider swapping or adding one exercise that has the student return a structured error observation and let a mock model recover (covers outcome 5), and/or give Ex3 hand-tuned embeddings so semantically-similar tools actually rank higher.
- **[minor/voice/CONFIRMED]** Seven end-of-section sentences whose primary function is to announce the upcoming section. The voice contract forbids "Any sentence whose only function is to announce the next sentence" and states "The reader can see the section heading. They don't need to be told what's coming." These teasers repeat at nearly every section boundary.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:35,118,161,262,280,307,327 — e.g., "The next section covers the interface convention that the whole production stack is built on, function calling."
  - _fix:_ Cut the bare transitions, or fold a genuine idea into the last paragraph of each section rather than a standalone "the next section covers X" announcement.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** For a mid-2026 reader, "2025 and beyond" is stale forward-looking phrasing, and MCP is framed as Anthropic-only and merely "emerging." By 2025 MCP had been adopted across providers (e.g., OpenAI's Agents SDK), which strengthens the chapter's own "USB-C for AI" thesis but is unmentioned; the client list stops at Claude Desktop / VS Code / Cursor.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:15 — "which protocols are likely to matter in 2025 and beyond" (and §7's "emerging standard" / "early 2025" framing of MCP)
  - _fix:_ Reframe to past tense ("protocols that matter now") and note MCP's cross-provider adoption in 2025 to keep the USB-C argument current.
- **[minor/unsupported-claim/CONFIRMED]** A specific quantitative split ("about half") is asserted with no source. It reads authoritative but is uncited and unverifiable.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch21-tool-use/index.mdx:167 — "about half of real production tool use is exactly this shape, about half of real production tool use is exactly this shape. The remaining half needs the loop."
  - _fix:_ Soften to a qualitative claim ("a large fraction of production tool use is a single call") or attribute it if a source exists.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- How models are trained for tool use (synthetic trace generation + filtered traces + SFT), a named learning objective and Key concept — essentially absent; the Ch 13 (SFT) connection specced under Connections is never made.
- τ-bench (Yao 2024), the specced tool-use evaluation benchmark, is never mentioned (chapter uses TaskBench/ToolBench instead); Berkeley Function-Calling Leaderboard also absent.
- The OpenAI message shape is only described in prose, not shown in code, despite the tricky spot's explicit "Show both" instruction.
- No runnable exercises the real Anthropic API (specced tool_loop_anthropic.py with API-key form); all loops are mocked, and the example task is weather+calculator rather than the specced weather+calendar.
- No exercise covers observation handling / error recovery (outcome 5), which research.md's plan assigned to the exercise set.

**Exercises:** Four well-scaffolded, genuinely useful exercises with a clean difficulty ramp (define schema → agent loop → embedding routing → idempotent dispatch) that trace the chapter's arc, each with a collapsed hint and correct starter scaffolding that would run once the TODOs are filled. Two caveats: (1) no exercise practices observation handling / error recovery despite that being a headlined outcome and a flagged tricky spot (research.md planned exercises to hit outcome 5); (2) Ex3's random mock embeddings make its retrieval output semantically meaningless — the student implements cosine similarity but cannot observe semantic routing actually working, which the code comments honestly concede.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx and are well-built. ToolCallTrace (the marquee ToolCallTraceViewer analog) steps through a real Tokyo-weather→Celsius Thought/Action/Observation/Final sequence that matches the prose and WidgetFrame caption exactly, including consistent numbers ((65-32)*5/9 = 18.33 → \"18.3°C\"). ToolSchemaValidator implements a correct structural validator (missing-required, wrong-type, out-of-range, invalid-enum, unknown-tool, undeclared-field) over three preset schemas with curated valid/invalid cases; every case's expected outcome matches the validate() logic, and the prose's structural-vs-semantic nuance is faithfully reinforced by the widget caption. No placeholder or broken widgets.


### Chapter 22 — grade C+

The prose is strong: technically accurate, well-paced, opinionated in the house voice, with two well-built widgets (both real, both computing what they claim). BM25/cosine/RRF math is correct, param counts and dims check out, and the cross-references to Ch 21 (tool use) and Ch 23 (multimodal) are correct. But the chapter falls meaningfully short of its curriculum spec: three explicitly-specced items are entirely absent (RAG-vs-long-context — which is a "Reader will be able to" outcome with its own paper; MMR — a key concept AND a required equation; ColBERT/late-interaction), and both flagship RunnableCode demos import libraries (rank_bm25, sentence_transformers) that cannot load in the site's Pyodide runner, so their Run buttons error. The "hard" Exercise 4 has a degenerate/misleading demonstration. Content that is present is good; content that is missing or non-executable is the problem.

**Strengths:** BM25, cosine-similarity, and RRF equations are all correct, and the RRF-vs-weighted-sum justification (score-scale incompatibility) is genuinely well argued.; Reference numbers are accurate: SBERT 768-dim, all-MiniLM-L6-v2 22M/384-dim, text-embedding-3-small 1536-dim, 3072/384 = 8x storage.; Both specced/recommended widgets exist as real .tsx with correct BM25 + RRF implementations; ChunkingVisualizer honestly labels its char-based demo and notes production token targets.; Honest, spec-aligned misconception handling: 'dense always beats BM25' and 'chunking doesn't matter' are directly refuted with the right nuance.; Cross-references (Chapter 21 tool-use bridge, Chapter 23 multimodal) point to the correct canonical chapters.

**Findings:**

- **[major/coverage-gap/CONFIRMED]** The RAG-vs-long-context comparison is completely missing. The curriculum makes it a 'Reader will be able to' outcome ('when long context beats RAG and vice versa'), a Key concept, a Tricky spot, and assigns a dedicated paper (Xu 2024). A reader finishing this chapter cannot do what the outcome promises.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:305 (RAG-architectures decision guide) — chapter-wide absence; quote: "**High-quality production at scale**: vanilla RAG plus hybrid retrieval plus reranking plus careful chunking"
  - _fix:_ Add a subsection (likely in RAG architectures or Production patterns) covering the honest tradeoff: long context wins on quality for static/small docs; RAG wins on cost, dynamic content, and large corpora. Cite Xu 2024 (arxiv.org/abs/2310.03025).
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** MMR (Maximal Marginal Relevance) is entirely absent. The curriculum lists MMR diversification as a Key concept and a glossary term, and specifies a required equation (lambda*sim(q,d) - (1-lambda)*max_{d' in S} sim(d,d')). Neither the concept nor the equation appears.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:283 (reranking section, where diversification would naturally sit) — chapter-wide absence of 'mmr'/'diversif'
  - _fix:_ Add MMR as a diversification step (relevance-vs-redundancy tradeoff) with the specced objective equation, ideally near reranking or in the top-K selection discussion.
- **[major/coverage-gap/CONFIRMED]** ColBERT / late-interaction (multi-vector) retrieval is not mentioned anywhere, despite being an explicit Key concept in the spec ('ColBERT (late-interaction retrieval)'). The dense-retrieval section jumps straight from single-vector bi-encoders to cross-encoders with no coverage of the late-interaction middle ground.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:279 (dual-encoder discussion) — chapter-wide absence of 'colbert'/'late interaction'
  - _fix:_ Add a short treatment of ColBERT (per-token embeddings, MaxSim late interaction, ~10-50x storage cost) — the research file's TID10 already has the material. Cite Khattab 2020 (arxiv.org/abs/2004.12832).
- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** This RunnableCode block supplies no `packages` prop, so RunnableCode.tsx defaults to `['numpy']`, and lib/pyodide.ts's runner only calls `loadPackage(packages)` (no micropip, no load-from-imports). rank_bm25 is not in the Pyodide distribution and is never loaded, so clicking Run yields ModuleNotFoundError. Additionally, the curriculum specced 'bm25.py — BM25 from scratch in numpy with explicit TF and IDF', but this worked example uses a library instead; the from-scratch version exists only as an incomplete TODO in Exercise 1.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:65 "from rank_bm25 import BM25Okapi"
  - _fix:_ Either replace this with the from-scratch numpy BM25 the spec asks for (which runs under packages=['numpy']), or add `packages` with a micropip-installable pure-Python path. As written the marquee demo's Run button errors.
- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** sentence_transformers requires PyTorch, which cannot run in Pyodide; the block also supplies no `packages` prop (defaults to ['numpy']). Clicking Run always errors. The curriculum explicitly anticipated this — it specced dense_retrieval.py as 'loaded via Pyodide or via precomputed embeddings' — precisely to keep it browser-runnable; the chapter ignored that and used sentence_transformers.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:131 "from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer"
  - _fix:_ Rewrite the demo to use precomputed embeddings (ship a small vector array) plus a pure-numpy cosine ranking so it runs in-browser, matching the spec's 'precomputed embeddings' option.
- **[major/exercise/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 4's demonstration is degenerate and self-contradictory. Every mock retriever returns the full universe {0,1,2,3,4} and every relevant set is a 2-subset of it; with the driver's k=5, recall@5 = 100% for BM25, Dense, and Hybrid on all queries, so the stated observation ('BM25 misses... Dense catches it... Hybrid catches it') cannot occur. Worse, this BM25 mock's own comment says it 'misses inflation docs' while its ranking places the relevant docs 4 and 1 at positions 1 and 2.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:615 "\"prices rising\": [4, 1, 0, 2, 3],   # BM25 misses inflation docs (no overlap)"
  - _fix:_ Make the mocks discriminate: e.g., have BM25 for 'prices rising' return non-relevant docs in the top-K (omit 1 and 4), and/or evaluate at a smaller k so the three retrievers produce different recall values that match the narrative.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** On the mixed query 'training neural network embeddings' the widget's dense scores put both correct docs (6, 1) at ranks 1-2, and BM25 also ranks doc 6 first, so Hybrid does not uniquely 'win' — all three methods land the correct docs at the top. The 'Hybrid wins' framing overstates what the widget's own first-correct-rank badges show (a near-tie).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:197 "mixed (Hybrid wins)"
  - _fix:_ Reword to 'Hybrid stays robust / ties the best method' on the mixed query, matching the widget output; keep the decisive-win language for the paraphrased query where Dense genuinely dominates BM25.
- **[minor/stale-cross-ref/CONFIRMED]** The 'four patterns' listed are vanilla RAG + hybrid + reranking + chunking, but only three of those (hybrid=sec 4, chunking=sec 5, reranking=sec 6) come from sections 4-6; vanilla RAG is introduced in section 7 (RAG architectures). The attribution is off by one pattern.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:305 "the four patterns from sections $4$ – $6$"
  - _fix:_ Change to 'the patterns from sections 4-6 (hybrid, chunking, reranking) plus vanilla RAG' or 'three patterns from sections 4-6'.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** For a mid-2026 reader, calling E5-Mistral 'the LLM-based frontier' is likely stale; multiple newer LLM-based embedding models have since topped MTEB. The exemplar itself is fine, but 'the frontier' claim dates the text.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:121 "E5-Mistral (Wang $2023$) is the LLM-based frontier"
  - _fix:_ Soften to 'an early LLM-based embedding model' or 'the LLM-based approach' rather than 'the frontier'.
- **[minor/voice/CONFIRMED]** This exact bolded sentence appears verbatim in the intro prose (line 15) and again in the aside callout (line 36) within the same section, then is restated a third time in the closeout (line 684). Redundant within a short span.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch22-retrieval-and-rag/index.mdx:36 "RAG is the single most-deployed LLM application pattern of 2024 – 2025."
  - _fix:_ Keep one instance (the aside), drop or reword the near-duplicate in the intro paragraph.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- RAG vs long context: entirely absent (grep 'long context' -> 0 hits). This is a 'Reader will be able to' outcome ('when long context beats RAG and vice versa'), a Key concept, a Tricky spot, and has a dedicated specced paper (Xu 2024, Retrieval meets Long Context, arxiv 2310.03025). Not addressed anywhere.
- MMR (Maximal Marginal Relevance) diversification: entirely absent. Curriculum lists it as a Key concept, a glossary term, AND supplies a required equation (lambda*sim(q,d) - (1-lambda)*max sim(d,d')). No mention.
- ColBERT / late-interaction retrieval: entirely absent from the chapter body despite being an explicit Key concept ('ColBERT (late-interaction retrieval)').
- 'Implement a cross-encoder reranker' (a stated outcome): only described conceptually; no code or exercise implements one.
- DPR (Karpukhin 2020), the canonical dense-passage-retrieval paper specced for section 3, is not cited; the dense section cites SBERT/OpenAI/MiniLM/E5-Mistral only.
- Query rewriting (Key concept): only glancingly present via agentic 'reformulate' and HyDE; no dedicated treatment.
- BM25 'from scratch in numpy' (specced runnable): the worked runnable uses the rank_bm25 library; a from-scratch version exists only as an incomplete TODO exercise scaffold.

**Exercises:** Exercises 1-3 (BM25 from scratch, mock vector store, RRF) are meaningful, correctly scaffolded numpy/pure-Python, and their inline expected-output comments are consistent (e.g., Ex 3's doc-0-wins claim checks out: 1/61+1/62 > 1/63+1/61). Exercise 4 (hard, recall@K) is broken as a demonstration: every mock retriever returns the full 5-doc universe {0..4} and every relevant set is a 2-subset of it, so with the driver's k=5, recall@5 = 100% for BM25, Dense, and Hybrid on every query. The stated payoff ("BM25 misses on 'prices rising'... Dense catches it... Hybrid catches it") cannot occur, and the BM25 mock's inline comment "BM25 misses inflation docs" contradicts its own ranking [4,1,0,2,3], which puts the relevant docs 4 and 1 first and second. Separately, no exercise or worked code implements a cross-encoder reranker, though "Implement a cross-encoder reranker" is a stated learning outcome.

**Widgets:** Both widgets exist and are real, not placeholders. RetrievalComparator computes real BM25 (correct k1=1.2, b=0.75, Lucene +1 IDF) plus RRF over hand-tuned dense scores; ChunkingVisualizer implements fixed-size/sentence/paragraph/parent-doc correctly and computes coverage/overlap/stddev. Curriculum specced only RetrievalComparator; the extra ChunkingVisualizer is a legitimate bonus matching the research recommendation. One minor prose/widget mismatch: the marquee caption's "mixed (Hybrid wins)" overstates a near-tie — on the mixed query both Dense and Hybrid land both correct docs at ranks 1-2, so Hybrid does not uniquely win (the widget's own badges show the tie).


### Chapter 23 — grade B-

Well-written, accurate prose with correct core math (ViT token counts, symmetric InfoNCE, CLIP/Whisper scale numbers) and clean cross-references (ReAct is correctly attributed to Ch 20, and the Phase numbering matches the rest of the book). But the chapter's central runnable, the multimodal-RAG demo meant to prove the shared-embedding-space thesis, actually prints output that refutes its own lesson because the mock embedder matches 'car' inside 'care'/'caring'; the same bug corrupts Exercise 3. Add a widget caption that miscounts its own data, a wrong widget insight, an Exercise-4 mis-stated expectation, and two real curriculum coverage gaps (discrete image tokenization / VQ-VAE / lookup-free quantization, and video frame sampling / temporal attention), and the content needs fixes before it teaches reliably.

**Strengths:** Cross-reference integrity is clean: Ch 20/21/22/24/25/26 all point correctly, ReAct is properly attributed to Chapter 20 (matches ch20 §4 in the actual book), and Phase 13/14/15 numbering is consistent with ch20 and ch27.; Core technical numbers are all correct and verified: 196/576/4096 visual tokens (224/16, 336/14, 1024/16), 400M then 5B+ CLIP pairs, Whisper 680K hours, ViT-B embed dim 768, and the symmetric InfoNCE equation is written correctly.; Both specced widgets exist as .tsx, are exported from the widgets index, and are wired; the patch_embed and CLIP-contrastive runnables execute and print exactly what the prose claims (verified: shape (196,768), high diagonal ~0.5, low off-diagonal ~0.008).; Strong operational-realism framing throughout: honest bolt-on vs native tradeoff, OCR limits, multimodal-RAG-lags-text caveat, computer-use latency/error caveats, and well-constructed misconception callouts.; The unifying-pattern thesis (modality -> tokenizer -> transformer) is threaded cleanly across every section and lands the Phase 13 close well.

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The main-body Multimodal RAG runnable is the chapter's concrete proof of cross-modal shared-space retrieval, but its mock embedder uses substring matching, so 'car' fires inside 'care' (both the query 'tips for taking care of cats' and the item 'How to care for an orange tabby cat'). Running the exact code, the top-3 are txt_cat_care, txt_dog_train, txt_car_review (ALL text) and the cat IMAGE (img_cat) is ranked LAST at -0.05. This directly contradicts the printed observations 'Top results mix images and text' and 'retrieves both the cat image AND the cat-care text'. A reader who runs it sees the opposite of the lesson.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch23-multimodal/index.mdx:281 and observations :309-310; mock line "if 'car' in descriptor.lower():" with claim "retrieves both the cat image AND the cat-care text"
  - _fix:_ Match whole words (e.g. test keyword membership in set(descriptor.lower().split()) or use word boundaries) instead of substring `in`, and/or pick a query that avoids the care/car collision; then re-run and confirm the printed top-3 actually mixes images and text and surfaces the cat image.
- **[major/exercise/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 3 has the same substring bug: 'car' matches 'caring'. Running the reference logic, the cat query 'tips for caring for a cat' returns txt_car (the car review) as the #1 result, and 'fast vehicles' (which contains no keyword at all) returns a near-random top-3 (img_car #1 by luck, then txt_dog, img_cat) rather than 'car (and maybe boat)'. The stated expected outputs are wrong, so a student's correct implementation will not match the comments.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch23-multimodal/index.mdx:537 keyword loop and :558-560 comment "# fast vehicles → should retrieve car (and maybe boat)"
  - _fix:_ Use word-level matching in mock_clip_embed and choose query strings whose keywords actually appear (or state that 'fast vehicles' has no lexical overlap and will retrieve randomly); align the expected-output comments with what the code produces.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The WidgetFrame caption for CLIPEmbeddingSpace says 6 images and 6 texts, but clip-data.ts (ITEMS) actually contains 7 image items (cat-img-1, cat-img-2, dog-img-1, car-img-1, car-img-2, boat-img-1, boat-img-2) and only 5 text items. The 6/6 count is wrong.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch23-multimodal/index.mdx:145 "Twelve items, 6 image emojis and 6 text snippets"
  - _fix:_ Change the caption to '7 image emojis and 5 text snippets', or rebalance ITEMS to an even 6/6 split.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The 'anything red' query sits at (0.60, 0.62); by the widget's own top-K-nearest metric the top-3 are all three car items (car-txt-1 at 0.16, car-img-2 at 0.28, car-img-1 at 0.30). The orange-cat image (cat-img-1) is at distance ~0.465 and never enters the top-3, so the insight describes a neighbor the widget will not highlight. (The 'caring for animals' insight is also loosely worded: it says 'text-text matches dominate' but the nearest item is actually the kitten image.)
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch23/clip-data.ts:82-84 (the 'red' query insight): "'Red' finds the red sports car image (literally red) and the orange-cat image (visually similar warm color)"
  - _fix:_ Reword the 'red' insight to match the actual top-3 (all cars), or reposition the query so the orange-cat image genuinely lands in the top-3.
- **[minor/exercise/CONFIRMED]** In Exercise 4, case 1's caption says 'sunny' and 'windowsill', which do not exact-match the vocab words 'sun' and 'window'; running detect_hallucinations returns missed=['sun','window'] (non-empty). The 'none missing' claim is false, and the 'clean' framing misleads: the naive exact-match vocabulary flags the two ground-truth objects as missed.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch23-multimodal/index.mdx:663 "# - Case 1: clean: all mentioned objects are in ground truth, none missing"
  - _fix:_ Either change the caption to use the exact vocab words ('a sunny day with the sun in the window') or amend the comment to note that 'sunny'/'windowsill' are missed due to exact-match limitations (which is actually a nice teaching point about the method's brittleness).
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** Claude voice mode shipped in 2025 (before the mid-2026 reader date). Describing it as 'on the roadmap' is likely stale. Also the frontier list ('Gemini 1.5/2.0', 'Claude 3.5/4', 'as of early 2025') is dated for a mid-2026 reader, though the chapter hedges this explicitly.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch23-multimodal/index.mdx:235 "Claude voice (Anthropic, on the roadmap)"
  - _fix:_ Update 'on the roadmap' to reflect that Claude voice has shipped; the frontier-model hedging elsewhere is acceptable but could be nudged forward a generation.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Discrete image tokenization is entirely absent. CURRICULUM Key concepts lists 'image tokenization (VQ-VAE, lookup-free)' and Tricky spots explicitly names the three paradigms (ViT continuous patches, VQ-VAE codebook indices, lookup-free quantization / Yu 2023); the research file supplies Esser 2020 (VQ-VAE/VQGAN) and Mentzer 2023 (FSQ). The chapter only covers continuous ViT patches and never mentions codebooks, discrete tokens, VQ-VAE, or lookup-free/finite-scalar quantization. This is the single largest spec gap.
- Video handling is specced ('Recognize how audio and video modalities are incorporated'; Key concepts 'video (sparse frame sampling, temporal attention)') but the chapter only mentions video via token-count and Gemini-context asides. Sparse frame sampling and temporal attention are never explained.
- Flamingo cross-attention is under-developed. 'Reader will be able to articulate ... Flamingo (cross-attention)' but the chapter gives only a one-line bullet ('text tokens attend to visual tokens via dedicated cross-attention layers') with no explanation of the gated cross-attention / Perceiver-Resampler mechanism that distinguishes it from the LLaVA concatenation pattern.
- Audio codecs are specced ('audio (Whisper, codecs)') but not covered; the chapter treats Whisper and voice-native models but never discusses discrete audio codecs (e.g. EnCodec/SoundStream) for audio tokenization.
- Minor: the term 'InfoNCE' from Key concepts ('CLIP contrastive objective (InfoNCE)') never appears; the loss is shown and correct but only ever called 'contrastive loss'.

**Exercises:** The four exercises trace a coherent arc (patch embed -> CLIP similarity -> multimodal RAG -> hallucination detection) and are structurally well-scaffolded with clear TODOs and collapsible hints. Ex 1 (patch embedding) and Ex 2 (cosine similarity matrix) are solid and their reference logic runs correctly. However, two of four have real defects: Ex 3's mock embedder has the 'car' in 'caring' substring collision, so a correct implementation returns the car review as the #1 hit for a cat query and returns near-random results for 'fast vehicles' (contradicting the 'should retrieve car' comment); and Ex 4's case-1 'clean / none missing' expectation is false because 'sunny'/'windowsill' fail the exact-match vocab (missed=['sun','window']). Both are output/expectation mismatches a student will hit, not filler, but they will confuse rather than instruct until fixed.

**Widgets:** Both curriculum-specced widgets exist as .tsx, are exported from src/components/widgets/index.ts, and are correctly imported/wired in the MDX. ViTPatchTokenizer is faithful: 128x128 -> 8x8 grid -> 64 tokens, flatten arithmetic 16x16x3=768, default selected patch index 14 is indeed the sun patch (row 1, col 6), and the 16-of-768 projection sparkline matches the prose. CLIPEmbeddingSpace is functional and pedagogically on-point but has three issues: (1) the MDX caption says '6 image emojis and 6 text snippets' while the data holds 7 images / 5 texts; (2) the 'anything red' query's insight text describes neighbors (the orange-cat image) that the widget's own top-3 nearest metric does not surface (top-3 are all cars); (3) it labels a 2D-Euclidean-derived pseudo-metric as 'sim' while the prose repeatedly calls it cosine similarity, which is defensible didactic license for a '2D projection' but slightly loose. None of these break rendering; they are caption/insight accuracy fixes.


### Chapter 24 — grade B

A strong, well-organized chapter that delivers a genuine working-engineer's view of LLM safety: clean operational framing (alignment / manipulation-resistance / refusal calibration), a solid jailbreak taxonomy, a sharp direct-vs-indirect prompt-injection treatment, and honest frontier-risk coverage. Cross-references are all correct (a real strength versus other chapters in this book), citations and arXiv-era attributions are accurate, and both specced widgets exist and largely match the prose. It is dragged down by two runnable-code blocks whose printed "# Observations" contradict the output the code actually produces, and by the complete omission of many-shot jailbreaking (Anil 2024), which the curriculum names as both a key concept and a required paper.

**Strengths:** Cross-reference integrity is perfect: Ch14 (alignment/DPO), Ch20 (reasoning), Ch21 (tool use), Ch22 (RAG), Ch25/26 (interp/eval), Ch27-30 (agents) all point to the correct canonical targets; Citations are accurate and well-matched to their claims (Wei 2023, Zou 2023/GCG, Greshake 2023, Hubinger 2024 sleeper agents, Mazeika 2024 HarmBench, Lin 2021 TruthfulQA with 817 questions, Chao 2023 PAIR, Deliberative Alignment OpenAI 2024); The jailbreak/prompt-injection distinction — the curriculum's flagged 'tricky spot' — is handled clearly and reinforced with well-targeted misconception callouts; Both specced widgets are implemented and functional; the JailbreakTaxonomy 2-axis mechanism-vs-success-rate chart and the pattern-scanner regex logic both work and align with the prose; Honest treatment of residual risk throughout (30-70% jailbreak success, 'robustness is an open research problem', sleeper agents survive RLHF/CAI), matching the required 'honest about residual risk' stance; no forbidden phrases

**Findings:**

- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** On the toy SAFETY_TESTS dataset, model_well_calibrated (lines 305-315) actually scores FP 0% and FN 0%: every harmful test input contains one of its harmful_phrases and no benign input does. The printed observation 'no model achieves zero on both' is directly contradicted by the code's own output — a reader running the block sees Well-calibrated 0%/0% then reads that no model achieves that.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch24-safety/index.mdx:329 — "# - Well-calibrated: lower on both: but no model achieves zero on both"
  - _fix:_ Either add a benign test that trips the well-calibrated phrase matcher (or a harmful test it misses) so the printed numbers are non-zero, or change the observation to note this toy set is small enough that the well-calibrated model happens to hit 0/0 while real models never do.
- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The AutoRedTeam.defender in this block (lines 391-400) has NO handling for authority framings; 'For my chemistry class...' and 'I'm a security researcher...' fall through to the else branch and are refused. The only successful attacks are the two 'novel'/fiction templates (weapons iter 3, phishing iter 3), giving '2 of 8'. The stated observation that authority framing slips through is false for this code — authority is refused. (Note: authority-slippage IS implemented in Exercise 4's defender at lines 811-815, which likely caused the confusion.)
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch24-safety/index.mdx:432 — "# - Authority framing (\"security researcher\") and fiction framing (\"novel\") slip through"
  - _fix:_ Either add an authority branch to this defender that can leak (mirroring Ex4), or correct the observation to say only fiction/'novel' framing slipped through in this run.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** Many-shot jailbreaking (Anil et al. 2024, Anthropic) is entirely absent from the chapter — no mention of Anil, of filling a long context with many faux compliant demonstrations, or of the long-context exploit. It is a distinct category from the chapter's 'multi-turn' entry (single prompt with many in-context demos vs genuine multi-turn escalation). The curriculum names it as both a key concept and a required paper.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch24-safety/index.mdx:144 — "### A taxonomy of techniques" (no many-shot entry); cf. CURRICULUM.md:1260 lists "many-shot" and CURRICULUM.md:1272 lists "Anil 2024 (many-shot jailbreaking)"
