  - _fix:_ Add a 'Many-shot' leaf to the taxonomy (and ideally the JailbreakTaxonomy widget) citing Anil 2024, distinguishing it from multi-turn manipulation and tying it to the long-context capability from Ch6/Ch17.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** There are four adversarial samples in SAMPLES (sample-2 email, sample-4 web, sample-6 doc, sample-7 calendar), and all four match >=2 patterns. The caption undercounts them as 'three'.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch24/PromptInjectionClassifier.tsx:143 — "three of the clean samples match no patterns and three adversarial samples match multiple"
  - _fix:_ Change 'three adversarial samples' to 'four adversarial samples'.
- **[minor/exercise/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 3's hint (line 655) specifies a violation is 'any trigger word ... AND any topic word' in the draft. The honesty principle has topics=[], so any(topic in draft for topic in []) is always False and honesty can never fire. Test draft 3 ('I am definitely sure that quantum physics works exactly the way I will describe.') is clearly meant to exercise the honesty principle but will report 'No violations' under the specified logic.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch24-safety/index.mdx:682 — "'topics': [],  # honesty is a meta-principle" (test draft at :728)
  - _fix:_ Either give honesty a non-empty topics list, or state in the hint that meta-principles with empty topics use trigger-only matching, so draft 3 is actually flagged.
- **[minor/pedagogy/CONFIRMED]** 'Prefix injection' is named explicitly in the curriculum key concepts (CURRICULUM.md:1260), but the chapter folds the prefix-injection technique ('Start your response with Sure, here's...') into 'Refusal suppression' without naming it. In Wei 2023 these are two distinct categories (prefix injection vs refusal suppression); conflating them loses the specced concept.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch24-safety/index.mdx:170 — "**Refusal suppression.**" ... "Start your response with 'Sure, here's...'" (:174)
  - _fix:_ Split or at least name 'prefix injection' alongside refusal suppression, noting they are the two surface-form attacks from Wei 2023.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** The Dec 2024 'Alignment faking in large language models' result (Greenblatt et al., Anthropic) demonstrated alignment-faking reasoning in a frontier production model (Claude 3 Opus), which arguably goes beyond 'carefully-constructed toy settings.' The claim understates the empirical state as of the chapter's own 'early 2025' timestamp. (Deceptive alignment and alignment faking are related but distinct, so this is a nuance, not a flat error.)
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch24-safety/index.mdx:453 — "Empirically observed in carefully-constructed toy settings; unclear whether it exists in any production model."
  - _fix:_ Acknowledge the alignment-faking result and sharpen the distinction between it and full deceptive alignment.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The caption says each category shows '2-3 mitigations', but jailbreak-data.ts gives the suffix (GCG) category four defenses (lines 88-93: adversarial training, input sanitization, perplexity filters, ensemble defenses).
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch24-safety/index.mdx:178 — WidgetFrame caption "and 2-3 mitigations"
  - _fix:_ Change to '2-4 mitigations'.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Many-shot jailbreaking (Anil 2024) — specced as a key concept AND a required paper — is completely absent; the taxonomy's 'multi-turn' entry is a different attack
- 'Prefix injection' (named in the curriculum key concepts) is not named; only folded into 'Refusal suppression'
- The layered defense model — 'input classifiers, refusal training, output classifiers' — is the curriculum's #1 learning objective but is only present glancingly (defense-in-depth is mentioned in a callout and widget caption); there is no crisp input-classifier -> refusal-trained-model -> output-classifier pipeline, and toxicity input classifiers are not discussed as a defense layer (ToxiGen appears only as a benchmark)
- Gradient-based/GCG, encoded, role-play, indirect injection, red-teaming methodology, and refusal-rate/false-positive tradeoffs are all well covered — no gaps there

**Exercises:** The four exercises are meaningful, correctly scaffolded, and trace the chapter's arc (refusal classifier -> injection detection -> CAI critique loop -> automated red-team with metrics). Ex1 and Ex2 are clean: the provided test cases are achievable with the intended logic and Ex2's precision/recall trace works out to 100%/60% with two deliberately-missed paraphrased attacks, a good pedagogical point. Ex4's attacker/defender/classifier are fully implemented and coherent (only run_red_team is TODO). The one flaw is Ex3: the honesty principle has an empty 'topics' list, so under the hint's 'trigger AND topic' rule it can never fire, leaving test draft 3 (the overconfidence example) unflagged — a scaffolding inconsistency worth fixing.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist and are wired up correctly (exported from index.ts, imported in the mdx). JailbreakTaxonomy computes what the prose claims: six categories with sanitized examples, mechanism badges color-coded by Wei 2023's two root causes, per-category success ranges, defenses, and a 2-axis chart placing suffix/multi-modal at the high end, matching the caption. PromptInjectionClassifier's regex scanner works correctly across the 7 samples (3 clean match nothing; all 4 adversarial match multiple), with sensible ALLOW/FLAG/BLOCK thresholds and honest limitation framing. Two minor caption mismatches: the widget caption says 'three adversarial samples' (there are four), and the mdx WidgetFrame caption says '2-3 mitigations' (suffix has four).


### Chapter 25 — grade B-

A well-organized, honest, and readable chapter that nails the intellectual-honesty stance (interpretability is progressing but unsolved) and ships two working, prose-aligned widgets. But it has one central-technique defect (the runnable SAE demo's printed conclusion is flatly contradicted by its own output), a genuine conceptual error (logit/tuned lens presented as "causal interventions" and a "five vs. six" miscount), a pedagogically broken exercise (activation-patching Ex3 recovers 100% at every layer, so it cannot teach localization), and real coverage gaps against explicit spec items (attribution, causal scrubbing, feature visualization, SAE-vs-PCA). Verified by executing the code blocks.

**Strengths:** Strong, sustained intellectual-honesty framing: every major technique gets a 'what it does NOT tell you' treatment, and the callouts explicitly rebut common misconceptions (probes != causation, neuron != feature, mech-interp does not yet scale).; Cross-reference integrity is clean: Chapter 24 (safety) and Chapter 26 (evaluation) are cited correctly throughout, section numbers ('section 6' = causal interventions) resolve, and paper attributions (Elhage 2021/2022, Olsson 2022, Bricken 2023, Templeton 2024, Meng 2022, Belrose 2023, nostalgebraist 2020, Conmy 2023) are accurate.; Both specced widgets exist and compute what the prose claims: SAEFeatureExplorer (10 curated features, family clustering, nearest-neighbor map) and LinearProbingVisualizer (6 concepts x 12 layers, peak-layer detection) match their captions.; The linear-probe and activation-patching demo code blocks in the body run correctly and produce sensible output (probe accuracy 98%, patching flips corrupted->clean exactly).; Good pedagogical arc: probes -> polysemanticity -> superposition -> SAEs -> circuits -> causal interventions -> state of field, with the exercises tracing the same progression.

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The toy-SAE runnable claims to recover ~n_true_features (12) live features, but executing it verbatim (seed 11, d=32, D=128, lambda=0.05, 1500 steps) prints '122 live of 128' at the last logged step and stays ~122 — nowhere near 12. The loop even prints the true count (122) on screen right above the text asserting it recovered ~12, a visible self-contradiction on the chapter's flagship technique. Root cause: no decoder-column unit-norm constraint, so the L1 penalty on f is degenerate (scale can move into W_dec), and dead-feature resampling is absent, so almost all 128 hidden units stay live.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch25-interpretability/index.mdx:248 (and observation :253) — "SAE recovers approximately this number of live features." / "# - Trained SAE recovers approximately the original feature count"
  - _fix:_ Either (a) constrain W_dec columns to unit norm each step and raise lambda so the L1 actually forces most features dead, making live-count approach ~12; or (b) stop claiming recovery of the live-feature count and instead report a valid signal (e.g., features whose decoder direction aligns with a true feature, or active-per-input sparsity), and soften the observation comment accordingly.
- **[major/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** Two coupled errors. (1) The sentence says 'Five techniques' but six are then bolded (Logit lens, Tuned lens, Activation patching, ROME, Steering vectors, Feature clamping). (2) Logit lens and tuned lens are observational read-outs (project activations through the unembedding) that do not intervene on the model at all — they are correlational, in the same category as probes. Labeling them 'causal-intervention' techniques directly contradicts this section's own thesis ('Probes correlate; interventions cause') and muddies the correlation/causation distinction the chapter is trying to teach.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch25-interpretability/index.mdx:294 & :296 — "Five techniques carry most of the causal-intervention work in modern interpretability." and "**Logit lens** ... The simplest causal-intervention technique"
  - _fix:_ Move logit lens / tuned lens out of the 'intervention toolkit' (or add a sentence flagging them as observational read-outs, not interventions), and correct the count so the number matches the list (four true interventions: activation patching, ROME, steering vectors, feature clamping).
- **[major/exercise/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 3 cannot demonstrate localization. Because the network is purely sequential and the only difference between clean/corrupt is the input, patching any layer's output with the clean activation fully overwrites everything downstream — running the intended patched_forward gives 100% recovery at layer 0, 1, 2, AND 3 (verified). So the recovery curve is flat at 100%, the exercise's stated lesson ('the earliest layer where recovery is high tells you where the info lives') is false for this construction, and the intended payoff of activation patching is undemonstrable.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch25-interpretability/index.mdx:641-642 — "# # - Recovery should approach 100% as we patch later layers" / "# # - The earliest layer where recovery is high tells you where the info lives"
  - _fix:_ Give the toy model a branching/parallel structure (so information can be routed through some layers and not others), or patch a single component/subspace rather than the entire layer output, so early-layer patches recover only partially and a genuine localization signal emerges.
- **[major/voice/CONFIRMED]** Seven standalone sentences whose only function is to announce the upcoming section. PROJECT_OVERVIEW's forbidden-phrases list explicitly bans 'Any sentence whose only function is to announce the next sentence' and states 'The reader can see the section heading. They don't need to be told what's coming.' This pattern recurs at the end of nearly every section.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch25-interpretability/index.mdx:43,138,162,258,284,357,377 — e.g. "The next section introduces the foundational technique, probes."
  - _fix:_ Delete the transition sentences, or fold a substantive claim into them instead of a pure announcement.
- **[minor/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** TransformerLens is not an EleutherAI project — it originated as Neel Nanda's EasyTransformer and is community-maintained under TransformerLensOrg. EleutherAI's interpretability contributions are different (e.g., the tuned-lens library by Belrose et al., the Pythia model suite). The research file (research.md:365) carries the same wrong attribution.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch25-interpretability/index.mdx:371 — "**EleutherAI**, open-source interpretability tools, including TransformerLens."
  - _fix:_ Attribute TransformerLens to Neel Nanda / the mech-interp community, and cite EleutherAI's actual interp tooling (tuned-lens, Pythia) instead.
- **[minor/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** Conjecture (a company) and Redwood Research (an independent nonprofit) are labeled 'academic groups.' Neither is an academic/university lab.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch25-interpretability/index.mdx:37 & :371 — "academic groups including Conjecture and Redwood Research."
  - _fix:_ Call them independent research organizations, and reserve 'academic groups' for university labs.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** The chapter's 'as of early 2025' snapshot presents circuit discovery as stuck on GPT-2-small. Anthropic's 2025 attribution-graph / circuit-tracing work ('Circuit Tracing' / 'On the Biology of a Large Language Model', published ~March 2025) applied circuit-style analysis to a production model (Claude 3.5 Haiku) via cross-layer transcoders, which partially updates the 'circuits don't scale' framing for a mid-2026 reader.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch25-interpretability/index.mdx:281 — "**SAEs scale better** (Templeton $2024$ reached Claude $3$ Sonnet); circuit discovery does not yet."
  - _fix:_ Add a sentence noting the 2025 attribution-graph line of work as the emerging answer to the cross-layer-circuit-discovery open problem the chapter itself lists.
- **[minor/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 4 hint lists 'won't' twice. Separately, the same hint suggests detecting French via 'accented characters,' but the provided extract_common_words uses regex [a-zA-Z']+, which strips accented characters, so a student following the hint literally cannot detect French that way.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch25-interpretability/index.mdx:745 — "if \"can't\" or \"won't\" or \"decline\" or \"won't\""
  - _fix:_ De-duplicate the keyword list; either broaden the tokenizer regex to keep accented characters or change the French cue to non-accented function words (vous, je, la) that survive the regex.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** The FAMILIES map declares an 'emotional' family that no feature in FEATURES uses (dead entry, and it reuses code's amber color). Meanwhile the SAE prose (index.mdx:176) lists 'abstract emotional states' as an example feature, but no such feature exists in the widget.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch25/sae-data.ts:212 — "emotional: { label: 'emotional', color: 'var(--amber-400)' }"
  - _fix:_ Either add an emotional feature to the widget or drop the unused family; align the prose example list with the 10 features actually shown.
- **[minor/widget/CONFIRMED]** The chart y-axis floor is 0.4, but token-identity accuracy at L11 is 0.36 (probing-data.ts:39), so that data point maps below the plotted axis region (norm negative). Minor visual clipping into the bottom padding; no other concept dips below 0.4.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch25/LinearProbingVisualizer.tsx:18 — "const norm = (accuracy - 0.4) / 0.6;"
  - _fix:_ Lower the axis floor to ~0.3 (and adjust the /0.6 span) so all plotted values sit inside the framed region.
- **[minor/code/CONFIRMED]** Running the probe demo verbatim gives direction alignment ~0.71 (about 45 degrees), which the comment frames as recovering 'the true direction.' 0.71 cosine is a moderate, not strong, recovery; the phrasing overstates the result.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch25-interpretability/index.mdx:127 — "# - Probe recovers the true direction even from noisy hidden states"
  - _fix:_ Soften to 'the probe direction is positively but only moderately aligned with the planted direction (~0.7),' or increase signal/decrease noise so alignment is genuinely near 1.0.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Gradient-based attribution / 'attribution' as a distinct method family is essentially absent, despite the explicit 'Reader will be able to: Distinguish mechanistic interpretability from probing from attribution' and the 'gradient-based attribution' Key concept. The chapter covers probing and mech-interp but never defines attribution (saliency, integrated gradients, attribution patching) as its own category.
- Causal scrubbing (listed in both Key concepts and the curriculum glossary) is never mentioned in the chapter.
- Feature visualization (Key concepts; Olah 2017 in the research file) is not covered — the chapter goes straight from neurons to SAEs without the optimize-the-input visualization technique.
- The specced Tricky-spot 'SAE vs PCA — both are dimensionality reductions, but SAEs add a sparsity constraint' is not addressed; PCA is never mentioned, so the contrast a reader might expect is missing.
- The induction-head Tricky spot ('a 2-head attention pattern that does in-context copying. Walk through how it works') is given only as a one-line description ('a pair of attention heads'); the distinct roles of the previous-token head and the induction head (QK match + OV copy) are not walked through.

**Exercises:** Four exercises that genuinely trace the chapter arc (probe -> SAE -> patching -> feature labeling), which is good scaffolding intent. Ex1 (linear probe) is solid and runs. Ex2 (toy SAE) is reasonable but inherits the body's false 'recovers ~n_true_features' claim in its observation comments. Ex3 (activation patching) is pedagogically broken: for its sequential toy model with input-only corruption, patching any layer yields 100% recovery, so it cannot demonstrate the localization it claims to teach (verified by running). Ex4 (feature labeling) is meaningful and open-ended but has minor scaffolding slips (duplicated 'won't' keyword; accent-based French detection defeated by the provided tokenizer regex). Net: meaningful, not filler, but Ex3 needs a structural fix and Ex2's framing should be corrected.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx and are well-built. SAEFeatureExplorer renders 10 curated monosemantic features with family clustering, top-5 activating inputs, and a top-3 nearest-neighbor 2D map (topKNearest computes real Euclidean nearest neighbors over the layout coords); this matches the prose caption (10 features, the exact concept list, Templeton-2024 inspiration). LinearProbingVisualizer renders 6 concepts x 12 layers with correct peak-layer/peak-accuracy computation and a layer-wise emergence story matching the prose. Two minor issues: (1) the y-axis floor of 0.4 clips token-identity's 0.36 point below the plotted region; (2) sae-data declares an unused 'emotional' family while the prose lists an 'abstract emotional states' feature the widget doesn't include. Overall the widgets teach what the curriculum specs and align with the surrounding text.


### Chapter 26 — grade B+

A well-written, technically sound chapter that delivers the "dashboard, not a number" thesis clearly and honestly. Cross-references are all correct (a notable strength given this book's recurring wrong-chapter-number defect), the three runnable code blocks actually run and produce the claimed outputs, and both specced widgets exist and compute what the prose says. The main weaknesses are coverage gaps against the curriculum: pass@1/pass@k (a fundamental code-eval metric) is entirely absent, Elo is used repeatedly but never defined or given the specced equation, and τ-bench (one of three explicitly-named agent benchmarks in the spec) is missing. No factual blockers; no broken code.

**Strengths:** Cross-reference integrity is perfect: every Chapter/Ch reference (24 safety, 25 interpretability, 21 tool use, 22 retrieval, 23 multimodal, 27-30 agents) points to the correct canonical target.; All three runnable code blocks are correct and produce the claimed outputs (benchmark harness 5/6=83% with math 100%/history 67%; swap-mitigation correctly shows verbosity bias surviving the swap; hard-coding detector yields clean 0% vs hardcoded 100%).; Benchmark facts are accurate: MMLU/HumanEval/HellaSwag/MATH/GPQA/BBH release years, TruthfulQA 817 questions, GPQA humans-with-web ~34%, GAIA humans ~92%, OSWorld 369 tasks, MT-Bench 80 questions/8 categories.; The 'dashboard, not a number' thesis is threaded coherently through every section and made concrete by the heatmap widget's column-resort behavior.; Honest treatment of failure modes (saturation, contamination, reward hacking, Goodhart, distribution shift, single-metric, capability-vs-propensity) with the widget disclaiming that its scores are illustrative ballparks.

**Findings:**

- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** pass@1 / pass@k is never introduced anywhere in the chapter, even though it is the canonical scoring metric for exactly the hidden-test code benchmarks (HumanEval) discussed here, and it is an explicit item in the curriculum glossary.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch26-evaluation/index.mdx:67 — "**Open-ended format with hidden tests** (HumanEval, MATH): test-case verification gives a cleaner signal but costs more compute."
  - _fix:_ Add a sentence defining pass@k (probability at least one of k sampled generations passes the hidden tests; pass@1 = greedy/single-sample) where HumanEval scoring is introduced, or in the Methodology subsection.
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** Elo is invoked repeatedly (here and at line 212 'Elo ratings update continuously') and appears in the glossary, and readers are told to treat Arena's Elo as the 'closest thing to ground truth,' but Elo is never defined and the curriculum-specced 'Elo rating for Chatbot Arena' equation is absent.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch26-evaluation/index.mdx:200 — "Aggregate judgments into win rates, Elo ratings, or pairwise scores."
  - _fix:_ Add a brief definition and the Elo update/expected-score formula (expected win prob = 1/(1+10^((Rb-Ra)/400)); R <- R + K(S - E)) in the Chatbot Arena subsection.
- **[major/coverage/CONFIRMED]** tau-bench (Yao 2024) is omitted entirely, though the curriculum Key concepts names it as one of the three agent benchmarks and the research file lists Yao 2024 as a paper to cover.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch26-evaluation/index.mdx:178 — "**SWE-bench** (Jimenez $2023$) ... **GAIA** (Mialon $2023$) ..."
  - _fix:_ Add one sentence on tau-bench (tool-agent-user interaction benchmark, Yao 2024) to the agentic landscape list.
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** Given the illustrative data, only HellaSwag reaches the >=6-models-at-90 threshold, so exactly one column shows a SAT badge; yet the WidgetFrame caption and in-widget caption describe saturated columns/'whole rows' in the plural, and the widget's own benchmark descriptions call MMLU ('Largely saturated'), HumanEval ('Saturated'), and MATH ('Mostly saturated') saturated without any of them earning a badge.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch26/benchmark-data.ts:135 — "return above90 >= 6;" (with index.mdx:73 caption "Saturated columns (SAT badge) cluster scores near the top")
  - _fix:_ Either lower/adjust the saturation heuristic or nudge the MMLU/HumanEval/MATH ballpark scores so the columns the text calls saturated actually earn the SAT badge, or soften the caption to reference the single saturated column.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** The chapter's frontier is pinned to early-2025 (GPQA '~55-70% frontier', heatmap top models = Claude 3.5 Sonnet / GPT-4o / Gemini 1.5 Pro, no reasoning models). By a mid-2026 reader's vantage, reasoning models pushed GPQA Diamond to ~85%+ and SWE-bench Verified past 70%, and the hard tier the chapter says GPQA anchors was itself displaced by AIME/FrontierMath/Humanity's-Last-Exam, none of which the chapter mentions (the curriculum tricky-spots explicitly reference 'AIME-like math').
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch26-evaluation/index.mdx:59 — "GPQA (Rein $2023$) is the modern hard benchmark; new ones are released as old ones max out."
  - _fix:_ Add a forward-looking note that GPQA/MATH have largely saturated under reasoning models and that AIME/FrontierMath/HLE are the current hard frontier; consider adding a reasoning-model row to the heatmap.

  - _fix:_ Change 'Chen 2024' to 'Chan 2024' for MLE-bench.
- **[minor/consistency/CONFIRMED]** The saturation window is quoted as 12-36 months in the intro (and again at line 59) but 12-24 months in the empirical-scale paragraph; a reader gets two different upper bounds within a few hundred words.

  - _fix:_ Pick one range (e.g., 12-36) and use it consistently, or clarify why the two ranges differ.
- **[minor/exercise/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 4's observation comment cites a 67% model accuracy, but the supplied mock_model (with classify_response implemented per the hint) scores 100% on all nine items; the 67% figure matches neither the actual output nor any computed baseline.

  - _at:_ src/pages/ch26-evaluation/index.mdx:7 — "import { Callout, Equation, EqRef, Figure, WidgetFrame } from '@components/content';"
  - _fix:_ Remove the unused EqRef and Figure imports (or add the intended equation cross-reference / figure).

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- pass@1 / pass@k is never mentioned or defined anywhere in the chapter, despite being in the curriculum glossary and being the canonical scoring metric for the code benchmarks (HumanEval) the chapter discusses at length.
- Elo rating is used 4+ times (win rates / Elo ratings; 'Elo ratings update continuously') and is in the glossary, but is never defined and the curriculum-specced 'Elo rating for Chatbot Arena' equation is absent (the only <Equation> is the symbolic three-flavors expression).
- tau-bench (Yao 2024) is missing entirely, though the curriculum Key concepts names it as one of three agent benchmarks ('agent benchmarks (tau-bench, SWE-bench, GAIA)') and the research file lists the Yao 2024 paper.
- G-Eval (named in Key concepts under LLM-as-judge) is not mentioned.
- BBQ (named in Key concepts under safety evals) is not mentioned.
- AIME-like math benchmarks are absent; the curriculum tricky-spots explicitly cite 'New benchmarks (GPQA, AIME-like math) are pushing back the ceiling,' and by a mid-2026 reader's vantage the hard-frontier landscape (AIME/FrontierMath/HLE and reasoning models) is exactly what replaced the now-saturated MATH/GPQA tier.
- The specced 'holdout eval design' detail (GPQA Diamond, GAIA private split as the standard post-cutoff holdout practice) is only gestured at generically ('held-out from training, ideally never published') without naming the concrete instances the tricky-spots flagged.
- The specced 'LLM-as-judge bias correction (position-swap averaging)' equation is demonstrated only in code, never stated as an equation.

**Exercises:** The four exercises are meaningful, correctly scaffolded, and trace the chapter arc (harness -> LLM-judge multi-mitigation -> contamination detection -> custom eval design), hitting curriculum outcomes 1/4/5/6 as intended. Templates are internally consistent: the mock data, keyword-matching, and provided helper functions (evaluate_detector precision/recall/F1, calibration_baselines) all work, and the TODO stubs are the right size for the stated difficulty. One minor blemish: Exercise 4's "Observations" comment claims "A 67% accuracy looks good until you see refuse-everything hits 33%," but the supplied mock_model, if classify_response is implemented per the hint, actually scores 100% (9/9) and refuse-everything computes to 33%, so the illustrative 67% figure matches neither the code's real output nor any computed baseline and could confuse a student who runs it.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist and are correct. BenchmarkHeatmap (marquee) renders 8 models x 10 benchmarks, sorts by column/category, and the sort/mean logic is correct; the LLMJudgeBiasDemo (secondary) correctly encodes verdicts in absolute A/B terms so mitigatedVerdict() yields the right swap-mitigation outcome for all five scenarios, matching the prose. One minor mismatch: the SAT-badge heuristic (isSaturated: >=6 of 8 models at >=90) fires only for HellaSwag given the data (MMLU maxes at 89, HumanEval has only 2 cells >=90, MATH maxes at 78), yet the prose captions describe saturated columns/"whole rows" in the plural and the widget's own benchmark descriptions call MMLU/HumanEval/MATH saturated. So the SAT visual under-delivers on what the surrounding text promises.


### Chapter 27 — grade B-

A well-structured, engaging chapter with correct cross-references, two strong widgets, and a genuinely honest "bounded autonomy" framing. But it ships a blocker: the flagship ReAct runnable never calls the weather tool and fabricates the weather in its final answer — accidentally demonstrating the exact "hallucinated tool output" anti-pattern the chapter warns against. It also carries wrong arithmetic in a widget scenario billed as "computation with verification," and it drops the single most-specced conceptual distinction (agent vs workflow, Anthropic "Building Effective Agents"), which the curriculum flags as both a Key concept and a Tricky spot.

**Strengths:** Cross-reference integrity is clean: Ch 20 (reasoning), Ch 21 (tool use), Ch 22 (retrieval/memory), Ch 24 (safety), Ch 28/29/30 forward-pointers, and Ch 1 (numpy) are all numbered correctly against the canonical map.; Honest, non-hyped tone with concrete numbers (GAIA 60-75%, SWE-bench ~50%, humans ~92%) and a clear central thesis ('the capability is the loop, not the model') carried consistently.; AgentPatternCatalog widget is thorough and accurate: five patterns with correct diagrams, maturity badges, pros/cons, and example tasks that align with the prose and the loop-visualizer scenarios.; Good pedagogical scaffolding on exercises 2-4 (Reflexion memory, retry-with-backoff, design-your-own-pattern) with working reference logic and sensible hints.; The control-systems analogy for the loop and the Wright-Brothers framing for AutoGPT are apt and land the intended intuitions.

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The flagship ReAct RunnableCode never calls the weather tool. The task string 'What's the weather in Tokyo, and what date is it today?' already contains 'Tokyo', so the guard `'Tokyo' not in prompt` is False on iteration 1 and the weather branch is dead. Running it as written produces: Iteration 1 calls date() (skipping weather), Iteration 2 returns final_answer("...18°C, partly cloudy.") — a weather value that was NEVER observed from any tool. This contradicts the prose trace at lines 100-111 (which shows weather→date→final) and, worse, the demo commits the chapter's own 'hallucinated tool output / hallucinated success' anti-pattern (lines 334, 84) while claiming 'Every step is inspectable: agent failures are diagnosable.' Confirmed by execution.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch27-agent-foundations/index.mdx:141 — "if 'weather' in prompt.lower() and 'Tokyo' not in prompt:"
  - _fix:_ Change the discriminator so the weather branch fires. E.g. gate on whether the weather observation is already present: `if 'partly cloudy' not in prompt:` (return weather action) then `elif '2025-05-22' not in prompt:` (return date action) then final. Verify the run prints Iteration 1 = weather("Tokyo"), Iteration 2 = date(), Iteration 3 = final_answer.
- **[major/math-error/CONFIRMED]** The 'Computation with verification' scenario reports calculator outputs that don't match the expressions shown. `5000 * (1 + 0.045)**7` = 6804.3092 (widget says 6802.4533, final says $6,802.45); `5000 * (1 + 0.045/12)**(12*7)` = 6847.2613 (widget says 6852.0987, final says $6,852.10); the stated difference '~$49.65 more' is actually $42.95. All three numbers are wrong. A reader who copies the exact expression into Python gets a different answer than the textbook's own 'verified' calculator, directly undercutting the chapter's grounding thesis. Confirmed with Python.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch27-agent-foundations/loop-data.ts:90 — observation: '6802.4533...' for action 'calculator("5000 * (1 + 0.045)**7")'
  - _fix:_ Replace the observations and final answer with the true values: annual 6804.31, monthly 6847.26, difference ~$42.95 (or re-derive and update all three consistently).
- **[major/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The curriculum spec makes the agent-vs-workflow distinction a Key concept ('agent vs workflow distinction (Anthropic 2024 "Building Effective Agents")', CURRICULUM.md:1408) AND a named Tricky spot ('Workflows are predetermined sequences; agents have dynamic control flow. Most production "agent" systems are actually workflows', CURRICULUM.md:1427), and the research file calls it 'foundational'. The chapter's distinctions table (chat model / tool-using LLM / agent) does not capture this axis — a workflow can be multi-step and multi-tool yet still be a predetermined sequence, not an agent. The chapter never uses the word 'workflow' in this sense nor cites 'Building Effective Agents'.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch27-agent-foundations/index.mdx:29-41 (section 'Distinctions that matter') — the agent-vs-workflow distinction is absent chapter-wide (grep for 'workflow'/'harness'/'Building Effective Agents' returns only generic uses)
  - _fix:_ Add a short subsection (or a row/paragraph in 'Distinctions that matter') on dynamic control flow vs predetermined sequences, citing Anthropic's 'Building Effective Agents' (2024), and note that most production 'agents' are actually workflows.
- **[minor/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 1's mock_llm carries the same dead-weather-branch bug as the section demo. A student who correctly completes the react_agent loop will still get a trace that skips the weather lookup and hallucinates '18°C, partly cloudy', while the exercise's own Observations note claims 'Each iteration appends 2-3 lines'. The scaffolding thus teaches the wrong lesson.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch27-agent-foundations/index.mdx:471 — Exercise 1 defaultCode: "if 'weather' in prompt.lower() and 'Tokyo' not in prompt:"
  - _fix:_ Apply the same discriminator fix as the section demo so a correct loop implementation calls weather then date then final.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** By mid-2026 the Assistants API is deprecated in favor of OpenAI's Responses API + Agents SDK (both launched early 2025, before the Jan-2026 cutoff). Presenting Assistants API as a current Layer-4 hosted service, with no mention of the Responses API or the OpenAI Agents SDK, reads as stale for the textbook's readers.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch27-agent-foundations/index.mdx:413 — "OpenAI Assistants API is a managed agent service with built-in tools" (also line 863 summary 'OpenAI Assistants')
  - _fix:_ Note that Assistants API is being sunset in favor of the Responses API and the OpenAI Agents SDK; mention the Agents SDK in Layer 3 (frameworks).
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** 787000/38394 = 20.49799..., so the shown calculator observation '20.4978...' has a wrong trailing digit (should be 20.4980... / 20.49799). The final answer '20.5' is fine, but the intermediate 'verified' calculator output is off.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch27-agent-foundations/loop-data.ts:137 — observation: '20.4978...' for 'calculator("787000 / 38394")'
  - _fix:_ Change observation to '20.4980...' or '20.49799...'.
- **[minor/voice/CONFIRMED]** Seven section-closing sentences whose only function is to announce the next section. PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md:131 explicitly forbids 'Any sentence whose only function is to announce the next sentence' — the reader can see the heading. This is a systemic house-style violation across the chapter.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch27-agent-foundations/index.mdx:47 — "The next section unpacks the loop itself." (also lines 92, 207, 231, 320, 399, 425)
  - _fix:_ Delete the standalone 'The next section...' bridges, or fold a substantive teaser into the preceding paragraph.
- **[minor/missing-depth/CONFIRMED]** The spec's 'Reader will be able to' includes 'Recognize memory systems (short-term, long-term, working memory)' and Key concepts lists 'scratchpad / working memory' (CURRICULUM.md:1404, 1408). The chapter covers short-term and long-term memory well but never names working memory / the scratchpad, even though the accumulating Thought/Action/Observation history IS the scratchpad — a one-line naming opportunity is missed.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch27-agent-foundations/index.mdx:233-247 (Memory and state) — grep 'working memory'/'scratchpad' returns nothing
  - _fix:_ Name the running loop history as the agent's working memory / scratchpad in the ReAct or memory section.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Agent vs workflow distinction (dynamic control flow vs predetermined sequence) and the Anthropic 'Building Effective Agents' reference — specced as a Key concept AND a Tricky spot AND a 'foundational' research reference — is entirely absent.
- 'The harness (system around the model)' — a specced Key concept and glossary term — is never introduced (the chapter says 'agent engineering' instead). Partly deferred to Ch 28, but Ch 27's spec lists it.
- 'Working memory / scratchpad' is never named, despite being in both 'Reader will be able to' and Key concepts.
- Runnable-code spec asked for a real Anthropic-API ReAct loop exposing a Python REPL as a tool ('react_loop.py ... using the Anthropic API. User supplies key'); the chapter delivers only mock-LLM/mock-tool code. Reasonable for in-browser Pyodide, but a deviation from spec with no note.
- BabyAGI is promised in the chapter description ('the 2023 agent boom (AutoGPT, BabyAGI)') and specced in research, but appears only as a one-word passing mention; its recursive task-list pattern is never explained.

**Exercises:** Four meaningful, well-scaffolded exercises that trace the chapter arc (ReAct → Reflexion memory → retry/backoff → pattern selection), not filler. Exercises 2, 3, and 4 have correct reference logic and sensible collapsed hints (Ex2 succeeds on attempt 3 as intended; Ex4's choose_pattern heuristics map the three tasks to linear/react/reflexion correctly). Exercise 1 is undermined by the same dead-weather-branch bug as the section demo: a correctly completed loop still skips the weather tool and hallucinates the weather, contradicting the exercise's own Observations note. Fixing the shared mock_llm discriminator repairs both.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as .tsx and are correctly wired (index.ts exports AgenticLoopVisualizer and AgentPatternCatalog). AgentPatternCatalog is accurate and well-aligned with the prose. AgenticLoopVisualizer's mechanics (step reveal of think/act/observe, accumulating context) match the prose intent and satisfy the spec's 'AgentLoopTrace / show working memory expanding' goal — but its data has correctness defects: the 'Computation with verification' scenario reports calculator outputs that don't match the shown expressions (6802.45 vs true 6804.31; 6852.10 vs true 6847.26; '$49.65' vs true $42.95), and the Bhutan scenario shows 20.4978 for a value that is 20.49799. For a widget whose pedagogical point is that tool outputs ground the answer, fabricated arithmetic is a meaningful mismatch.


### Chapter 28 — grade B

A well-organized, engaging engineering chapter whose prose is technically sound and whose two widgets (ToolSchemaBuilder, AgentTraceInspector) exist, render, and largely match the prose. Chapter-number cross-references are all correct. The main weaknesses are (1) a confirmed logic bug in the capstone Exercise 4 that makes the demonstration fabricate an answer, (2) substantial divergence from the CURRICULUM spec (the specced state-machine widget, Anthropic-API abstraction layer, and type-hint/Pydantic schema generation are all absent, though the research file deliberately re-scoped), and (3) a security misconception ("sandboxed eval") plus several voice-contract signposting violations. Solid but not spec-complete.

**Strengths:** Every chapter-number cross-reference is correct, including the long end-of-chapter recap of the whole book's arc (transformer internals Ch 4-6, pretraining Ch 7-10, post-training Ch 13-16, inference Ch 17-19, etc.) which maps exactly to the canonical numbering.; The two runnable code blocks in the body (tool registry, circuit-breaker+retry, tracer) are correct and actually run to the claimed output.; Widget data is internally consistent for token/cost sums (clean, retries, hallucinated, cost-blown traces all sum correctly), and the flame-graph inspector genuinely computes what the prose describes.; Strong, honest tonal frame (80/20 engineering) with concrete, well-chosen anti-patterns and misconception callouts that match the research file.; Internal counting is disciplined: 'Six principles', 'Four patterns', 'Six patterns', 'Seven components', 'Five patterns' all list the promised number of items.

**Findings:**

- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 4's mock_llm uses `'Tokyo' not in prompt` as the proxy for 'weather not yet fetched', but the test task string itself contains 'Tokyo', so the get_weather branch is never selected. The reference run calls only get_date, then '2025-05-22' is now in the prompt so it jumps to final_answer('Today in Tokyo: 18C, partly cloudy.') — a hardcoded answer for weather it never retrieved. The capstone's whole point (multi-turn ReAct exercising both registered tools) silently fails.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch28-agent-from-scratch/index.mdx:855 (and test task line 922) — "if 'weather' in prompt and 'Tokyo' not in prompt:" with task "What's the weather in Tokyo and the date today?"
  - _fix:_ Make the guard key off the observation content, not the task — e.g. gate on whether an Observation line containing the weather result (like '18C') is present, or change the test task to omit the city name ('What's the weather and the date today?'), which makes the intended two-tool flow work.
- **[major/security-misconception/CONFIRMED]** eval with an empty __builtins__ is not a real sandbox; it is famously escapable (e.g. via ().__class__.__bases__[0].__subclasses__()). Labeling the pattern 'sandboxed eval' (also the PATTERN_LABELS badge) and claiming it 'blocks dangerous operations' teaches a classic false-safety misconception, in a book that has a dedicated safety chapter (Ch 24) and a whole sandboxing subsection.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch28-agent-from-scratch/tool-data.ts:406 — "the function uses sandboxed `eval` with an empty `__builtins__` to block dangerous operations"
  - _fix:_ Reword to 'restricted eval (blocks the obvious cases but is NOT a real sandbox; use ast.literal_eval, a real expression parser, or a subprocess/container for untrusted input)'. Rename the badge accordingly.
- **[major/voice/CONFIRMED]** Six section-transition sentences whose only function is to announce the next section. PROJECT_OVERVIEW's forbidden list explicitly bars 'Any sentence whose only function is to announce the next sentence' and states 'The reader can see the section heading. They don't need to be told what's coming.' The list is marked enforceable.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch28-agent-from-scratch/index.mdx:168 — "Section 4 covers how the LLM sees these tools, schemas." (also lines 39, 67, 213, 321, 469)
  - _fix:_ Delete the standalone signpost sentences; let the section headings do the work, or fold a substantive lead-in into the next section's first paragraph.
- **[minor/widget-math-mismatch/CONFIRMED]** Three inconsistencies in the cost-blown trace: (1) cumulative cost is 0.2474, strictly below the stated $0.25 cap, yet the span/insight say the budget was exceeded and forced termination; (2) 'Cost grew 6x per turn at the peak' is wrong — per-turn ratios peak at 2.1x; 5.7x is the turn-1-to-turn-5 total; (3) the note '$0.014 -> $0.083 in a single turn' spans five turns, not one.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch28-agent-from-scratch/trace-data.ts:226/232 — "Cost budget triggered at $0.25 and forced termination" with totalCostUsd 0.247; insight "Cost grew 6x per turn at the peak"
  - _fix:_ Either bump the peak turn cost so cumulative >0.25, or state the cap as 'projected next turn would exceed $0.25'. Reword the growth insight to 'total cost grew ~6x across 5 turns' and fix 'single turn'.
- **[minor/code-efficiency/CONFIRMED]** On the last failing attempt the helper still sleeps `base_wait * 2**(max_retries-1)` before returning the error, an unnecessary wait. Present in both the body §5 code and Exercise 2's reference.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch28-agent-from-scratch/index.mdx:277 — with_retry loops `for attempt in range(max_retries)` and calls time.sleep(wait) even on the final attempt
  - _fix:_ Skip the sleep on the final iteration (e.g. `if attempt < max_retries - 1: time.sleep(wait)`).
- **[minor/widget-prose-mismatch/PLAUSIBLE]** Per-call cost figures do not correspond to Claude Sonnet 4 pricing (320in/95out at $3/$15 per M = ~$0.0024, not $0.0058), and back-solving the four clean-trace calls implies output tokens are cheaper than input, which is backwards for every current provider. A sharp reader who trusts the 'realistic-feeling' framing will get a wrong mental model of token economics.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch28-agent-from-scratch/trace-data.ts:64 — cost_usd 0.0058 for tokens_in 320, tokens_out 95
  - _fix:_ Recompute costs from a stated pricing constant (input < output), or add a visible caveat that costs are illustrative and not to scale.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** Illustrative traces/code pin the model to 'claude-sonnet-4' (released May 2025). By the reader's mid-2026 vantage this is a dated model id (Sonnet 4.5 and later exist). Not wrong, but reads as stale in a chapter about production practice.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch28-agent-from-scratch/index.mdx:407 and trace-data.ts — model="claude-sonnet-4"
  - _fix:_ Use a generic placeholder like 'claude-sonnet' or note the id is illustrative.
- **[minor/code-hygiene/CONFIRMED]** EqRef and Figure are imported but never used in the chapter (the labeled Equation 28.real-agent is never cited via <EqRef/>, and no <Figure> appears). Dead imports.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch28-agent-from-scratch/index.mdx:7 — "import { Callout, Equation, EqRef, Figure, WidgetFrame } from '@components/content';"
  - _fix:_ Drop EqRef and Figure from the import, or add the intended EqRef citation to the production-agent equation.
- **[minor/voice/CONFIRMED]** PROJECT_OVERVIEW's cross-reference convention is the explicit form ('as introduced in Chapter 4') with a link to the anchor. The chapter mixes one full 'Chapter 27' (line 11) with many bare 'Ch 27/29/30/22' and provides no cross-chapter links, so 'Ch 27 §5' and similar pointers aren't navigable.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch28-agent-from-scratch/index.mdx:21 — "all covered in Ch 27" (abbreviated 'Ch NN' used throughout; no anchor links)
  - _fix:_ Standardize on 'Chapter NN' and link the anchors (e.g. Ch 27 §5 memory, Ch 22 RAG).

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- CURRICULUM specs an AgentStateMachineDiagram widget and lists 'agent state machines' as a key concept; neither the widget nor an explicit state-machine treatment is delivered (research re-scoped to ToolSchemaBuilder + AgentTraceInspector).
- 'Reader will be able to: Implement a clean abstraction layer over the Anthropic API' is not delivered; the chapter only ever uses a hardcoded mock_llm and never shows an API-client wrapper.
- Tricky spot 'Tool registration via decorators with type-hint introspection - Pydantic's BaseModel makes this clean; show how' and the runnable 'auto-generated JSON schemas from Python type hints' are not delivered; the chapter uses hand-written parameters_schema dicts and Ex 1 explicitly says 'Schema generation uses metadata only (no inspection); explicit is better' - the opposite of the spec.
- 'Message protocol design' (a typed dataclass message protocol) is a spec key concept and glossary term but is not covered in the chapter.
- 'Cost and latency' in-scope items caching and concurrency get only glancing treatment (async is mentioned; result caching / (tool,args) memoization from research TID10 is absent).
- The chapter carries zero inline citations or links (ReAct, Anthropic 'Building Effective Agents', MCP docs, OpenTelemetry, LangSmith), whereas the book's citation convention expects inline first-author/year or linked references.

**Exercises:** Exercises 1-3 are meaningful, correctly scaffolded, and trace the chapter's arc (registry -> retry -> tracer); the TODO comments give accurate reference solutions. Exercise 4 (the capstone) is undermined by a confirmed bug: the mock_llm gates tool selection on `'Tokyo' not in prompt` as a proxy for "weather not yet fetched", but the test task string itself contains "Tokyo", so get_weather is never selected. Running the reference solution calls only get_date, then jumps straight to a hardcoded final_answer that claims weather data it never retrieved. The exercise runs and terminates, but its whole pedagogical point (a multi-turn ReAct task exercising both registered tools) fails silently. Also, with_retry (Ex 2 and the body code) sleeps after the final failed attempt, an unnecessary wait.

**Widgets:** Both delivered widgets exist, compile, and match their captions, but they are not the CURRICULUM-specced AgentStateMachineDiagram, and the trace/tool data contain a security misconception and several numeric inconsistencies (detailed in findings).


### Chapter 29 — grade C+

A well-written, voice-compliant chapter that nails its chosen "calibration / honest-assessment" framing and ships two solid, prose-aligned widgets. But it silently drops two of the four stated learning objectives — inter-agent communication protocols MCP and A2A — along with the specced mcp_server_reference.py deliverable and the Cognition "Don't Build Multi-Agents" attribution; the flagship manager-worker code sample has a confirmed logic bug that prints the plan instead of the final answer; and it carries a very likely fabricated historical citation ("Hoff and Anderson 1976"). Strong on rhetoric and pedagogy, materially incomplete on specced technical substance.

**Strengths:** The central thesis (single-agent default, 80/20 calibration against multi-agent hype) is executed with genuine clarity and restraint, exactly matching the spec's tonal anchor of 'respectful skepticism.'; All chapter-to-chapter cross-references (Ch 27/28 build-on, Ch 30 setup, the full Ch 1-30 recap in the closing) are correct against the canonical map — no stale numbering.; Both widgets exist as real .tsx with data that matches the prose numerically (Bhutan 787,000 / 38,394 = 20.4978 ≈ 20.5; Sydney→Canberra correction; the degenerate 3-reviewer anti-pattern).; The proposer-critic-judge runnable (lines 198-249) and the multi-vs-single cost runnable (lines 334-377) are correct and run as claimed.; Exercises trace a coherent arc (classify → implement → audit/refactor → framework-select) and are real problems with working mock-LLM scaffolds, not filler.

**Findings:**

- **[blocker/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The manager-worker mock branches on system_prompt, but the manager's system_prompt is 'You decompose tasks and aggregate results.' which permanently contains the substring 'decompose'. So BOTH manager.respond() calls take the first branch. The final `manager.respond("aggregate: results above")` returns the Subtasks plan again instead of 'Final answer: Bhutan ~20.5 people/km^2.' — the '=== Final ===' block prints the plan, directly contradicting the pedagogical point that 'manager decomposes; workers execute; manager aggregates.'
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch29-multi-agent/index.mdx:102, and the aggregate call at :133 — "    if 'decompose' in system_prompt:"
  - _fix:_ Branch on the message, not the system prompt: `if 'decompose' in message:` (the plan call passes 'decompose: {task}', the aggregate call passes 'aggregate: results above').

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- MCP (Model Context Protocol) — a stated learning objective ('Understand inter-agent communication protocols (MCP, A2A)') and Key concept, entirely absent.
- A2A (Agent-to-Agent protocol, Google 2025) — stated learning objective and Key concept, entirely absent; the specced MCP-vs-A2A and 'MCP is for tools, not multi-agent' misconceptions are never addressed.
- mcp_server_reference.py — a specced Runnable code deliverable ('minimal MCP server example as static reference') is not present.
- The Cognition AI 'Don't Build Multi-Agents' blog — spec's tricky-spot says it 'deserves engagement, not dismissal.' The chapter channels its thesis convincingly but never names or cites the source, so the attributed engagement the spec asked for is missing.
- Wu 2023 (AutoGen, arXiv:2308.08155) — AutoGen is discussed repeatedly but the paper is never cited.
- Anthropic's 2025 multi-agent research system — listed in the research scope as a key source, not cited (and its existence complicates the chapter's 'everything ships single-agent' claim).
- Generative-agent simulations and the 2025 framework landscape are covered in depth but are chapter-added emphasis; the trade was made against the specced protocol content (MCP/A2A), not in addition to it.

**Exercises:** Meaningful and well-scaffolded, not filler — the four exercises deliberately trace the chapter's argument (classify when multi-agent is warranted → implement proposer-critic-judge with genuinely distinct role prompts → audit and refactor a degenerate 3-reviewer design → select a framework by scenario), each with a collapsed hint and a runnable mock-LLM template. Ex 2's mock is internally correct (the sqrt(225) trap: proposer wrong → critic catches the 15*15=225 contradiction → judge fixes to 15). Ex 3 has a real scaffold defect: the mock's 'pr author' guard is case-sensitive and never matches AUTHOR_PROMPT ('PR author'), making the author branch dead code, and REVIEWER_DETAILED_PROMPT collides with the generic 'code reviewer' branch so Fix B's 'distinct roles' produce identical mock output — quietly undermining the exercise's own thesis. Ex 1 and Ex 4 are judgment exercises with sensible heuristic tables. Overall solid, with one exercise needing a mock-matching fix.

**Widgets:** Both specced-in-spirit widgets exist as real .tsx and compute what the prose claims. MultiAgentTopologyExplorer renders 5 topologies (single-agent baseline first, per the chapter's calibration claim) with SVG node/arrow diagrams, use cases, pros/cons, example task, and maturity badges — data in topology-data.ts matches the prose. InterAgentConversationViewer steps through 4 scenarios; conversation-data.ts numbers align exactly with prose (Bhutan 787,000 / 38,394 km² = 20.4978 ≈ 20.5; the Sydney→Canberra proposer-critic-judge correction; the degenerate 3-reviewer 'anti-pattern demonstrated' outcome). No placeholder or broken widgets. One deviation: the CURRICULUM specced a question-driven flowchart recommender (MultiAgentTopologySelector); the delivered Explorer is a static side-by-side comparison that covers the same content but does not ask about the task and recommend. The chapter's captions describe what the widgets actually do, so there is no prose/widget contradiction — only the spec-shape deviation.


### Chapter 30 — grade B-

A fluent, well-structured closing chapter with functional widgets and mostly-correct runnable code. Its serious problem is coverage: it silently redefines "frameworks" from the specced agent-building frameworks (LangGraph, smolagents, OpenAI Agents SDK, Pydantic AI, Anthropic native) to observability platforms (LangSmith, Helicone, Braintrust, OpenTelemetry), omits the explicitly-flagged Upwork HAPI tricky spot, and ships no "build a small custom agent eval" runnable code — so two of the four "Reader will be able to" outcomes are unmet. It also carries one confirmed math error in the Exercise 3 domination annotation and a wrong release year for BrowseComp in the widget data.

**Strengths:** Prose is disciplined and on-voice; the 'agent eval = LLM eval x system complexity x environmental verification' framing and the Ch 26 mirror give the chapter a clear spine.; The three body runnable-code blocks (pass^k, Pareto frontier, regression check) are correct and their inline observation numbers match what the code actually produces (0.75^4 ~= 32%, 0.6^4 ~= 13%, etc.).; Both widgets are fully implemented (not placeholders), wired into the barrel export, and their data matches the prose numbers; the AgentBenchmarkExplorer benchmark counts (SWE-bench 500, GAIA 466, OSWorld 369, tau-bench 165, BrowseComp 1266) are accurate.; The production-readiness checklist and deployment-patterns section are concrete and genuinely useful, and the Exercise 4 audit is a well-designed hard exercise.; The curriculum-closing retrospective maps Parts I-IX to chapter ranges correctly (Ch1-3, 4-6, 7-10, 11-12, 13-16, 17-19, 20-23, 24-26, 27-30) with correct topic attributions and the Chinchilla/Hoffmann 2022 citation.

**Findings:**

- **[major/coverage-vs-spec/CONFIRMED]** The chapter's 'frameworks' half covers observability PLATFORMS (LangSmith, Helicone, Braintrust, Anthropic eval, OpenTelemetry, custom) instead of the agent-building FRAMEWORKS the curriculum requires. The spec's 'Reader will be able to: Compare major agent frameworks (LangGraph, smolagents, OpenAI Agents SDK, Pydantic AI, Anthropic native)' and the specced FrameworkPicker (features: tool use, multi-agent, persistence, streaming, observability) are unmet; smolagents / Pydantic AI / OpenAI Agents SDK are never mentioned.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/index.mdx:223, '## Production observability frameworks' / 227 'Five options dominate the $2025$ landscape'
  - _fix:_ Add an agent-framework comparison section and repurpose (or add) a FrameworkPicker over LangGraph/smolagents/OpenAI Agents SDK/Pydantic AI/Anthropic-native with the honest tradeoffs the spec names, or move the observability material into a clearly-secondary subsection.
- **[major/coverage-vs-spec/CONFIRMED]** Upwork HAPI is entirely absent. The curriculum's Tricky Spots explicitly says 'Upwork HAPI deserves real attention given the author's background. Show what makes it different from prior agent benchmarks (real-world tasks from Upwork's marketplace, end-to-end evaluation).'
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/index.mdx (whole chapter; grep for 'upwork'/'hapi' returns nothing)
  - _fix:_ Add a subsection (and a benchmark-data entry) on Upwork HAPI: real marketplace tasks, end-to-end evaluation, and how it differs from SWE-bench/GAIA-style benchmarks.
- **[major/code-bug/CONFIRMED]** The claim is numerically false. Domination requires the dominator to have quality >= the dominated config's quality. hybrid-haiku-sonnet has quality 0.62, which is LESS than sonnet-bloated's 0.64, so it cannot dominate it. sonnet-bloated is actually dominated by sonnet-only-default (0.15, 0.65) and sonnet-tuned (0.20, 0.71). A student who implements is_dominated_by correctly gets output that contradicts the printed annotation.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/index.mdx:568, "'sonnet-bloated',       'cost': 0.30, 'quality': 0.64},  # dominated by hybrid-haiku-sonnet" (echoed at :611 'higher cost AND lower quality than hybrid-haiku-sonnet')
  - _fix:_ Either lower sonnet-bloated's quality below 0.62 (e.g., 0.60) so hybrid-haiku-sonnet genuinely dominates it, or correct both comments to 'dominated by sonnet-only-default'.
- **[major/coverage-vs-spec/CONFIRMED]** Neither the four exercises nor the three body code blocks deliver the specced runnable 'custom_agent_eval.py — build a small 10-task eval set with verifiable answers, run an agent against it, compute pass@1', which is also the 'Build a small custom agent eval' reader outcome. Exercise 2 only computes pass^k over pre-baked mock boolean lists.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/index.mdx:366-727 (Exercises) and body code blocks
  - _fix:_ Add a runnable code block that constructs a small eval set with verifiable answers (e.g., string/exact-match verifiers), runs a mock agent, and reports pass@1, matching the spec.
- **[minor/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** BrowseComp is dated 2024 in the widget, but it is an OpenAI April-2025 benchmark. The chapter's own research file lists it as 'BrowseComp (OpenAI 2025)'. The header comment also calls these 'Five 2025 agent benchmarks' while tagging BrowseComp 2024.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/benchmark-data.ts:175 'year: 2024,' and :171 "'OpenAI 2024; recent benchmark with growing adoption',"
  - _fix:_ Change BrowseComp year to 2025 and the characteristic to 'OpenAI 2025'.
- **[minor/currency/PLAUSIBLE]** For a mid-2026 reader these headline numbers materially understate current agent capability (SWE-bench Verified frontier passed ~70% and OSWorld computer-use scores rose well above 15% during 2025). The numbers are honestly timestamped 'early 2025', but the closing repeats them as an 'honest reminder' of where capability stands.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/index.mdx:56 'Frontier agents reach around $50\%$ as of early $2025$' and :362 'SWE-bench Verified around $50\%$, OSWorld around $15\%$'
  - _fix:_ Refresh the SWE-bench Verified and OSWorld headline ranges to reflect 2025-2026 progress, or soften the closing so it does not present early-2025 numbers as the current state.
- **[minor/technical-error/CONFIRMED]** 'GPT-4 mini' is not a real model name; the small OpenAI model is 'GPT-4o mini'.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/index.mdx:162 'Small models (Claude Haiku, GPT-4 mini)'
  - _fix:_ Change 'GPT-4 mini' to 'GPT-4o mini'.
- **[minor/stale-cross-ref/PLAUSIBLE]** The transformer block is introduced in Chapter 5 (Multi-head attention & the transformer block); Chapter 4 covers the attention operation itself. Earlier the same chapter correctly cites 'Ch 4's attention' (:761), so pinning 'the transformer block' to Chapter 4 mislocates the block.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/index.mdx:767 'The transformer block is still the same matrix-multiplication-and-softmax it was in Chapter 4.'
  - _fix:_ Either say 'the attention operation ... in Chapter 4' or 'the transformer block ... in Chapter 5'.
- **[minor/voice/CONFIRMED]** Seven end-of-section lines whose main function is to announce the next section. The voice contract's forbidden-phrase list bans 'Any sentence whose only function is to announce the next sentence' and notes 'The reader can see the section heading.'
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/index.mdx:48 'Section 2 covers the benchmark landscape ...' (also :80, :152, :221, :253, :341, :364)
  - _fix:_ Cut or fold these into the section's substantive content instead of previewing the next heading.
- **[minor/pedagogy/CONFIRMED]** Exercise 1's coding task is explicitly JavaScript, but SWE-bench Verified is Python-only. The hint/heuristic maps 'Coding tasks -> SWE-bench Verified' without flagging the language mismatch, so a student following the hint reaches a technically-wrong benchmark choice rather than 'no public benchmark directly fits'.
  - _at:_ src/pages/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/index.mdx:405 "'A coding agent that fixes GitHub issues in a JavaScript repository.'" with hint :383 'Coding tasks | SWE-bench Verified'
  - _fix:_ Add a note in the hint that SWE-bench is Python-only, so a JS repo needs a multilingual variant or a custom eval — reinforcing the exercise's own 'no benchmark fits' branch.
- **[minor/technical-error/PLAUSIBLE]** The 'agents exceed humans' framing is potentially misleading: BrowseComp's ~30% human figure comes from human trainers who gave up on the majority of problems, not a clean head-to-head human ceiling. The prose (:64) does not make this claim, so the widget also diverges from the prose.
  - _at:_ src/components/widgets/ch30-agent-eval-and-frameworks/benchmark-data.ts:170 'Frontier agents actually exceed human baseline' / :174 'frontier agents (~42%) actually exceed human performance (~30%)'
  - _fix:_ Qualify the human baseline (humans solved ~30% within a time limit and abandoned the rest) rather than asserting agents outright exceed human capability.

**Coverage gaps vs curriculum:**
- Agent-framework comparison (LangGraph, smolagents, OpenAI Agents SDK, Pydantic AI, Anthropic native) — a core 'Reader will be able to' and the specced FrameworkPicker content — is entirely absent; the chapter substitutes observability platforms. smolagents, Pydantic AI, and the OpenAI Agents SDK are never mentioned.
- Upwork HAPI is completely missing, despite the spec flagging it as a tricky spot deserving 'real attention given the author's background' (real-world Upwork-marketplace tasks, end-to-end evaluation).
- 'Build a small custom agent eval' outcome and the specced runnable code custom_agent_eval.py (10-task eval set with verifiable answers, run an agent, compute pass@1) are not delivered.
- WebArena, listed in the spec's benchmark set and key concepts, is not covered in prose or widget.
- 'Articulate what's coming next in agent infrastructure' is addressed only at the field level (better reasoning/agents/safety/efficiency) rather than agent-infra specifics (sandboxes, retry layers, observability standardization).

**Exercises:** The four exercises are meaningful, well-scaffolded, and trace the chapter's arc (design eval -> compute pass^k -> Pareto -> readiness audit); hints are collapsed and the TODO scaffolding is genuine, not filler. Two defects: (1) Exercise 3's domination annotation is numerically wrong — sonnet-bloated (quality 0.64) is labeled 'dominated by hybrid-haiku-sonnet' (quality 0.62), but a config with lower quality cannot dominate; the real dominator is sonnet-only-default, so the correctly-implemented code will contradict the printed comment. (2) Exercise 1's hint maps a JavaScript coding task to SWE-bench Verified without noting SWE-bench is Python-only. None of the exercises delivers the specced 'build a small custom agent eval with verifiable answers, compute pass@1' (custom_agent_eval.py); Exercise 2 only computes pass^k from mock trial lists.

**Widgets:** Both specced widgets exist as functional .tsx (AgentBenchmarkExplorer, FrameworkPicker), are exported from the widgets barrel, and match the prose numerically. Two problems: (1) FrameworkPicker covers six OBSERVABILITY frameworks (LangSmith/Helicone/Braintrust/Anthropic-eval/OpenTelemetry/custom) rather than the AGENT frameworks the spec required (LangGraph, smolagents, OpenAI Agents SDK, Pydantic AI, Anthropic native) across features (tool use, multi-agent, persistence, streaming) — it aligns with the prose but both deviate from spec. (2) benchmark-data.ts gives BrowseComp year 2024 / 'OpenAI 2024' when BrowseComp is an OpenAI April-2025 benchmark (the research file itself says 'BrowseComp (OpenAI 2025)'). The AgentBenchmarkExplorer also silently drops WebArena and Upwork HAPI, both of which the spec asked for.
